DU LL.B. II Term · Paper LB-205 · Topic 7 · 5 Hours

Sovereign, Diplomatic and Consular Immunity & Privileges

Three immunities that stand between the State and the courtroom — the immunity of the State itself (sovereign or jurisdictional immunity, resting on par in parem non habet imperium), the immunity of the diplomatic mission and its agents (the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961), and the narrower, function-based immunity of consuls (the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963). Built on the DU LB-205 case material — United States Diplomatic and Consular Staff in Tehran (casebook pp. 189–207) and the Jadhav case (casebook pp. 208–219) — together with the two cases which the syllabus prescribes by citation but the casebook does not print (The Schooner Exchange v. McFaddon, 1812, and Jurisdictional Immunities of the State (Germany v. Italy), ICJ 2012), S.K. Verma's Chapter 8 (Diplomatic and Consular Relations) and Chapter 7, the UN Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, 2004, the US Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act, 1976, the UK State Immunity Act, 1978, and sections 86–87 of the Indian Code of Civil Procedure, 1908.

☰How to Use These Notes

Topic 7 asks one question in three costumes. The question is: when may a State, its envoys and its agents be made answerable in the courts or to the police of another State? The first costume is sovereign (State) immunity — the immunity of the foreign State itself, together with its property, from the jurisdiction of the forum State's courts; its central battleground is the distinction between acts done in the exercise of sovereign authority (acta jure imperii) and acts of a private, commercial or managerial character (acta jure gestionis). The second is diplomatic immunity — the near-total inviolability and jurisdictional immunity of a diplomatic mission and of diplomatic agents under the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961. The third is consular immunity — the narrower, function-based protection of consular posts and consular officers under the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963. All three rest on the same bedrock propositions: the sovereign equality of States (par in parem non habet imperium — an equal has no authority over an equal), the absolute territorial jurisdiction of the State over its own territory, and the functional necessity of leaving the channels of inter-State communication unblocked. Marshall C.J. stated both halves of that paradox in 1812 in The Schooner Exchange v. McFaddon: the jurisdiction of a nation within its own territory is "necessarily exclusive and absolute", and yet every sovereign is understood, by implication, to have waived a part of that jurisdiction in favour of the foreign sovereign, his ships, his envoys and their missions.

What is inside

Every sub-head of 7.1 to 7.5 is treated in full: the types of diplomatic agents (VCDR Article 1 definitions, the three classes of heads of mission in Article 14, the older four-class classification of the Congress of Vienna, and the five categories of mission personnel); consuls (the four classes of heads of consular post, the exequatur, Article 5 functions, and the diplomat–consul comparison); sovereign and non-sovereign acts (the absolute and restrictive theories, the nature and purpose tests, the UN Convention of 2004, the US and UK statutes, and CPC sections 86–87); diplomatic immunity, personal and property (Articles 20–47 of the VCDR, article by article); and consular privileges and immunities (Articles 28–57 of the VCCR, article by article). Each of the four prescribed cases — Schooner Exchange (40), Tehran Hostages (41), Germany v. Italy (42) and Jadhav (43) — is given its facts, issues, the arguments of both sides, the decision and the principle evolved, as the standing instruction for this project requires.

What is added

Because the instruction is to place every related order, rule and Act at the point where it arises, the notes carry the Vienna Conventions of 1961 and 1963 article by article; the Optional Protocols on the compulsory settlement of disputes (which gave the ICJ jurisdiction in Tehran and in Jadhav); the UN Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, 2004; the European Convention on State Immunity, 1972; the US Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act, 1976 (including the Tate Letter of 1952) and the UK State Immunity Act, 1978; the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972 of India; sections 86–87 CPC; the Convention on Special Missions, 1969; the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of Crimes against Internationally Protected Persons, 1973; the International Organisations (Privileges and Immunities) Act, 1948; Articles 104–105 of the UN Charter; the ILC's 1989 Draft Articles on the Diplomatic Courier and Bag; the Pakistan Army Act provisions used in Jadhav; and the diplomatic-law incidents that illustrate every rule (Tehran 1979, the Iraqi Embassy raid in Pakistan 1973, the Libyan People's Bureau 1984, the Dikko affair 1984, the Kuwait 1990 embassy seizures, the Chinese Embassy in Belgrade 1999, the Devyani Khobragade arrest 2013, and the India–Canada expulsions of 2024). Real examples, diagrams and comparison tables are given at every stage.

The standing instruction and how it is honoured here

The instruction for this project is that the notes be built primarily on the DU LB-205 course material ("maximum portion based on DU case material"). For Topic 7 that instruction has to be read carefully, because the course material prints only two of the four prescribed cases. The casebook contains the Tehran Hostages judgment (case 41, printed pages 189–207) and the Jadhav judgment (case 43, printed pages 208–219). It does not print The Schooner Exchange v. McFaddon (case 40) or Jurisdictional Immunities of the State (Germany v. Italy) (case 42): those two appear in the syllabus list by citation only, without page numbers. Cases 41 and 43 are therefore digested here from the casebook text itself — with the casebook's own paragraph numbers, its own quotations and its own structure preserved — and cases 40 and 42 are digested from the sources themselves (the report of Marshall C.J.'s opinion of 1812 and the ICJ's judgment and official summary of 3 February 2012), each under an express provenance note. Nothing taken from outside the casebook is ever presented as casebook material.

7.0The Problem Stated — Three Immunities, One Idea

A. Three Immunities, One Idea

International law begins with a world of sovereign equals and no superior. Every State has exclusive and absolute jurisdiction within its own territory; no State is subject to the jurisdiction of another without its consent. Two consequences follow, and they pull in opposite directions. The first is that a State's courts may normally take cognisance of any dispute with a foreign element that comes before them. The second is that a foreign State, its organs, its envoys and its property are carved out of that jurisdiction — not because they are above the law, but because the machinery of one State's courts cannot, consistently with sovereign equality, be set in motion against another State without its consent. Three distinct bodies of rule do the carving out.

The three immunities in one paragraph each

(1) Sovereign immunity (State immunity; jurisdictional immunity). A foreign State is not amenable to the jurisdiction of the courts of another State, and its property is immune from measures of constraint taken in those courts. The immunity was once absolute; since the twentieth century it has in most States of the world become restrictive: a foreign State may be sued in respect of acts of a private-law, commercial or managerial character (acta jure gestionis), but not in respect of acts performed in the exercise of sovereign authority (acta jure imperii). The rule is reflected in the UN Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, 2004, in the European Convention on State Immunity, 1972, and in national statutes (US 1976, UK 1978, Australia 1985, Canada 1982, and others).

(2) Diplomatic immunity. Once diplomatic relations exist by mutual consent (VCDR Article 2) and a mission is established, the mission is protected (the premises inviolable — Article 22; archives inviolable everywhere — Article 24; free communication including an unopenable bag — Article 27) and the diplomatic agent is protected (the person inviolable and exempt from any form of arrest or detention — Article 29; immunity from criminal jurisdiction without exception and from civil and administrative jurisdiction with only three exceptions — Article 31; immunity from execution — Article 31(3)). The immunity belongs to the sending State, not to the individual, and may be waived only by that State, and only expressly (Article 32).

(3) Consular immunity. Consuls are the sending State's representatives "for particular purposes" in a consular district, admitted by exequatur, and they do not enjoy the diplomatic agent's complete immunity. Their protection is functional: consular officers are inviolable except that they may be arrested or detained pending trial "in the case of a grave crime and pursuant to a decision by the competent judicial authority" (Article 41(1)); they are not amenable to the jurisdiction of the receiving State's judicial or administrative authorities "in respect of acts performed in the exercise of consular functions" (Article 43(1)), with two civil exceptions; and the inviolability of the consular premises itself is partial, not absolute (Article 31).

One idea holds the three together: the receiving State's own courts must not be used to interrupt the channels through which States speak to one another. The ICJ put the point in its most compressed form in Tehran: there is "no more fundamental prerequisite for the conduct of relations between States than the inviolability of diplomatic envoys and embassies", and the institution of diplomacy is "an instrument essential for effective co-operation in the international community" (United States Diplomatic and Consular Staff in Tehran, Order of 15 December 1979, I.C.J. Reports 1979, p. 19, repeated in the Judgment of 24 May 1980 at paragraph 91). Marshall C.J. had said something structurally similar in 1812 about public armed ships: they enter a friendly port under an "implied promise" that, while necessarily within the territory and demeaning themselves in a friendly manner, they shall be exempt from its jurisdiction.

B. Why the Law Protects the Diplomat — the Four Theories

For the examination, the "basis of immunities" is a standing question and must be answered with the theories, not with adjectives. The International Law Commission, when it prepared the codification that became the VCDR, listed several competing explanations; S.K. Verma groups them, and the course notes follow him. Learn all four, then say which one the Convention adopted.

Table 7.1 — The four theories of diplomatic immunity, and how each one fares
TheoryThe claimAssessment
Extra-territorialityThe diplomat and the mission are, by a legal fiction, deemed to remain on the territory of the sending State; the receiving State therefore has no jurisdiction over them, just as it has none over foreign soil.Discarded. The fiction is not true (the diplomat is physically inside the receiving State, is bound to respect its laws by Article 41(1), and his immunity is from jurisdiction, not from the territorial reach of the receiving State's law). It cannot explain why a diplomat who commits a private tort outside his functions remains immune, or why immunity ends when he leaves. The Australian Supreme Court in R v. Turnbull, ex parte Petroff (1971) 16 FLR 438 rejected the theory outright.
Representative characterThe diplomat personifies the sending State and its sovereign; since the sending sovereign himself is immune, so is his representative. This is why the immunity is the State's and can be waived only by the State.Accepted in part. This theory explains why the immunity belongs to the sending State and not to the individual, why it survives the individual's own wishes, and why Article 32 vests waiver in the sending State. It does not by itself explain the extent of the privileges (why, for instance, family members and service staff share them).
Functional necessityPrivileges and immunities exist so that the mission and its members can perform their functions independently, without interference, pressure or fear. The preamble to the 1961 Convention adopts exactly this: "the purpose of such privileges and immunities is not to benefit individuals but to ensure the efficient performance of the functions of diplomatic missions as representing States."The dominant modern basis, and the one to lead with in an answer. It explains the ranking of immunities (diplomatic agents > administrative and technical staff > service staff > private servants), the narrower protection of consuls, and the rule in Article 38 that a diplomatic agent who is a national of or permanently resident in the receiving State enjoys immunity only in respect of official acts. The VCDR is "the sum total" of the theories, but where the customary law was unclear, the functional theory supplied the answer.
Comity and reciprocityStates grant immunities because they expect the same treatment for their own missions, and because a failure to protect a foreign mission is an unfriendly act. The Convention's Article 47 (non-discrimination) and the practice of reciprocal withdrawal of privileges rest on this footing.Supporting, not foundational. Reciprocity explains why the system is stable and why retaliatory withdrawal is tolerated (Verma cites the Indian Act's section 4 and the British Diplomatic Immunities Restriction Act, 1955), but it cannot justify the protection of a mission of a State with which relations are hostile, which the Convention requires all the same.

Link forward: the same four reasons are used, with different emphasis, for State immunity in 7.3 — see §7.3 B, where Marshall C.J.'s reasoning in Schooner Exchange supplies par in parem non habet imperium, non-intervention, comity, and implied licence.

C. The Vocabulary — Inviolability, Immunity, Jurisdiction, Enforcement

Four pairs of words carry most of the marks in this topic, and most lost marks come from confusing them.

Table 7.2 — Four distinctions that decide most Topic-7 questions
The pairWhat the distinction is, and why it matters
Inviolability vs immunityInviolability is a duty of the receiving State not to touch — not to arrest or detain (Article 29), not to enter the premises without consent (Article 22(1)), not to open the bag (Article 27(3)). It is a positive obligation of the receiving State, breach of which is actionable directly by the sending State. Immunity is a bar to the exercise of jurisdiction by the receiving State's authorities — courts, police, tax officers. A diplomat may be inviolable but not immune for certain purposes; a State's property may be immune from execution even when the State itself has no immunity from jurisdiction in the suit.
Immunity from jurisdiction vs immunity from enforcementA court may have jurisdiction to hear a case against a foreign State (because the claim falls within an exception to immunity) and yet no measure of execution may be taken against that State's property in satisfaction of the judgment. The UN Convention, 2004, keeps the two separate (Articles 5–17 deal with jurisdiction; Articles 18–21 with constraint). The ICJ in Jurisdiction Immunities (2012) said the immunity from enforcement "goes further" than jurisdictional immunity, and that a judgment lawfully rendered does not automatically make assets executable.
Personal immunity vs functional (official-act) immunityPersonal immunity attaches to certain persons (diplomatic agents during their mission; the "troika" of Head of State, Head of Government and Foreign Minister) and covers all acts, private as well as official, but for a limited time. Functional immunity attaches to official acts and survives the end of office (VCDR Article 39(2); VCCR Article 53(4)). The Arrest Warrant case (Topic 6, case 37) is the classic illustration: the Foreign Minister enjoyed full immunity while in office, but the judgment expressly noted that immunity is procedural and does not confer impunity.
Sovereign (jure imperii) vs non-sovereign (jure gestionis) actsThe organising distinction of §7.3. An act "in the exercise of sovereign authority" — legislation, nationalisation, armed forces operations, diplomatic activity, public loans — attracts immunity; an act of a private-law character — buying boots, repairing a heating system, issuing a letter of credit — does not, under the restrictive theory. The test of classification is the nature of the act, not the purpose for which it was done (see §7.3 D).

D. The Instruments You Must Know

Every answer on Topic 7 should name the instrument at the right point. Learn Table 7.3 as a map; the texts themselves are given article by article at §7.4 and §7.5.

Table 7.3 — The instruments of Topic 7, and the point at which each one is used
InstrumentDate / placeWhat it governs, and where it is used in these notes
Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations1961; in force 24 April 1964; UN Treaty Series vol. 500, p. 95The core of §7.1 and §7.4. 53 articles and a preamble; codifies and progressively develops the law on diplomatic relations, mission premises, immunities and privileges. India is a party; implemented in India by the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972.
Optional Protocol to the VCDR concerning the Compulsory Settlement of Disputes1961Article I confers compulsory ICJ jurisdiction over disputes arising out of the interpretation or application of the VCDR. This is one of the two jurisdictional gateways used in Tehran.
Vienna Convention on Consular Relations1963; in force 19 March 1967; India acceded in November 1977The core of §7.2 and §7.5. 79 articles in three chapters: consular relations in general; facilities, privileges and immunities of career posts and their members; honorary posts. Article 36 is the provision litigated in Jadhav.
Optional Protocol to the VCCR concerning the Compulsory Settlement of Disputes1963Article I is the sole basis of the ICJ's jurisdiction in Jadhav (India v. Pakistan), 2019.
Convention on Special Missions1969 (GA Res. 2530 (XXIV))Temporary missions sent by one State to another with consent, to deal with specific questions or perform a specific task (Article 1(a)). Privileges and immunities modelled on the 1961 Convention. See §7.1 G and §7.4 A.
Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of Crimes against Internationally Protected Persons, including Diplomatic Agents1973; in force 20 February 1977; India acceded 11 April 1978Creates a prosecute-or-extradite regime for attacks on Heads of State, ministers, representatives and officials of international organisations, and on their premises, accommodation and transport. Invoked in Tehran as part of the applicable law. See §7.4 L and §7.5 M.
Convention on the Representation of States in their Relations with International Organisations of Universal Character1975Regulates permanent missions and delegations to international organisations. Verma notes that it has attracted little support; practice is governed by headquarters agreements and the 1961 Convention by analogy. See §7.4 A.
UN Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their PropertyAdopted by GA Res. 59/38, 2 December 2004; opened for signature 17 January 2005The general rule of immunity (Article 5) plus the exceptions (Articles 10–17) and the separate rules on constraint against property (Articles 18–21). Not yet in force (30 ratifications required by Article 30); treated as largely reflective of customary international law and relied on by the ICJ in 2012. See §7.3 E.
European Convention on State Immunity (with Additional Protocol)1972; in force 1976The first treaty to embody the restrictive theory; the model for the national statutes that followed. See §7.3 C.
US Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act · UK State Immunity Act1976 · 1978The two statutory models of restrictive immunity, and the source of the "commercial activity" exception. The Tate Letter of 1952 marks the US shift from absolute to restrictive immunity. See §7.3 C and E.
Indian Code of Civil Procedure, 1908, sections 86–87 (as amended in 1976, read with the orders and notifications made under section 86(4))1908; s. 86 amended in 1976India has no separate State-immunity statute: consent of the Central Government, certified in writing by a Secretary to the Government, is required before a foreign State may be sued (§86(1)); consent is not to be given unless one of the four conditions in §86(2) is satisfied; execution against the property of a foreign State requires the written consent of the Central Government (§86(3)); the protection extends to rulers, ambassadors, envoys and specified staff (§86(4)); §87 provides for certain ruler-related exceptions. See §7.3 G.
Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972 (India)1972Gives the VCDR the force of law in India; contains provisions on withdrawal of privileges in cases of breach or lesser treatment (section 4), customs (section 6), nationals and permanent residents (section 7), and — crucially — the certificate of the Secretary in the Ministry of External Affairs as conclusive evidence of entitlement to privileges and immunities (section 8), and the bar on serving legal process within mission premises without the head of mission's consent. See §7.4 N.
International Organisations (Privileges and Immunities) Act, 1948 (India)1948The Indian statute under which privileges and immunities are conferred on international organisations and their personnel (and the statutory route by which India implements Article 105 of the UN Charter). See §7.4 A.
UN Charter, Articles 104–1051945Article 104: the Organisation enjoys in the territory of each member such legal capacity as is necessary for the exercise of its functions. Article 105: the Organisation enjoys privileges and immunities in the territory of each member; representatives of members and officials enjoy such privileges and immunities as are necessary for the independent exercise of their functions. See §7.4 A.

E. The Syllabus Mapped — 7.1 to 7.5 and Cases 40–43

The syllabus head, as printed in the University's course outline, reads:

Topic 7 — Sovereign, Diplomatic, and Consular Immunity/Privileges (5 hours)

7.1. Diplomatic agents — Types · 7.2. Consuls · 7.3. Sovereign and Non-Sovereign Acts · 7.4. Diplomatic Immunity: personal and property · 7.5. Consular Privileges and Immunities

40. The Schooner Exchange v. McFaddon (1812) 7 Cranch 116 · 41. Case Concerning U.S. Diplomatic and Consular Staff in Tehran, ICJ Rep. 1980, p. 3 … 189 · 42. Jurisdictional Immunities of the State (Germany v. Italy: Greece Intervening) ICJ Rep. (2008) · 43. Jadhav Case (India v Pakistan), ICJ Rep. (2019) … 208

Two points of care about that list. First, the page numbers printed against cases 41 and 43 are the casebook pages where those digests begin — 189 and 208 — and the two entries without page numbers (40 and 42) are the two that the casebook does not print. Second, the year given for case 42 in the syllabus, "ICJ Rep. (2008)", is the year the application was filed (Germany instituted proceedings on 23 December 2008); the judgment was delivered on 3 February 2012 and is reported at I.C.J. Reports 2012, p. 99. The point is worth a sentence in the examination because it shows you know the case rather than the citation.

Why this matters for the examination

In the DU paper the examiner marks what the course material contains. For Topic 7 the course material gives you two judgments in full: Tehran and Jadhav. Master those two in the casebook's own language and paragraph numbers first; then add Schooner Exchange and Germany v. Italy as the syllabus requires, using the classic formulation of Marshall C.J. and the ICJ's 2012 findings. In the notes below, every proposition drawn from Verma, from a judgment, from the Court's summary or from a statute is identified as such; nothing outside the casebook is ever attributed to it.

Fig. 7.1 — The taxonomy of Topic 7: three immunities, their governing instruments, and the four prescribed cases.

H. Ten Sentences That Answer Any Question on Topic 7

  1. The starting point of the whole topic is Marshall C.J.'s proposition in Schooner Exchange (1812) that "the jurisdiction of a nation within its own territory is necessarily exclusive and absolute", coupled with the corollary that "all exceptions to the full and complete power of a nation within its own territories must be traced up to the consent of the nation itself" — consent being express or implied.
  2. Sovereign immunity, or State immunity, is a rule of customary international law by which a State may not be made a defendant before the courts of another State without its consent; it rests on par in parem non habet imperium, on non-intervention, on comity and on the implied licence on which a foreign sovereign enters the territory of another.
  3. The immunity is now restrictive rather than absolute: it covers acts performed in the exercise of sovereign authority (acta jure imperii) but not acts of a private, commercial or managerial character (acta jure gestionis), the distinction being drawn by the nature of the transaction rather than by the State's subjective purpose.
  4. If the foreign State claims immunity for property, it must show at least a right to possession or control; and immunity from jurisdiction must be distinguished from immunity from execution, for which a separate waiver or a separate exception is required.
  5. Diplomatic relations exist only by mutual consent (VCDR Article 2); the sending State must obtain the agrément of the receiving State for the head of mission (Article 4), and the receiving State may at any time and without explanation declare any member of the mission persona non grata (Article 9).
  6. The premises of the mission are inviolable; agents of the receiving State may not enter them except with the consent of the head of the mission; the receiving State is under "a special duty to take all appropriate steps to protect" them; and they are immune from search, requisition, attachment and execution (Article 22).
  7. The person of a diplomatic agent is inviolable, not liable to any form of arrest or detention, and the receiving State must take all appropriate steps to prevent any attack on his person, freedom or dignity (Article 29); his private residence enjoys the same protection (Article 30).
  8. A diplomatic agent enjoys complete immunity from the criminal jurisdiction of the receiving State and immunity from its civil and administrative jurisdiction except in three cases — a real action over private immovable property, a succession in which he is involved as a private person, and a professional or commercial activity exercised outside his official functions (Article 31(1)) — and he is neither obliged to give evidence nor subject to measures of execution save within those exceptions (Articles 31(2), 31(3)).
  9. Waiver of immunity belongs to the sending State, must always be express, is implied in respect of connected counterclaims if the agent himself sues, and does not extend to execution of the judgment unless separately waived (Article 32).
  10. Consuls are not diplomats: their privileges are functional and limited — inviolability of the person subject to the "grave crime" exception (Article 41), immunity from the jurisdiction of the receiving State only in respect of acts performed in the exercise of consular functions (Article 43), and only partially inviolable premises — while their single most important right, access to and communication with nationals of the sending State, is regulated by Article 36, which the ICJ held in Jadhav (2019) to create individual rights and to admit no espionage exception.
The topic in one line, for the top of the answer script

"Topic 7 governs the extent to which a State, its property, its diplomatic envoys and their missions, and its consular officers may be called to account before the courts and authorities of another State; the governing instruments are the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961, the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963, the UN Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, 2004, and, in India, sections 86–87 of the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908 together with the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972."

↑ top

7.1Diplomatic Agents — Types

"Types" of diplomatic agents has three layers in the course materials, and an answer that separates them at once reads as an answer written by someone who has understood the Convention. The first layer is the legal definition: who counts as a "diplomatic agent" under Article 1 of the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961 — because the Convention's protection is given by category, and the category determines the immunity. The second layer is the rank: the classes of heads of mission in Article 14, and behind it the older four-fold classification of the Congress of Vienna (1815) which the class notes reproduce. The third layer is the function: the five categories of persons attached to a mission — diplomatic staff, administrative and technical staff, service staff and private servants — each of which receives a different, carefully graded set of privileges under Article 37. Get the three layers right and 7.1 is finished; confuse them and 7.4 will be a muddle.

A. Who Is a "Diplomatic Agent"? (VCDR Article 1)

The Convention does not define "diplomatic mission" as such; it defines the persons and the premises. Article 1 is therefore the gateway provision on which every later article depends. Learn the nine definitions together, because the examination frequently asks simply: "who is a diplomatic agent, and who is not?"

Table 7.6 — The cast of characters: the nine definitions in VCDR Article 1
Expression (Article 1)DefinitionWhy it matters
(a) "head of the mission"The person charged by the sending State with the duty of acting in that capacity.He is the person whose consent is required before agents of the receiving State may enter the premises (Article 22(1)), who presents letters of credence (Article 13), and who may waive the immunity of members of the mission below him.
(b) "members of the mission"The head of the mission and the members of the staff of the mission.The umbrella term. Notice that the head is a member of the mission; the phrase "members of the mission" is wider than "members of the diplomatic staff".
(c) "members of the staff of the mission"The members of the diplomatic staff, of the administrative and technical staff, and of the service staff.The three-fold division of the staff — the skeleton of Article 37. Private servants are not members of the staff of the mission; they are in the domestic service of a member of the mission.
(d) "members of the diplomatic staff"Members of the staff of the mission having diplomatic rank.Diplomatic rank is a matter for the sending State, subject to the receiving State's right to require the names of military, naval and air attachés to be submitted for approval (Article 7).
(e) "diplomatic agent"The head of the mission or a member of the diplomatic staff of the mission.The definition to quote. It is narrower than "member of the mission": it excludes administrative and technical staff, service staff and private servants. Where the VCDR says "diplomatic agent" — Articles 29, 30, 31, 32, 33, 34, 35, 36, 40, 42 — only this class is meant.
(f) "members of the administrative and technical staff"Members of the staff of the mission employed in the administrative and technical service of the mission.Clerks, archivists, typists, translators, cipher clerks, communications technicians. They get a slightly abridged version of the diplomatic agent's immunity under Article 37(2).
(g) "members of the service staff"Members of the staff of the mission in the domestic service of the mission.Chauffeurs, porters, kitchen staff, gardeners. Their immunity is functional only (Article 37(3)) — a point the Indian courts have applied when claims are brought against drivers and domestic staff.
(h) "private servant"A person in the domestic service of a member of the mission and who is not an employee of the sending State.The nanny, the cook, the gardener employed personally by an ambassador. Their privileges are only those admitted by the receiving State (Article 37(4)).
(i) "premises of the mission"The buildings or parts of buildings and the land ancillary thereto, irrespective of ownership, used for the purposes of the mission, including the residence of the head of the mission.The definition on which Article 22 rests. Because it is ownership-blind, a mission housed in rented premises is as inviolable as one in owned property; and because the head of mission's residence is included, the "office" and the "home" share the same protection.
Two traps in the definition

Trap one: the receiving State's approval of an individual is not an element of the definition of a diplomatic agent. A person validly appointed and notified is a diplomatic agent, and enjoys immunity, even before he presents his credentials — indeed from the moment he enters the territory to take up his post (Article 39(1)). Conversely, a person may be declared non grata before arriving (Article 9(1)). Trap two: "diplomatic agent" is a term of art; the newspapers' "diplomat" (anyone who works in an embassy, including locally recruited clerks, drivers and security guards) is not the Convention's "diplomatic agent", and the immunity of the two is different.

Compare the parallel gateway provision in the consular Convention: VCCR Article 1 defines "consular post", "consular district", "head of consular post", "consular officer", "consular employee", "member of the service staff", "members of the consular post", "members of the consular staff", "member of the private staff", "consular premises" and "consular archives" — see §7.2 A.

B. The Three Classes of Heads of Mission (VCDR Article 14) and the Older Four Classes

Rank matters in diplomacy because it regulates precedence, not protection. The Convention states the modern classification in Article 14:

Article 14(1) — "Heads of mission are divided into three classes, namely: (a) that of ambassadors or nuncios accredited to Heads of State, and other heads of mission of equivalent rank; (b) that of envoys, ministers and internuncios accredited to Heads of State; (c) that of chargés d'affaires accredited to Ministers for Foreign Affairs."

Two further sentences complete the modern rule. Article 14(2) provides that, "except as concerns precedence and etiquette, there shall be no differentiation between heads of mission by reason of their class"; and Article 15 leaves it to States to agree which class their missions are to be assigned. That is the whole law: class = precedence, not privilege. Precedence within each class is determined by the date and time of taking up functions (Article 16(1)), without prejudice to any accepted practice regarding the representative of the Holy See (Article 16(3)).

Before the Convention the position was otherwise, and it is that older position which the course notes preserve. The Regulation annexed to the Congress of Vienna of 19 March 1815 established four classes of diplomatic representatives — (i) ambassadors, papal legates and nuncios; (ii) envoys extraordinary and ministers plenipotentiary; (iii) ministers resident; and (iv) chargés d'affaires — and the Congress of Aix-la-Chapelle (1818) confirmed the rank of ministers resident. The rank a representative held then decided the honours he received, the order of signing treaties, and (in the practice of some courts) the extent of his ceremonial immunities. Article 14 abolished the third of those consequences by fixing the modern three classes; the class notes' remark that ministers plenipotentiary "enjoy less privilege and immunities" than ambassadors is therefore correct as a description of the pre-1961 position and of ceremonial practice, but must be corrected in an examination answer by Article 14(2).

Table 7.7 — The classes of heads of mission: the older four and the Convention's three
Class (Congress of Vienna, 1815)Modern equivalent (VCDR Article 14)Notes
Ambassadors, papal legates and nunciosFirst class — ambassadors or nuncios accredited to Heads of State, and heads of mission of equivalent rankThe ambassador is the personal representative of the Head of State in the receiving State. High Commissioners exchanged between Commonwealth countries are of equivalent rank (and, in Indian practice, take precedence with ambassadors). The Pope's representatives are nuncios (formerly also legates and internuncios).
Envoys extraordinary and ministers plenipotentiarySecond class — envoys, ministers and internuncios accredited to Heads of StateHeads of mission of the second class. Rare today: a State that does not wish to exchange ambassadors may send a minister.
Ministers residentAbsorbed into the second class (the class was dropped in 1961)Ministers resident were accredited to the government, not to the Head of State. Article 14 no longer gives them a separate class; a State wishing to send such a mission must agree the class with the receiving State under Article 15.
Chargés d'affairesThird class — chargés d'affaires accredited to Ministers for Foreign AffairsNot accredited to the Head of State; hence the exception in Article 14(1)(c). A chargé d'affaires ad interim is a different person altogether — the officer left in charge when the post of head of mission is vacant or the head is unable to act (Article 19(1)).

C. The Categories of the Course Materials — Ambassadors, Ministers, Chargés d'Affaires

The LB-205 materials follow S.K. Kapoor's classification and set out the diplomatic agents "according to their status and functions" in three categories. Reproduce them in the examination as the course-material classification, and then reconcile them with Article 14 as above. The three are:

  • (1) Ambassadors and legates. In the words of the notes, "the diplomatic agent of the first category … the representatives of the completely sovereign states". Ambassadors are appointed to Heads of State and may also be accredited as Permanent Representatives to the United Nations; the representative appointed by the Pope is called a legate. To this category may be added High Commissioners (within the Commonwealth) and heads of mission of equivalent rank.
  • (2) Ministers plenipotentiary and envoys extraordinary. The second category. The notes observe that "as compared to the diplomatic agents of the first category, they enjoy less privilege and immunities" — the statement of the older law; under Article 14(2) the difference is one of precedence and etiquette only.
  • (3) Chargés d'affaires. "They are not appointed by the head of the state but in fact by the foreign minister." Precisely: the chargé d'affaires is accredited by the sending State's Minister for Foreign Affairs to the receiving State's Minister for Foreign Affairs, and takes precedence after all heads of mission of the first and second classes. It is because he is not accredited to the Head of State that he forms the third class in Article 14(1)(c).
Correction worth making in the answer script — letters of credence, not "exequatur"

Some of the class notes describe the ambassador's instrument of appointment as a "letter of exequature". That is a slip of terminology worth quietly correcting: a head of mission presents letters of credence (and, on recall, receives a lettre de récréance from the receiving State), while exequatur is the instrument by which the receiving State admits a head of consular post to the exercise of his functions (VCCR Article 12). The two instruments belong to two different Conventions, and the examination rewards the distinction. See §7.2 B for the exequatur.

Three further persons complete the picture of "types" at the top end. A head of mission may be accredited to more than one State (Article 5(1)) — the "non-resident ambassador" — and may also act as the sending State's representative to an international organisation (Article 5(3)); two or more States may accredit the same person as head of mission to another State (Article 6, the practice of "cumul", once common in the Commonwealth); and if the post of head of mission is vacant, or he is unable to act, a chargé d'affaires ad interim acts provisionally, his name being notified to the receiving State by the head of mission or, if the head cannot do so, by the sending State's Foreign Ministry (Article 19(1)). Where no member of the diplomatic staff is present, a member of the administrative and technical staff may, with the receiving State's consent, be designated to be in charge of current administrative affairs (Article 19(2)).

D. The Other Members of the Mission — Diplomatic, Administrative, Technical, Service, Private

Under the Convention the staff of a mission falls into three classes (Article 1(c)), with a fourth category — private servants — standing outside the mission's staff altogether. The distinctions look pedantic until one sees that Article 37 gives each a different immunity, which is the practical heart of 7.1.

Table 7.8 — The categories of persons attached to a mission and the immunities each receives (VCDR Articles 1 and 37–38)
CategoryExamplesImmunities if not a national of or permanently resident in the receiving StateArticle
Diplomatic agent (head of mission and members of the diplomatic staff)Ambassador; minister-counsellor; first, second and third secretaries; attachés (defence, trade, press)Full inviolability of person and residence; immunity from criminal jurisdiction without exception; immunity from civil and administrative jurisdiction subject to the three exceptions; immunity from giving evidence; exemption from taxes, customs and social security; inviolability of papers, correspondence and property.Arts 29–36 (via Art. 37(1) for family)
Members of the administrative and technical staffArchivists, cipher and communications clerks, translators, typists, administrative assistantsInviolability of person and residence; immunity from criminal jurisdiction; immunity from civil and administrative jurisdiction only for acts performed in the course of their duties; exemption from taxes on emoluments, from social security, and (in respect of articles imported at first installation) from customs duties.Art. 37(2)
Members of the service staffChauffeurs, porters, cooks, kitchen and domestic staff of the missionImmunity only in respect of acts performed in the course of their duties; exemption from dues and taxes on emoluments received by reason of employment; social security exemption. No general inviolability.Art. 37(3)
Private servants of members of the missionA nanny, cook or gardener employed personally by a diplomat and not by the sending StateExemption from dues and taxes on emoluments if not a national of or permanently resident in the receiving State; in all other respects, privileges only to the extent admitted by the receiving State — but the receiving State must exercise jurisdiction so as not to interfere unduly with the mission's functions.Art. 37(4)
Family members forming part of a householdSpouse, dependent children, dependent parents living with the agentFamily of a diplomatic agent: Articles 29–36 in full, if not nationals of the receiving State. Family of administrative and technical staff: Articles 29–35 (with the abridged civil immunity), plus customs privileges for first-installation imports.Art. 37(1), (2)
Nationals of, or permanently resident in, the receiving StateLocally engaged staff; a diplomat who is a citizen of the receiving StateA diplomatic agent who is a national of or permanently resident in the receiving State enjoys only immunity from jurisdiction and inviolability in respect of official acts performed in the exercise of his functions. Other members of staff and private servants enjoy privileges only to the extent admitted by the receiving State (but jurisdiction must be exercised without unduly interfering with the mission's functions).Art. 38

Three rules complete the personnel picture. First, the sending State appoints freely — "subject to the provisions of articles 5, 8, 9 and 11, the sending State may freely appoint the members of the staff of the mission", though in the case of military, naval or air attachés the receiving State may require names to be submitted beforehand for its approval (Article 7). Secondly, members of the diplomatic staff should in principle be nationals of the sending State, and may not be appointed from among persons having the nationality of the receiving State except with that State's consent, which may be withdrawn at any time (Article 8). Thirdly, the receiving State has a right to limit the size of a mission to what it considers reasonable and normal, and to refuse, on a non-discriminatory basis, to accept officials of a particular category (Article 11); the sending State may not, without the prior express consent of the receiving State, establish offices forming part of the mission in localities other than that in which the mission itself is established (Article 12).

Real example — how the categories work in practice in Delhi

India's Ministry of External Affairs publishes and maintains the diplomatic list and the list of persons entitled to privileges under the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972. When an embassy's driver ("service staff") is involved in a road accident, the claim proceeds against him and the mission is asked to waive any immunity; when a first secretary ("diplomatic agent") is alleged to have committed an offence, the police may not arrest him and the remedy of the receiving State is to demand waiver or to declare him persona non grata under Article 9. That difference in outcome — one accident, two categories, two entirely different legal routes — is the practical reason why Table 7.8 has to be memorised.

↑ top

E. Appointment, Agrément, Precedence and Reception (Articles 4–19)

Diplomatic relations are consensual from beginning to end. Article 2 states the rule in one sentence — "the establishment of diplomatic relations between States, and of permanent diplomatic missions, takes place by mutual consent" — and every step thereafter reflects that consent: consent to the person (agrément), consent to the mission's size and offices, consent to the mission's very presence (which may be withdrawn by declaring the head of mission persona non grata or by breaking off relations). This is the point to make when a question asks whether there is a "right of legation": there is a capacity to establish diplomatic relations, but no right to have them. Verma puts it exactly: "though every independent State has the capacity to establish diplomatic relations, there is no such right of legation vested in a State under international law", and the establishment of relations need not be accompanied by the establishment of permanent missions, just as recognition of a State need not be followed by diplomatic relations.

1. The seven steps from appointment to reception

  • Step 1 — Agrément (Article 4). The sending State "must make certain that the agrément of the receiving State has been given for the person it proposes to accredit as head of the mission to that State". The receiving State "is not obliged to give reasons to the sending State for a refusal of agrément". The request for agrément is confidential, made through the diplomatic channel or by a senior official of the sending State's mission; the receiving State's refusal is unappealable and unexplained, and in practice the person's name is withdrawn quietly.
  • Step 2 — Appointment and notification (Articles 7 and 10). The head of mission is appointed by the sending State; the Ministry for Foreign Affairs of the receiving State must be notified of the appointment of members of the mission, their arrival and final departure or the termination of their functions, of the arrival and departure of family members, and of the engagement and discharge of persons resident in the receiving State who are members of the mission or private servants entitled to privileges (Article 10(1)). Where possible, prior notification of arrival and final departure is also given (Article 10(2)).
  • Step 3 — Arrival and the taking up of functions (Article 13). The head of mission is considered as having taken up his functions either when he has presented his credentials or when he has notified his arrival and a true copy of the credentials has been presented to the Foreign Ministry, "in accordance with the practice prevailing in the receiving State which shall be applied in a uniform manner". The order of presentation of credentials or of a true copy is determined by the date and time of arrival (Article 13(2)).
  • Step 4 — Precedence (Articles 16–18). Heads of mission take precedence within their class in the order of the date and time of taking up their functions; alterations in credentials not involving a change of class do not affect precedence; the practice relating to the representative of the Holy See is preserved (Article 16). The precedence of members of the diplomatic staff is notified by the head of mission to the receiving State (Article 17), and the procedure of reception must be uniform for each class (Article 18).
  • Step 5 — Reporting for duty and registration. In practice the receiving State's protocol division registers the new mission and issues identity cards; in India, the Ministry of External Affairs issues the certificates and, where required, the special identity documents referred to in the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972.
  • Step 6 — The permanent presence. Members of the diplomatic staff are, in principle, to be nationals of the sending State (Article 8(1)); a charge may be appointed ad interim if the head's post falls vacant (Article 19); and the mission may not open offices outside its seat without the receiving State's prior express consent (Article 12).
  • Step 7 — Termination. The function of a diplomatic agent ends on notification by the sending State that it has come to an end, or on notification by the receiving State that, under Article 9(2), it refuses to recognise him as a member of the mission (Article 43). Termination of the whole mission may occur by recall, by rupture of relations, by the achievement of the mission's object, or by the expiry of its stipulated time; war does not ipso facto terminate a mission, though in practice the delivery of passports to the envoy and his staff does. Verma lists these modes of termination in the order: recall of the envoy (with the letter of recall and the lettre de récréance in return), notification, persona non grata, achievement or expiry of the object, and armed conflict.

2. Persona non grata — Article 9, the receiving State's principal weapon

Article 9(1) — "The receiving State may at any time and without having to explain its decision, notify the sending State that the head of the mission or any member of the diplomatic staff of the mission is persona non grata or that any other member of the staff of the mission is not acceptable. In any such case, the sending State shall, as appropriate, either recall the person concerned or terminate his functions with the mission. A person may be declared non grata or not acceptable before arriving in the territory of the receiving State."
Article 9(2) — "If the sending State refuses or fails within a reasonable period to carry out its obligations under paragraph 1 of this article, the receiving State may refuse to recognize the person concerned as a member of the mission."

Three features of Article 9 must be underlined. First, no reasons need be given, and the decision is not reviewable by any tribunal — the receiving State's discretion is plenary. Secondly, the sanction is not punishment of the individual but removal of recognition: the sending State may name someone else, or simply leave the post vacant. Thirdly, and this is the point the ICJ made in Tehran (at paragraphs 85–86 of the judgment, discussed in the case digest below), Article 9 is the remedy that diplomatic law itself provides for abuse — the remedy which Iran did not use. Because the sending State's refusal to recall the person entails the "almost immediate loss of his privileges and immunities", the ejection of the person is in practice secured without any use of force against him.

Real examples of Article 9

Iraq–Pakistan, 1973. Pakistan, suspecting that arms were being smuggled for Baloch insurgents, told the Iraqi Ambassador that evidence showed arms stored in the Iraqi Embassy in Islamabad; on the Ambassador's refusal to permit a search, armed police raided the premises in his presence and recovered a large quantity of arms. Pakistan declared the Iraqi Ambassador and an attaché persona non grata and recalled its own ambassador. Verma's assessment is that the raid was a clear breach of Article 41(3) (premises must not be used incompatibly with the mission's functions), but that a forcible entry may be justified in an extreme case of abuse — and, in that case, ex post facto.

Israel, 1982. India declared the Israeli Consul, Mr. Hassan, persona non grata for statements which India described as interference in its internal affairs — an example of the VCCR Article 23 counterpart of Article 9 (see §7.5).

Espionage cases. India recalled its High Commissioner to Zambia, Mr. Ravi Tandon (1981), after remarks on President Kaunda's book; and the deputy military attaché at the French Embassy in Delhi, Mr. Allan Bolley (1985), was recalled after being found involved in espionage — Verma's examples of the two ends of the spectrum: displeasure and misconduct.

F. The Functions of the Mission (Article 3)

The functions explain the immunities. Article 3 is written as a list, and Verma rightly calls it "very comprehensive":

Article 3(1) — "The functions of a diplomatic mission consist, inter alia, in: (a) representing the sending State in the receiving State; (b) protecting in the receiving State the interests of the sending State and of its nationals, within the limits permitted by international law; (c) negotiating with the Government of the receiving State; (d) ascertaining by all lawful means conditions and developments in the receiving State, and reporting thereon to the Government of the sending State; (e) promoting friendly relations between the sending State and the receiving State, and developing their economic, cultural and scientific relations."
Article 3(2) — nothing in the Convention prevents the performance of consular functions by a diplomatic mission.

Under customary international law, before the Convention, three functions were regarded as essential — protection of the interests of the sending State and its nationals, negotiation with the receiving government, and observation and reporting — and the course notes (following Kapoor and the older manuals) describe the ambassador's functions in the same practical terms: negotiation (he is the mouthpiece and representative of his State, negotiating on its behalf), observation (attending social and official gatherings and gathering information "with a serpent's ear and an eagle's eye"), reporting (keeping his government informed of all political and other developments), and a miscellaneous group (protecting the interests of nationals in the receiving State, officiating at marriages, keeping records of births and deaths). Article 3(1) adds the fifth and modern function, the promotion of friendly relations, and modern practice adds the conduct of public diplomacy and consular work under Article 3(2).

The link between function and immunity — the examiner's favourite sentence

Every privilege in the Convention is traceable to a function in Article 3. The premises are inviolable so that the mission can represent and negotiate (Article 3(1)(a), (c)); archives and correspondence are inviolable "at any time and wherever they may be" so that the mission can report (Article 3(1)(d)); freedom of movement is guaranteed so that the mission can ascertain conditions (Article 3(1)(d)); and the person of the diplomatic agent is inviolable so that none of these functions is performed under duress. This is the functional theory of immunity (§7.0 B) applied article by article — and it is why the Convention's preamble insists that privileges "are not to benefit individuals but to ensure the efficient performance of the functions of diplomatic missions as representing States".

G. The Immunities Ladder — Who Gets What

Fig. 7.2 — The immunities ladder. Read downwards: each step down the mission's hierarchy loses a slice of protection, and everything below the line of Article 38 depends on the receiving State's own grant.

The ladder is the answer to the common question "do all members of an embassy enjoy diplomatic immunity?" — No. Three propositions should be stated: (i) full diplomatic immunity belongs to diplomatic agents (Article 1(e)) and to members of their families forming part of their household who are not nationals of the receiving State (Article 37(1)); (ii) administrative and technical staff receive the same personal inviolability and criminal immunity, but their civil and administrative immunity is confined to acts in the course of their duties (Article 37(2)); (iii) service staff and private servants have no general immunity at all (Article 37(3)–(4)). A fourth, and important, proposition is Article 38: the receiving State's own nationals and permanent residents on the mission's staff enjoy only official-act immunity, however senior they may be.

H. Examination Angle for 7.1

Five question-types, and how to answer each

(1) "Who are diplomatic agents? Explain their types/classes." Answer in three layers: definition (Article 1(e)); classes (Article 14, with the older four classes of 1815 noted and Article 14(2) explaining that class today affects only precedence); and categories of staff (Article 1(c) with Article 37's graded immunities). Finish with a line on special missions and representation to international organisations.

(2) "What is the basis of diplomatic privileges and immunities?" The four theories from §7.0 B, then the preamble of the VCDR ("not to benefit individuals but to ensure the efficient performance of the functions"), then the functional theory as the dominant one, illustrated by Articles 22, 24, 27 and 29.

(3) "Can the receiving State refuse to accept a head of mission? Can it expel a diplomat?" Yes and yes: agrément may be refused without reasons (Article 4(2)); any member of the mission may be declared non grata at any time and without explanation (Article 9(1)); failure to recall entails loss of recognition (Article 9(2)). Add Tehran at paragraphs 85–86 for the proposition that this is the lawful remedy for abuse, and that a State which instead attacks the mission forfeits the protection that comes from having used the remedy.

(4) "State the functions of a diplomatic mission." Article 3(1)(a)–(e) verbatim, plus the customary trio (protection, negotiation, observation and reporting — Verma), plus Article 3(2) on consular functions performed by a mission.

(5) A problem question with an embassy, its staff and a crime. Identify the status of each person (Article 1 categories), the applicable immunity (Articles 29–31, 37, 38), the remedy of the receiving State (waiver under Article 32, persona non grata under Article 9, or severance of relations), and — where the mission itself has been attacked — the obligations under Articles 22(2), 24, 25, 26, 27 and 29, with Tehran as authority. This is exactly the shape of the University's 2025 question, for which a full model answer is given in the PYQ chapter.

One-paragraph answer to memorise

"A diplomatic agent is, by Article 1(e) of the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961, the head of the mission or a member of the diplomatic staff of the mission. Heads of mission fall into three classes under Article 14 — ambassadors or nuncios accredited to Heads of State; envoys, ministers and internuncios accredited to Heads of State; and chargés d'affaires accredited to Foreign Ministers — the older four-fold classification of the Congress of Vienna having been abandoned, and class now determining precedence and etiquette alone (Article 14(2)). The staff of the mission is divided into diplomatic, administrative and technical, and service staff, with private servants standing outside it; and Article 37 grades their privileges so that full immunity is enjoyed by diplomatic agents and their households, an abridged immunity by administrative and technical staff, functional immunity only by service staff, and whatever the receiving State allows by private servants — while, under Article 38, nationals of and permanent residents in the receiving State enjoy only immunity in respect of official acts."

↑ top

7.2Consuls

The consul is the oldest institution in the law of foreign relations and the most misunderstood. He looks like a diplomat, works beside a diplomat, and is often told by the public that he "enjoys diplomatic immunity" — and almost everything about him in law is different. He is not accredited to the receiving State (as an ambassador is to the Head of State), but admitted to the exercise of his functions by a licence called the exequatur; he is confined to a consular district rather than accredited to the whole country; his functions are commercial, notarial, administrative and protective rather than political; and his immunities are not those of a diplomatic agent but the narrower, function-based immunities of the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963. This head of the syllabus — 7.2, "Consuls" — is therefore studied as a contrast: who the consul is, what he may do, how he is admitted and removed, and precisely how he differs from the diplomat. The University's 2025 paper asked exactly that, in the first limb of question 7: "Explain the difference between diplomats and consuls." A model answer is given in the PYQ chapter; the material on which it is built is here.

A. Who Consuls Are — and the Four Classes (VCCR Article 9)

The Vienna Convention on Consular Relations was adopted on 24 April 1963 — on the initiative, as the course notes record, of the International Law Commission, which produced the draft articles on consular relations out of the "fairly uniform practices evidenced by a large number of bilateral treaties". It entered into force on 19 March 1967 and India acceded to it in November 1977. Verma's formulation of the office is worth quoting for the answer script: consuls "are the representatives of their State in a foreign country, but they are not accredited to the receiving State and, therefore, are not entitled to the privileges and immunities accorded to diplomatic agents. They are often appointed under a commission issued by the governments, and the receiving State accepts them by issuing 'exequatur' … They generally look after the commercial and trade interests of the sending State and its nationals."

Article 1 supplies the definitions, and Article 9 the classes:

Article 1(a) — "consular post" means any consulate-general, consulate, vice-consulate or consular agency. (b) — "consular district" means the area assigned to a consular post for the exercise of consular functions. (c) — "head of consular post" means the person charged with the duty of acting in that capacity. (d) — "consular officer" means any person, including the head of a consular post, entrusted in that capacity with the exercise of consular functions. (e) — "consular employee" means any person employed in the administrative or technical service of a consular post. (k) — "consular archives" includes all the papers, documents, correspondence, books, films, tapes and registers of the consular post, together with the ciphers and codes, the card-indexes and any article of furniture intended for their protection or safe-keeping.

Article 9(1) — "Heads of consular posts are divided into four classes, namely: (a) consuls-general; (b) consuls; (c) vice-consuls; (d) consular agents." Article 9(2) adds that this classification does not restrict the right of States to fix other designations for consular officers who are not heads of post.

The course notes and Verma set out the same four classes in descriptive terms, and add the practice: the consul-general is often appointed by the Head of State, is the head of the consulate-general and appoints the consuls and vice-consuls; consular agents are appointed either by the consul-general or by the vice-consul. A second and more important classification cross-cuts the first: under Article 1(2) consular officers are of two categories — career consular officers and honorary consular officers — and the Convention's Chapter II governs posts headed by career officers while Chapter III governs posts headed by honorary officers. The same post (a vice-consulate in a port city, for instance) may be held by either, with materially different privileges.

B. Consular Posts, Districts, the Commission and the Exequatur

  • Establishment by consent (Article 2 and Article 4). "The establishment of consular relations between States takes place by mutual consent" (Article 2(1)); consent to the establishment of diplomatic relations implies, unless otherwise stated, consent to the establishment of consular relations (Article 2(2)); and — a rule of great practical importance — "the severance of diplomatic relations shall not ipso facto involve the severance of consular relations" (Article 2(3)). A consular post may be established in the territory of the receiving State only with that State's consent (Article 4(1)).
  • The consular commission (Article 11). The head of a consular post is provided by the sending State with a document, in the form of a commission or similar instrument, made out for each appointment, certifying his capacity; it is transmitted to the receiving State through the diplomatic channel.
  • The exequatur (Article 12). "The head of a consular post is admitted to the exercise of his functions by an authorization from the receiving State termed an exequatur, whatever the form of this authorization" (Article 12(1)). A State which refuses an exequatur is not obliged to give reasons (Article 12(2)); and, subject to Articles 13 and 15, a head of consular post "shall not enter upon his duties until he has received an exequatur" (Article 12(3)).
  • Provisional admission (Articles 13–15). Pending delivery of the exequatur the head of post may be admitted provisionally, in which case the Convention applies as if he had been admitted (Article 13). Once admitted, even provisionally, the receiving State must notify the competent authorities of the consular district and enable him to carry out his duties (Article 14). If the head of post is unable to act or the post is vacant, an acting head of post may act provisionally, his name being notified in advance, and where he is neither a diplomatic agent nor a consular officer of the sending State, the receiving State may make his admission conditional on its consent (Article 15).
  • Precedence (Article 21), nationality (Article 22) and removal (Article 23). Precedence among the consular officers of a post is notified to the receiving State; consular officers should in principle be nationals of the sending State and may not be appointed from among nationals of the receiving State except with that State's express consent, which may be withdrawn at any time (Article 22). Article 23 mirrors Article 9 of the VCDR: the receiving State may at any time notify the sending State that a consular officer is persona non grata or that another member of the consular staff is not acceptable; if the sending State fails to recall or terminate him, the receiving State may withdraw the exequatur or cease to consider him a member of the consular staff; and it "is not obliged to give to the sending State reasons for its decision" (Article 23(4)). The functions of a member of a consular post come to an end, under Article 25, on notification by the sending State, on withdrawal of the exequatur, or on notification that the receiving State has ceased to consider him a member of the consular staff.
Exequatur in one line

Agrément admits the person proposed as head of mission; exequatur admits the head of consular post to the exercise of his functions. Neither requires reasons to be given; neither is reviewable; and withdrawal of the exequatur ends the consul's functions and his privileges (Articles 23(2), 25(b)).

C. The Functions of a Consular Post (VCCR Article 5)

Article 5 is the longest list of functions in either Convention — thirteen sub-paragraphs — and it explains why the consul's immunity is carefully calibrated to official acts. Verma summarises the office as looking after "commercial and trade interests" and, "quite often", the granting of passports and visas, the execution of notarial acts, the registration of marriages, deaths and births, and the supervision and inspection of vessels and aircraft attributed to the sending State. Article 5 puts it as follows:

Table 7.9 — The consular functions under VCCR Article 5, grouped for memory
GroupFunction (Article 5 sub-paragraph)The examiner's point
Protection of interestsProtecting in the receiving State the interests of the sending State and of its nationals, both individuals and bodies corporate, within the limits permitted by international law (a); helping and assisting nationals (e); safeguarding the interests of nationals in cases of succession mortis causa (g); safeguarding the interests of minors and others lacking full capacity (h)The protective function. Note the repeated qualification "within the limits permitted by international law" and "in accordance with the laws and regulations of the receiving State": consuls act within the local law, unlike diplomats who act through the Foreign Ministry.
Relations and informationFurthering the development of commercial, economic, cultural and scientific relations and otherwise promoting friendly relations (b); ascertaining by all lawful means conditions and developments in the commercial, economic, cultural and scientific life of the receiving State, reporting thereon and giving information to persons interested (c)The "promotion and reporting" function — the counterpart of Article 3(1)(d)–(e) of the VCDR, but confined to commercial, economic, cultural and scientific matters.
Documents and civil-registry workIssuing passports and travel documents to nationals and visas to persons wishing to travel to the sending State (d); acting as notary and civil registrar and performing administrative functions, if the local law permits (f)The everyday work of most consulates, and the reason why consular immunity attaches to "acts performed in the exercise of consular functions" (Article 43).
Litigation and legal assistanceRepresenting or arranging representation for nationals before the tribunals and authorities of the receiving State to obtain provisional measures for the preservation of their rights (i); transmitting judicial and extrajudicial documents or executing letters rogatory (j)The functions which generate most modern litigation — and the functions with which Article 36 (communication with nationals) is designed to connect.
Ships, aircraft and crewsExercising rights of supervision and inspection over vessels having the nationality of the sending State and aircraft registered in it, and over their crews (k); extending assistance to such vessels and aircraft and their crews, taking statements, examining and stamping ship's papers, investigating incidents and settling disputes between master, officers and seamen (l)Historically the consul's oldest function; and the reason why coastal States and port authorities deal with consuls in shipping matters such as crew abandonment, wages and discipline.
ResidualPerforming any other functions entrusted by the sending State which are not prohibited by the local law, or to which no objection is taken, or which are referred to in agreements in force (m)The open-ended clause; it is by this route that modern functions (for example, assistance in civil-aviation accidents or in disaster relief) are read in.

Two structural points complete the picture of consular functions. First, consular functions are exercised by consular posts, but may also be exercised by diplomatic missions in accordance with the Convention (Article 3), and Article 70 spells out how: the VCCR applies to a mission exercising consular functions so far as the context permits, the names of members of the mission assigned to the consular section are notified, the mission may address local authorities and — if the local law or an agreement permits — the central authorities, and the privileges of those members remain those of diplomatic agents. Secondly, a consular officer may in special circumstances, with the consent of the receiving State, exercise his functions outside his district (Article 6), and in a third State only with the consent of that State (Article 7).

D. Diplomat versus Consul — the Comparison the 2025 Paper Asked For

The single most useful table in this topic is the comparison. It is the answer to the 2025 question, the framework for the Khobragade problem (§7.5 K), and the reason why Jadhav is a consular case and Tehran a diplomatic one.

Table 7.10 — Diplomat and consul compared, point by point
Point of comparisonDiplomatic agent (VCDR 1961)Consul (VCCR 1963)
RepresentationRepresents the sending State politically; accredited to the Head of State (ambassador) or to the Foreign Minister (chargé d'affaires); performs the functions of Article 3.Represents the sending State "for particular purposes" in a consular district; not accredited to the receiving State; performs the functions of Article 5 — commercial, notarial, protective, administrative.
Appointment and admissionAgrément of the receiving State for the head of mission (Article 4); letters of credence; taking up functions under Article 13.Commission or similar instrument (Article 11) transmitted through the diplomatic channel; admitted by exequatur (Article 12(1)); may act provisionally pending the exequatur (Article 13).
Extent of immunity of the personPerson inviolable; not liable to any form of arrest or detention; complete criminal immunity (Articles 29, 31(1)).Not liable to arrest or detention pending trial except in the case of a grave crime and pursuant to a decision of the competent judicial authority (Article 41(1)); imprisonment only in execution of a judicial decision of final effect (Article 41(2)).
Immunity from jurisdictionCriminal: absolute. Civil and administrative: general, subject to the three exceptions in Article 31(1).Only "in respect of acts performed in the exercise of consular functions" (Article 43(1)); no immunity in a civil action on a contract in which the consul did not contract expressly or impliedly as agent of the sending State, or for damage caused by a vehicle, vessel or aircraft (Article 43(2)).
Inviolability of premisesAbsolute: agents of the receiving State may not enter except with the consent of the head of the mission (Article 22(1)).Partial: the authorities may not enter the part used exclusively for consular work except with the consent of the head of post (or his designee, or the head of the diplomatic mission), but consent may be assumed in case of fire or other disaster requiring prompt protective action (Article 31(2)); consent is a matter of permission, and consular premises are not inviolable in the diplomatic sense.
Archives and documentsInviolable at any time and wherever they may be (Article 24).Inviolable at all times and wherever they may be, provided they are kept separate from other papers and from the private correspondence and professional materials of the persons working at the post (Article 33 read with Article 61 for honorary posts).
Communication and bagsFree communication for all official purposes; the diplomatic bag "shall not be opened or detained" (Article 27(3)) — absolute.Free communication for all official purposes (Article 35(1)); the consular bag may not be opened or detained, but if the receiving State's authorities have serious reason to believe it contains something else, they may request that it be opened in their presence by an authorised representative of the sending State, and if the request is refused, the bag is returned to its place of origin (Article 35(3)).
Giving evidenceNot obliged to give evidence as a witness (Article 31(2)).May be called as a witness; a consular officer may decline, in which case no coercive measure or penalty may be applied; members of the post are in any event under no obligation to give evidence on matters connected with their functions or to produce official correspondence (Article 44).
Third StatesInviolability and such immunities as are required for transit (Article 40).All immunities provided by the Convention as required for transit (Article 54).
Ambit of the officeNational territory of the receiving State (with restrictions on prohibited zones under Article 26).The consular district, and beyond it only in special circumstances with the consent of the receiving State (Article 6).
Honorary variantNot applicable — every diplomatic agent holds a commission from his State.Part-time, often local businessmen may be honorary consular officers: Article 43 immunity still applies (Article 58(2)), but personal inviolability under Article 41 does not, and family members get no privileges (Article 58(3)).

For the answer script, the difference can be compressed into one sentence: "The diplomat is accredited, the consul is admitted; the diplomat represents the State politically, the consul serves its nationals and its commerce locally; and the diplomat's immunity is personal and near-absolute, while the consul's immunity is functional, limited to acts performed in the exercise of consular functions, and subject to the grave-crime exception."

E. Honorary Consular Officers

Chapter III of the VCCR (Articles 58–68) governs consular posts headed by honorary consular officers. The institution itself is optional: "each State is free to decide whether it will appoint or receive honorary consular officers" (Article 68). The scheme is deliberately narrow, and it is worth stating its structure precisely because examiners like to test it.

  • What applies to the post. Articles 28, 29 (use of the flag and coat of arms), 30 (accommodation), 34, 35, 36, 37, 38 and 39, together with Article 54(3) and Article 55(2)–(3), apply to posts headed by honorary officers; and Articles 59–62 add special rules (Article 58(1)) — protection of the premises against intrusion or damage and of the peace and dignity of the post (Article 59); exemption from taxation of consular premises owned or leased by the sending State (Article 60); inviolability of the consular archives, provided they are kept separate from private correspondence and professional materials (Article 61); and customs exemption for articles for the official use of the post (Article 62).
  • What applies to the officer.
