Articles 42 (notification of arrest, detention or prosecution), 43 (immunity from jurisdiction in respect of acts performed in the exercise of consular functions, with the two civil exceptions), 44(3) (no obligation to give evidence about official matters), 45 (waiver) and 53 (beginning and end of privileges), together with Article 55(1), apply to honorary consular officers; and Articles 63–67 add the rest (Article 58(2)) — criminal proceedings to be conducted with respect for his official position and with minimum delay in institution (Article 63); a duty on the receiving State to accord such protection as his official position requires (Article 64); exemption from alien registration and residence permits (Article 65); exemption from dues and taxes on remuneration and emoluments received from the sending State (Article 66); and exemption from personal services, public services and military obligations (Article 67).
  • What is withheld. Personal inviolability under Article 41 is not extended to honorary consular officers, and Article 58(3) provides that privileges and immunities are not accorded to members of the family of an honorary consular officer or of a consular employee at a post headed by one. Article 58(4) forbids the exchange of consular bags between two honorary posts in different States without the consent of the two receiving States.
The one-line examination point on honorary consuls

An honorary consul keeps the functional immunity of Article 43 — he cannot be sued for acts done in the exercise of consular functions — but he does not enjoy personal inviolability, and his family enjoys nothing. This is the clearest proof in either Convention that consular privileges are attached to the function and not to the person.

F. Indian Practice — Consular Relations and the 1977 Accession

India's consular relations are conducted within the VCCR framework: India acceded to the Convention in November 1977 (Verma's date), and administers consular work through the Consular, Passport and Visa Division of the Ministry of External Affairs and through consular posts in cities abroad (consulates-general, consulates and consular wings of high commissions and embassies). Three features of Indian practice deserve the student's attention.

  • Consular work is done largely through diplomatic missions. India's larger missions include consular sections, which is precisely the arrangement contemplated by VCCR Article 70 (and Article 3(2) of the VCDR): consular functions performed by a mission, with the members assigned to the consular section continuing to enjoy the diplomatic privileges of the 1961 Convention. The diplomatic and consular character of such staff is what made the Tehran case a case under both Conventions.
  • Bilateral consular agreements supplement the Convention. The best-known is the India–Pakistan Agreement on Consular Access of 21 May 2008, examined in detail in the Jadhav case: it provides for immediate notification of arrest, detention or imprisonment (point (ii)), consular access within three months (point (iv)), release and repatriation within one month of confirmation of national status and completion of sentence (point (v)), and, in point (vi), that "[i]n case of arrest, detention or sentence made on political or security grounds, each side may examine the case on its merits". The ICJ held in 2019 that point (vi) does not displace or restrict Article 36 of the VCCR — the agreement is a subsequent agreement of the kind contemplated by Article 73(2) that "confirms, supplements, extends or amplifies" the Convention, not one that derogates from it.
  • Consular protection is a live political question in India. Indian consular officers deal with the arrest and detention of Indian nationals abroad (the classic case being the notification and access claims at the heart of Jadhav), with the welfare of Indian workers in the Gulf and in South-East Asia, with the repatriation of stranded seafarers, and with the verification of nationality in immigration detention. Where a foreign State denies access, India's remedy is diplomatic and, if the VCCR Optional Protocol applies between the two States, judicial: an application to the ICJ under Article I of the Protocol, as in Jadhav.

G. Examination Angle for 7.2

Four question-types on consuls

(1) "Who are consuls? What are their functions?" Definitions from VCCR Article 1; the four classes under Article 9; the two categories (career and honorary) under Article 1(2); functions under Article 5 grouped as in Table 7.9; then the exequatur (Articles 11–13) and removal (Article 23).

(2) "Explain the difference between diplomats and consuls." Table 7.10 in prose: representation, mode of appointment and admission, inviolability, jurisdiction, premises, archives, bags, evidence, third States, district, honorary variant. Conclude with the one-sentence compression at the end of §7.2 D. This is the 2025 paper's first limb — write it as a structured answer with headings, not as a paragraph.

(3) "Consuls do not enjoy full diplomatic immunity. Discuss." Trace the functional basis; Article 41's grave-crime exception; Article 43's function-limited immunity and its two civil exceptions; the partial inviolability of consular premises and the fire presumption in Article 31(2); and the "open or return" rule for the consular bag in Article 35(3). Add the reason: consuls exercise commercial, notarial and protective functions which bring them into daily contact with local law and local courts, so that absolute immunity would defeat rather than serve the function.

(4) A problem question involving an arrested national or a detained ship. Identify the consul's functions under Article 5(e), (i), (k) and (l); the notification duties of the receiving State under Article 36(1)(b) and Article 42; the individual rights recognised in LaGrand (2001), Avena (2004) and Jadhav (2019); and, if the facts involve an arrest for an ordinary crime, the Article 41(1) gateway ("grave crime", competent judicial authority).

↑ top

7.3Sovereign and Non-Sovereign Acts

This head of the syllabus contains the whole of the modern law of State immunity, and it is the head in which the two prescribed cases that the casebook does not print — The Schooner Exchange (case 40) and Jurisdictional Immunities of the State (Germany v. Italy) (case 42) — do their work. The question the head asks is deceptively simple: when is the conduct of a foreign State "sovereign" so that the local courts must stay their hand, and when is it "non-sovereign" — commercial, managerial, private — so that the foreign State must answer like any other litigant? The answer has four parts: the rationale of immunity (Marshall's propositions and the four principles in Verma); the historical movement from absolute to restrictive immunity; the test by which acts are classified (jure imperii or jure gestionis, judged by the nature of the act rather than its purpose); and the instruments in which the classification is now written down — the UN Convention of 2004, the European Convention of 1972, the American and British statutes of 1976 and 1978, and, in India, sections 86–87 of the Code of Civil Procedure. Cases 40 and 42 sit at the two ends of that story: Schooner Exchange (1812) stated the immunity in its absolute form, and Germany v. Italy (2012) restated its modern limits — and its modern stubbornness — in the face of the most serious violations of international humanitarian law.

A. State Immunity — What the Two Words Mean

Verma's definition is the one to reproduce: "The concept of 'sovereign immunity' or 'State immunity' is imbibed in the customary international law, according to which a foreign sovereign or State is not amenable to the jurisdiction of the State of the forum unless voluntarily submitting to the jurisdiction of the local courts and the law enforcement agencies." Six features of the concept must be stated before any case is discussed.

  • It is a rule of customary international law, and now also of treaties and statutes. It has been codified in the European Convention on State Immunity, 1972, in national statutes (the US Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act, 1976; the UK State Immunity Act, 1978; the Australian Foreign States Immunities Act, 1985; the Canadian State Immunity Act, 1982; and the statutes of Pakistan, Singapore and South Africa), and in the UN Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, 2004.
  • It attaches to the State and its organs, and originally to the person of the sovereign. "The concept was earlier attached to the person of the sovereign but later extended to the State and its organs. The two terms 'State immunity' and 'sovereign immunity' have become interchangeable." In its strict sense, Sinclair notes, "sovereign immunity" refers to the immunity of a personal sovereign or Head of State present in the territory of another State — a narrower usage which the modern law has generalised.
  • There is no bar against the foreign State itself suing. Immunity protects a State from being made a defendant; a foreign State may bring proceedings in the courts of the forum State, and if it does so it is bound by the ordinary incidents of the suit — set-off and counterclaim by the other side. The same principle is written into diplomatic law at Article 32(3) of the VCDR.
  • It extends beyond the State to certain persons and things. Verma lists its extension to "the person of the sovereign from arrest or detention within a foreign territory", to foreign troops present under licence, to diplomatic agents and other representatives of the foreign sovereign, and to property within the territory of the forum — "public ships belonging to the foreign State or foreign sovereign are immune from seizure or detention or judicial disposition". A former ruler or sovereign of a foreign country is entitled to immunity only if the forum State regards him as a foreign sovereign.
  • It covers acts and property, jurisdiction and execution. The legislative, administrative and executive acts of a foreign State and its agents cannot be called into question by the territorial State; the immunity is "referable to the entire judicial process and the execution of the judgment rendered"; and it applies as much to proceedings in personam as to proceedings in rem — a sovereign cannot be impleaded even indirectly (United States v. Dollfus Mieg et Cie [1952] A.C. 582 (HL); Rahimtoola v. Nizam of Hyderabad [1957] 3 All ER 441 (PC); [1958] A.C. 379).
  • It may be waived — and the waiver is construed narrowly. The immunity may be waived by express or implied consent. An express waiver must be made with full knowledge of the consequences and with the authority of the foreign sovereign; an implied waiver depends on all the circumstances, and a mere clause in a contract submitting to the jurisdiction has not always been accepted as amounting to waiver (though section 2 of the UK Act of 1978 and section 1605(a)(1) of the US Act of 1976 now negative that position). Waiver of immunity from jurisdiction does not automatically include waiver of immunity from execution, for which a separate waiver is necessary. And if the foreign State claims immunity for property, "it should at least show right to possession or control in order to claim immunity" — it is not bound to prove its title completely (Compania Naviera Vascongado v. S.S. Christina [1938] A.C. 485).

B. The Rationale — Marshall's Propositions and the Four Principles

The rationale is the part of this head in which examiners most often ask for "principles". Verma sets out four, all traceable to The Schooner Exchange and to Lord Wright's speech in The Christina. Learn them as a list and be able to add a sentence to each.

The four principles, with the authority for each

(1) Par in parem non habet imperium — an equal has no authority over an equal. Lord Wright in The Christina: "there are general principles of international law according to which a sovereign State is held to be immune from the jurisdiction of another sovereign State … the rule may be said to be based on the principle of par in parent non habet imperium". Marshall C.J. in Schooner Exchange expressed the same idea through the equality and absolute independence of sovereigns, which give rise to a class of cases in which each sovereign is understood to waive part of the territorial jurisdiction that is otherwise the attribute of every nation.

(2) The principle of non-intervention. The acts, policy and transactions of a foreign State should not be scrutinised by the courts of another State, since to do so would amount to interference in that State's internal affairs. Verma cites F.A. Mann's study on the subject.

(3) Comity and reciprocity. Immunity reflects "the accepted rules of mutual conduct as between States, which each State adopts in relation to other States and expects other States to adopt in relation to itself"; failure to accord it "may otherwise be considered as an unfriendly act" (Buck v. Attorney-General [1965] Ch. 745).

(4) Implied grant of licence. The foreign sovereign or State enters the territory of another by an implied licence, which carries with it an implied obligation on the territorial State not to derogate from the licence (Wright v. Cantrell (1943) 44 S.R. (N.S.W.) 45). This is Marshall's central insight — the licence to enter is read as containing an exemption from the local jurisdiction.

Two further reasons are commonly added, both drawn from Marshall's own opinion: the functional need "to leave the foreign State, its organs and representatives unencumbered in the pursuit of their mission" (Verma's phrase); and the impracticability of enforcement — that a local court cannot, in the end, execute its judgment against a foreign sovereign, so that the questions involved "are rather questions of policy than of law" and are for diplomatic rather than judicial discussion. In an answer, state the four principles first and then add these two as supporting considerations.

C. Absolute Immunity and Its Decline

The classical position. "Historically, a foreign State could not be subjected to the jurisdiction of the territorial courts than could the State itself. The foreign State enjoyed the absolute immunity for all its acts, whether of public nature (acts jure imperii) or private nature, for example, trading, and non-public functions (acts jure gestionis)." The absolute immunity extended to State organs as well. In Britain, immunity was held to be available even in the face of a contractual submission to the jurisdiction of the English courts, unless the submission was made before the court (Duff Development Co. v. Government of Kelantan [1924] A.C. 797; Kahan v. Pakistan Federation [1951] 2 K.B. 1003).

Why it changed. Verma's explanation is the one to reproduce: with "the increasing involvement of States in trading activities and other non-governmental functions, not many States are now willing to apply the doctrine of absolute immunity"; and the prominence given to the public sector and to State-owned corporations in many national economies, "particularly those belonging to the erstwhile communist bloc and many developing countries, such as India, to develop a modern economy and conduct their business", raised doubts about the rationale of the absolute rule. The result is the restrictive or relative doctrine, which grants immunity for acts of public administration but not for acts that are "truly commercial".

Table 7.11 — From absolute to restrictive immunity: the cases and the instruments
StageAuthorityWhat it decides
Absolute immunityThe Schooner Exchange v. McFaddon (1812) 7 Cranch 116Public armed ships of a foreign sovereign at peace, entering an open port, are exempt from local jurisdiction; exceptions to territorial jurisdiction are traced to the sovereign's consent, express or implied.
Absolute immunityThe Parlement Belge (1880) 5 P.D. 197A Belgian mail vessel, used in part for commercial purposes, was held immune: British courts had no jurisdiction over the person or property of a foreign sovereign.
Absolute immunityMighell v. Sultan of Johore (1894) 1 Q.B. 149A sovereign cannot be sued even for breach of a promise of marriage: immunity attaches to the status, not to the subject-matter.
Absolute immunityCompania Mercantil Argentina v. United States Shipping Board (1924) 40 T.L.R. 601A sovereign does not lose immunity from legal process by entering into a trading contract: an action in personam against a sovereign fails.
Absolute immunityKrajina v. Tass Agency [1949] 2 All ER 274 (CA)Immunity accepted for the defendant as the central information organ of the USSR — the "State organ" extension of the rule.
Absolute immunityBaccus S.R.L. v. Servicio Nacional del Trigo [1957] 1 Q.B. 438 (CA)A Spanish State organ, separately incorporated, held entitled to immunity in a commercial contract with an Italian company — the high-water mark of the absolute rule and of the "instrumentality" problem.
Absolute immunity defendedThai-Europe Tapioca Service v. Government of Pakistan [1975] 2 W.L.R. 1485The West Pakistan Agricultural Development Corporation chartered a ship to carry Polish fertiliser to Karachi; the ship was damaged at Karachi by Indian air raids; before service the Corporation was dissolved and its work taken over by the Ministry of Food and Agriculture; the plea of sovereign immunity was upheld.
The turn — United StatesTate Letter (State Department, May 1952)The State Department announced that it would follow the restrictive theory "in considering requests from foreign governments for a grant of immunity": immunity for sovereign (public) acts, none for private commercial acts. The courts thereafter took over the inquiry (Victory Transport Inc. v. Comisaria General de Abastecimientos y Transporte (1964), listing the political acts — internal administrative acts such as expulsion of an alien; legislative acts such as nationalisation; acts concerning the armed forces; acts concerning diplomatic activity; and public loans).
The turn — United KingdomOwners of the Ship Philippine Admiral v. Wallem Shipping (Hong Kong) Ltd [1976] 1 All ER 71 (PC); Trendtex Trading Corp. v. Central Bank of Nigeria [1977] Q.B. 529 (CA)State-owned vessels engaged in commercial activities are not immune (action in rem); the issue of a letter of credit is a non-governmental function (action in personam). Lord Denning M.R.: "If a government department goes into the market places of the world and buys boots or cement, as a commercial transaction that government department should be subject to all the rules of the market place." These decisions were placed on a statutory footing by the State Immunity Act, 1978.
The turn — legislationEuropean Convention on State Immunity, 1972; US Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act, 1976; UK State Immunity Act, 1978Restrictive immunity embodied in treaty and statute, with a "commercial activity" exception and a separate regime for enforcement.
The surviving difficultyClaim against the Empire of Iran (1963) 45 I.L.R. 57 (Federal Constitutional Court of Germany); I Congreso del Partido [1983] 1 A.C. 244 (HL)Classification of acts as jure imperii or jure gestionis is done by national courts applying national criteria, which hinders uniformity; and a transaction which begins as jure gestionis may become jure imperii at a later stage, in which case immunity may still succeed.
The codificationUN Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, 2004A compromise between absolute and restrictive immunity, following the pattern of the 1972 European Convention and the national statutes; it leaves unclear the position in cases like I Congreso del Partido.

Two further points complete the historical account. First, not every State abandoned absolute immunity: "some countries, mainly developing countries, still adhere to the rule of absolute immunity", and the former Soviet Union maintained until its dissolution that the restrictive doctrine could not apply to socialist States, "since in the political theory such States all governmental acts are sovereign so that the distinction between acts jure imperii and acts jure gestionis has no meaning" — although it accepted the restrictive approach in many bilateral treaties, and other socialist States avoided the problem by trading through corporations with distinct legal personality. Secondly, the entity problem: "there is … no clear State practice regarding the immunity of foreign governmental agencies or instrumentalities, and of semi-public corporations". A separately incorporated legal entity may nonetheless be an organ of the State if the degree of governmental control makes it the State's alter ego (Mellenger v. New Brunswick Development Corporation [1971] 2 All E.R. 593); under section 14 of the UK State Immunity Act, 1978, a "separate entity" — one that is not a department of State but is capable of being sued — is not entitled to State immunity unless it is acting in the exercise of sovereign authority. The contrast between Thai-Europe (immunity upheld) and Trendtex (the Central Bank of Nigeria held to be a legal entity in its own right and not immune) is the best illustration of how fact-sensitive the inquiry is.

D. Jure Imperii and Jure Gestionis — the Nature Test and the Purpose Test

The classification of an act as sovereign or non-sovereign is the pivot of the whole head, and the authority to lead with is the German Federal Constitutional Court's decision in the Claim against the Empire of Iran (1963), which concerned a contract claim by a local firm for the cost of repairs to the heating system of the Iranian Embassy in Cologne. The Court rejected the plea of immunity: the conclusion of such a contract "does not fall within the core of the State's political authority".

"For determining the distinction between acts jure imperii and acts jure gestionis, one should rather refer to the nature of the State transaction or the resulting legal relationships, and not to the motive or purpose of the State activity. It thus depends on whether the foreign State has acted in exercise of its sovereign authority, that is in public law, or like a private person, that is in private law." (Claim against the Empire of Iran (1963) 45 I.L.R. 57, quoted in I Congreso del Partido [1983] 1 A.C. 244.)

Fig. 7.3 — Classifying an act as sovereign or non-sovereign: the four questions to ask, in order.

Four refinements must be added to the basic nature test.

  1. Nature, not purpose — but the purpose test has not disappeared. Where a State buys boots or cement, the seller is not concerned with the purpose to which the purchaser intends to put the goods (Lord Denning in Trendtex). Yet Article 2(2) of the UN Convention, 2004, adopts a nuanced rule: in determining whether a contract or transaction is "commercial", reference should be made primarily to the nature of the contract, "but its purpose can also be taken into account if the parties to the contract so agree, or if, in the practice of the State of the forum, that purpose is relevant to determine the non-commercial character of the contract or transaction".
  2. The nature of the breach may matter as much as the nature of the contract. In I Congreso del Partido [1983] 1 A.C. 244 the House of Lords held that the court must look not only at the nature of the contract but also at the nature of the breach: if the contract is in the nature of an act jure imperii there is immunity; if it is jure gestionis, a defence of immunity may still succeed if the act in breach is itself an act jure imperii. The facts of that case repay study: two Cuban State trading enterprises contracted to carry sugar to Chile; after President Allende's death the Cuban Government ordered the vessel to North Vietnam, where the cargo was discharged; the cargo owners arrested the second vessel, I Congreso, in British waters; the Cuban Government's plea of sovereign immunity was rejected.
  3. The classification of acts by the forum State's courts defeats uniformity. This is Verma's criticism, and it is a good point to end an answer with: "determination of the nature of transaction by national courts would hinder the uniformity of the rule"; the statutes "differ in much of their details"; and the UN Convention, being a compromise, "has also left the position unclear on cases like I Congreso del Partido".
  4. Immunity from jurisdiction does not mean immunity from the applicable substantive law. The act may be unlawful and the State responsible; what immunity does is to prevent the forum State's courts from adjudicating it, leaving the remedy to the diplomatic channel or to the State's own courts. The ICJ's Germany v. Italy judgment says exactly this: rules of State immunity "address different matters" from the rules prohibiting the conduct, and immunity is procedural, not a licence.

↑ top

E. Where the Distinction Is Written Down — the Four Instruments

There is no Indian statute of State immunity; there is a treaty, a statute, a code, and a model. Learn all four, because a good answer moves from custom (case 40) to codification (2004) to the two national statutes (1976, 1978) and finally to the Indian position (§7.3 G).

Table 7.12 — The four instruments of State immunity compared
InstrumentGeneral rule of immunityExceptions and special features
UN Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, 2004 (GA Res. 59/38; opened for signature 17 January 2005)Article 5: "A State enjoys immunity, in respect of itself and its property, from the jurisdiction of the courts of another State", subject to the Convention's provisions.Exceptions in Articles 10–17: commercial transactions (Art. 10 — immunity is lost unless the transaction was concluded between States or the dispute is covered by another agreement); contracts of employment (Art. 11); personal injuries and damage to property (Art. 12 — with the express carve-out for the activities of armed forces during an armed conflict and activities of military forces in the exercise of official duties); ownership, possession and use of property (Art. 13); intellectual and industrial property (Art. 14); participation in companies or other collective bodies (Art. 15); ships owned or operated by a State (Art. 16); and the effect of an arbitration agreement (Art. 17). Article 2(2) defines "commercial" by the nature of the contract primarily, with purpose relevant if the parties so agree or if the practice of the forum State so requires. Article 3 excludes criminal proceedings from the Convention's scope. Articles 18–21 deal separately with measures of constraint (see §7.3 F). Article 30 requires thirty ratifications for entry into force; the Convention has not yet come into force, but it is treated as largely declaratory of customary law and was relied upon by the ICJ in 2012.
European Convention on State Immunity, 1972 (in force 1976), with Additional ProtocolArticle 15: a Contracting State shall be entitled to immunity from the jurisdiction of the courts of another Contracting State save as provided in the Convention.The first treaty embodiment of the restrictive theory: no immunity for obligations to be performed in the forum State (Art. 4), for contracts of employment (Art. 5), for participation in companies (Art. 6), for patents and similar rights (Art. 7), for immovable property and succession (Art. 8), for torts causing injury in the forum State (Art. 9), for taxes and charges (Art. 10), where the State has agreed to arbitration (Art. 12), or as to counterclaims (Art. 11). Article 23 restricts measures of execution; and Article 31 preserves immunities in respect of a Contracting State's armed forces present in the territory of another Contracting State — the predecessor of the carve-out in Article 12 of the 2004 Convention.
US Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act, 1976 (following the Tate Letter of 1952)Section 1604: a foreign State is immune from the jurisdiction of US courts except as provided in sections 1605–1607.Section 1605(a) lists the exceptions: (1) express or implied waiver; (2) commercial activity — with a threefold nexus (carried on in the United States; or an act performed in the United States in connection with a commercial activity elsewhere; or an act outside the United States in connection with a commercial activity elsewhere causing a direct effect in the United States); (3) certain takings of property; (4) rights in property in the United States acquired by succession or gift, or in immovable property there; (5) personal injury or death, or damage to or loss of property, occurring in the United States and caused by the tortious act or omission of the foreign State (with exceptions, including the exercise of discretionary functions); (6) arbitration; and (7)–(9) certain terrorism-related suits. Section 1607 governs counterclaims; sections 1609–1610 deal with attachment and execution (see F).
UK State Immunity Act, 1978Section 1(1): a State is immune from the jurisdiction of the courts of the United Kingdom except as provided in the Act.Section 2 (submission to jurisdiction, including by an agreement or by intervening in proceedings); section 3 (commercial transactions — contracts for the supply of goods or services, loans and similar financial transactions, and obligations to be performed wholly or partly in the United Kingdom; the nature-not-purpose test is written in expressly); section 4 (employment contracts); section 5 (personal injuries and damage to property in the United Kingdom); section 6 (ownership, possession and use of immovable property, and succession); section 7 (patents, trade marks, etc.); section 8 (membership of bodies corporate); section 9 (arbitration); section 10 (ships used for commercial purposes — the statutory reversal of The Parlement Belge); section 11 (separate entities); sections 13–14 (execution; heads of State); section 16 (proceedings to which the Act does not apply, leaving intact the separate regimes of the Vienna Conventions of 1961 and 1963).

Verma's summary of the 2004 Convention is an accurate description of the compromise that the text represents: it "takes a compromise approach between the absolute immunity and restrictive immunity rules", follows "the pattern of the 1972 European Convention on State Immunity and the national legislations brought out recently", "does not cover criminal proceedings, nor does it allow civil actions for human rights abuses against State agents if the abuse has occurred in another country", and leaves the position unclear in cases like I Congreso del Partido. Add Verma's own reservation about all four instruments: their detail differs; the classification of an act is done by national courts on national criteria; and the statutes contain a "most striking lacuna" at the stage of enforcement (see F).

F. Immunity from Jurisdiction and Immunity from Enforcement

Suppose a claim against a foreign State survives the restrictive test, a decree is passed, and the decree-holder seeks to attach the State's bank account or sell its property. The statute books and the cases say, in effect: not so fast. Immunity from execution is a separate and stricter immunity.

Fig. 7.4 — Two stages, two tests: jurisdiction and enforcement must be argued separately, and success at the first does not carry the second.

  • The UN Convention, 2004 (Articles 18–21). No pre-judgment measures of constraint against the property of a foreign State except where the State has expressly consented, has allocated the property for the satisfaction of the claim, or has been shown to possess property in the forum State used for a commercial purpose connected with the claim (Article 18); no post-judgment measures of constraint except on one of those three bases (Article 19); consent to measures is governed by Article 20; and Article 21 identifies categories of property that are not to be considered as used for commercial purposes — property, including bank accounts, used for the purposes of diplomatic missions, consular posts, special missions or missions to international organisations; property of a military character or used for military purposes; property of a central bank; and property forming part of the State's cultural heritage or archives. Article 21 also safeguards the rules of diplomatic and consular immunity.
  • The United States (FSIA sections 1609–1610). Section 1609 states the general rule that the property in the United States of a foreign State is immune from attachment and execution, to which section 1610 provides exceptions — most importantly property "used for a commercial activity in the United States" (section 1610(a)), and the separate rule in section 1610(b) for agencies and instrumentalities. Verma notes that section 1609 "incorporates the customary international law rule, also followed in common law, that the property of a foreign sovereign shall be immune from attachment and execution, unless this immunity has been waived by the foreign sovereign expressly or impliedly".
  • The United Kingdom (SIA section 13). Section 13(2) and (3) make execution available only against property for the time being "in use or intended for use for commercial purposes", and section 13(4) requires that the property be for the time being so used. Verma's criticism applies here: "this half-hearted approach by the adherents to the restrictive immunity rule at the critical stage of the judicial process has failed to carry the rule to its logical conclusion".
  • India (CPC section 86(3)). As will be seen in G, no decree may be executed against the property of any foreign State without the consent of the Central Government in writing.
  • A counter-example: the immovable-property exception at work. In Permanent Mission of India v. City of New York, 551 U.S. 193 (2007), the US Supreme Court construed the FSIA to allow a suit by the City of New York to recover unpaid property taxes levied on real estate owned in the city by India and Mongolia — a neat illustration of Verma's point that the restrictive theory bites on "suits relating to title to land within the territorial State, having vital interest in the land".

G. State Immunity in India — CPC Sections 86–87 and the Case Law

India has no statute of State immunity of the kind enacted in the United States, the United Kingdom, Australia or Canada. Verma's statement of the position is the one to reproduce: "India does not have any specific legislation on the subject, and the matter of sovereign immunity is governed by Secs. 86 and 87 of the Civil Procedure Code."

1. The statutory scheme, section by section

  • Section 86(1). No foreign State may be sued in any court, otherwise than as permitted by the section, "except with the consent of the Central Government certified in writing by a Secretary to that Government". The consent is an administrative act of the executive, and it is the gate through which every suit against a foreign State must pass.
  • Section 86(2). Consent "shall not be given unless it appears to the Central Government" that the foreign State: (a) has instituted a suit in the court against the person desiring to sue it; or (b) by itself or another "trades within the local limits of the jurisdiction of the Court"; or (c) is in possession of immovable property situated within those limits and is to be sued with reference to such property or for money charged thereon; or (d) has expressly or impliedly waived the privilege accorded to it by the section.
  • Section 86(3). Non-execution of a decree against the property of a foreign State without the written consent of the Central Government.
  • Section 86(4). The protection applies, as it applies to a foreign State, to any ruler of a foreign State, any ambassador or envoy of a foreign State, any High Commissioner of a Commonwealth country, and such members of the staff of those persons as the Central Government may specify by general or special order — and such persons cannot be arrested.
  • Section 87. "Style of foreign Rulers as parties to suits": the Ruler of a foreign State may sue, and shall be sued, in the name of his State, though the Central Government may, in giving the consent referred to in section 86, direct that the Ruler be sued in the name of an agent or in any other name. Section 87B applies sections 85 and 86 to the Rulers of former Indian States in respect of causes of action arising before the commencement of the Constitution.
  • The 1972 Act, section 8. Overlapping with the above, the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972, provides that a certificate issued by or under the authority of the Secretary to the Government of India in the Ministry of External Affairs, as to whether a person is entitled to any privilege or immunity, is conclusive evidence of the matter; and no public servant or agent of the Central Government, a State Government or any public authority may enter the premises of a diplomatic mission for the purpose of serving legal process, except with the consent of the head of the mission.

2. The Indian case law

Table 7.13 — The Indian authorities on sovereign immunity
CaseFacts and holdingWhat it establishes
Century Twenty One (P) Ltd. v. Union of India, AIR 1987 Del. 124The Ambassador of Afghanistan had rented residential premises on lease from a private party and refused to vacate on expiry of the lease. The Government of India declined consent to sue. On a writ petition under Article 226 of the Constitution by the landlord, the Delhi High Court distinguished sovereign acts from commercial activities of a foreign sovereign government, "termed the renting of the private premises as commercial activity of a foreign sovereign government", and directed the Government of India to accord sanction to the petitioner to sue the Afghan Ambassador for the recovery of arrears of rent.The leading Indian decision applying the restrictive theory to a lease of private premises. It also shows that the refusal of consent under section 86 is not beyond judicial reach.
Harbhajan Singh v. Union of India, AIR 1987 SC 9The petitioner had done building-maintenance and renovation work at the Algerian Embassy and the residence of the Algerian Ambassador in New Delhi in 1976; his dues were unpaid and his representations failed. The Ministry of External Affairs refused permission to sue on "political grounds", pleading its discretion under sections 86(1) and (2). The Supreme Court held that it is for the judiciary to adjudicate whether a prima facie case exists under the Code; that there was a dispute over the petitioner's claim which had not been judicially determined; that if a foreign State in India fulfils the conditions stipulated in section 86(2), it is liable to be sued in this country; and it directed the Union of India to reconsider the matter and pass a reasoned order in accordance with natural justice, keeping in view the trend and development of international law.Consent under section 86 is not unreviewable; the executive must apply section 86(2) lawfully and give reasons. Verma adds the comment of K. Narayana Rao that to "frustrate their (i.e., the Indian citizens') grievances in the name of diplomatic immunities hardly contributes to the dignity of foreign missions", and that the Government would be well advised to evolve a code to resolve just claims.
Mirza Ali Akbar Kashani v. United Arab Republic, AIR 1966 SC 230 (reversing the Calcutta High Court in United Arab Republic v. Mirza Ali Akbar Kashani, AIR 1962 Cal. 387)The Supreme Court considered the relationship between section 86(1) (before its 1976 amendment) and the rules of general international law and observed that the rule stated in the section is applicable to a foreign State as well — so that the immunity which the section confers extends to foreign States and not merely to foreign rulers.The statutory immunity of section 86 covers the foreign State itself, consistently with the customary rule.
Raja Sir Harinder Singh v. Commissioner of Income Tax, AIR 1972 SC 202The Ruler of an erstwhile Indian State was held liable to pay income tax on his personal income accruing in India. The Supreme Court observed that section 86 does not impose an absolute prohibition against a ruler of a foreign State being sued in India.Section 86 is not an absolute bar; its immunity gives effect to the absolute approach only subject to the express exceptions in section 86(2).
Institute Indo-Portuguese v. Borges, 27 I.L.R. 111 (Bom. 1960)A trust created by a foreign sovereign. Datar J.: if a trust is created by a foreign sovereign, "it does not mean that the foreign sovereign has created this trust in the exercise of his foreign powers … when a trust is created by a sovereign, the sovereign would be no better than a settler amenable to the law of the country in which the charity is created and is to be conducted".A sovereign's private-law acts (creating a charitable trust in India) do not attract immunity.
M/s Uttam Singh Duggal & Co. (P) Ltd. v. United States of America Agency for International Development (USAID), ILR (1982) II Delhi 273 (Delhi High Court, 24 May 1982)The appellants had contracted to construct staff houses and apartments for USAID, with an arbitration clause. When the dispute was not referred to arbitration, the appellants sued. The defendants pleaded sovereign immunity and argued that a building contract is not a commercial or trading activity. The Court rejected the plea: "In order to differentiate between a sovereign act and a private act, one will have to look into the nature or to the purpose of the transaction. The transaction … was purely a construction contract and it would … best be termed a private commercial act."The Indian application of the nature-and-purpose approach, and the decision closest in spirit to I Congreso del Partido and to the Empire of Iran case; the immunity of a foreign State agency was refused for a purely commercial construction contract.
Maharaja Sahib Shiv Bhagwat Singh Bahadur of Udaipur v. State of Rajasthan, AIR 1964 SC 444; Nawab Usman Ali Khan v. Sagar Mal, AIR 1965 SC 1798Immunity under section 86 is available only in "suits": it does not extend to disputes decided by an Industrial Tribunal under the Industrial Disputes Act, or to arbitration proceedings.The procedural scope of the immunity — it attaches to the jurisdiction of civil courts in suits, not to every adjudicative proceeding.
State of Kerala v. K. Cheru Babu, AIR 1978 Ker. 43The Kerala High Court analysed the shift in Indian law from absolute to restrictive immunity.Cited by Verma for the proposition that "the rule of absolute immunity in India is now confined to narrow regions, i.e. immunity of a foreign sovereign is restricted to acts jure imperii as distinguished from acts jure gestionis".

Verma's conclusion on the Indian position is worth quoting in the answer because it is both accurate and examinable: "The rule of absolute immunity in India is now confined to narrow regions … But what constitute acts jure imperii are left to the discretion of the courts in the absence of any legislation on the lines of British State Immunity Act or Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act of the United States. The courts in India take into account the nature or the purpose of the transaction." He adds two practical observations. First, the requirement of government consent is "reminiscent of the American practice before the passing of the 1976 Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act", and "the suits against foreign States can be brought only with the consent of the Central Government, which is not always forthcoming, thereby denying any relief against the foreign State even for its non-public or commercial acts, or where the foreign sovereign has waived the immunity expressly or impliedly" — though "the recent judicial pronouncements have eased the rigour of government consent". Secondly, even after consent is obtained, "the petitioner may not succeed because the diplomat may claim immunity from the court's jurisdiction and further, claim immunity from the execution of the decree of the court" — the two-stage problem of §7.3 F in its Indian form.

3. The Indian route for a foreign judgment against a State — CPC sections 13, 44A and 86(3)

Because the ICJ's 2012 judgment lays such stress on the enforcing court's own duty to ask the immunity question, the Indian provisions that govern the enforcement of foreign judgments belong at this point. A foreign judgment is conclusive between the parties as to any matter directly adjudicated upon, and may be enforced in India, but section 13 of the Code of Civil Procedure makes conclusiveness subject to six exceptions: the judgment is not conclusive where it was not pronounced by a court of competent jurisdiction; where it was not given on the merits; where it appears on the face of the proceedings to be founded on an incorrect view of international law or a refusal to recognise the law of India where that law is applicable; where the proceedings were opposed to natural justice; where it was obtained by fraud; and where it sustains a claim founded on a breach of any law in force in India. Execution itself follows the route of section 44A (for judgments of reciprocating territories) or a suit upon the judgment. Four consequences follow for a judgment against a foreign State, and they are worth stating in an answer on either 7.3 or 7.4. First, the question whether the foreign court was "competent" to give the judgment is the very question that the ICJ in 2012 required an exequatur court to ask for itself: would the respondent State have enjoyed immunity before the enforcing court? Secondly, a foreign judgment rendered against a State that was entitled to immunity is, for that reason, one pronounced by a court "not of competent jurisdiction" within section 13(a) and will not be enforced in India. Thirdly, even a conclusive and executable foreign judgment cannot be executed against the property of a foreign State without the written consent of the Central Government under section 86(3); and section 86(1)'s consent requirement governs the suit on the judgment itself. Fourthly, the whole structure reflects the two-stage analysis of §7.3 F: jurisdiction first, enforcement second, with the second stage stricter than the first and, in India, mediated by the executive.

↑ top

H. Heads of State, Ministers and Officials — the "Troika" and Its Companions

State immunity shields a State; but a State acts only through people. This sub-head therefore answers the question the examiner asks as soon as the State has been understood: who among the foreign State's human beings stands behind the immunity of the State, and for how long? The answer is given by the distinction between immunity ratione personae (by reason of the person) and immunity ratione materiae (by reason of the act).

  • Immunity ratione personae — status-based. Attaches to the person of certain high officers — the "troika" of Head of State, Head of Government and Minister for Foreign Affairs, and (under the Vienna Conventions) diplomatic agents, whom the VCDR places "in the place of the sovereign" — covers official and private acts together, but only while the person holds office (and for the diplomat, only while the mission lasts).
  • Immunity ratione materiae — act-based. Attaches to conduct performed in an official capacity on behalf of the State, and therefore continues beyond the end of the office: the person departs, the State's act remains. Its rationale is that the official act is the State's own act and is attributable to the State (ILC Articles on Responsibility of States for Internationally Wrongful Acts, 2001, Articles 4–8: the conduct of any State organ, of persons or entities exercising elements of governmental authority, and of persons acting on the instructions or under the direction or control of the State, is an act of that State — and Article 7 adds that a State organ's ultra vires conduct is still attributable to the State).
  • Immunity is procedural — it is not impunity. This is the sentence for which the Arrest Warrant case is remembered: "immunity from jurisdiction is not the same as immunity from the substantive law"; the procedural bar may be lifted, the responsibility remains. The same thought was repeated by the ICJ in 2012: the rules of State immunity "are procedural in nature" and "are distinct from … substantive rules"; and the rules on immunity "determine whether or not the courts of one State may exercise jurisdiction in respect of another State", not whether the conduct was lawful.
  • Ways of lifting it. The immunity of the person may cease, be waived by the sending State, or be displaced before an international criminal tribunal; individual criminal responsibility under general international law is not affected by the official position of the accused (Nuremberg Charter, Article 7: "The official position of defendants, whether as Heads of State or responsible officials in Government Departments, shall not be considered as freeing them from responsibility or mitigating punishment"; and Article 27 of the Rome Statute of the ICC, "Irrelevance of official capacity").

Arrest Warrant of 11 April 2000 (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Belgium), ICJ Rep. 2002, p. 3

International Court of Justice · Judgment of 14 February 2002 · Cross-reference: the case is dealt with fully in the notes on Topic 6 (Case 37); what follows is the part of it that belongs to the immunity of the State official as a species of State immunity.

The facts. Belgium's courts, acting under Belgian legislation on universal jurisdiction (as amended in 1993 and 1999), investigated grave breaches of the Geneva Conventions of 1949 and the Additional Protocols and crimes against humanity committed in the Congo. On 11 April 2000 a Belgian investigating judge issued an arrest warrant in absentia against Mr. Abdulaye Yerodia Ndombasi, then Minister for Foreign Affairs of the Congo, and the warrant was circulated internationally (a "red notice" or international request for his arrest). The Congo applied to the ICJ on 17 October 2000, complaining that Belgium had violated the immunities of an incumbent Minister for Foreign Affairs; Belgium responded that the application had become moot when Mr. Yerodia ceased to hold office, and objected to the admissibility of the claim.

The holdings. (i) In customary international law, the Head of State, the Head of Government and the Minister for Foreign Affairs, while in office, enjoy full immunity from the criminal jurisdiction of the courts of foreign States — and the Court drew no distinction between warrants issued while the person was in office and those issued before, or between acts done in an official and a private capacity during office: immunity ratione personae covers all acts. (ii) Customary international law recognises no exception to that immunity even for war crimes or crimes against humanity; unlike the position before certain international criminal tribunals, national courts of one State cannot try the incumbent holder of such office. (iii) Belgium had therefore violated international law by issuing the warrant and circulating it, and must cancel the warrant and its international circulation. (iv) The Court rejected Belgium's objection to the admissibility of the Congo's claim, and added the much-quoted statement that "the immunity from jurisdiction enjoyed by the incumbent … does not mean that they enjoy impunity in respect of any crimes they might have committed", because: they remain punishable under the criminal law of their own State; the State they represent may waive the immunity; once out of office they may be prosecuted in respect of acts committed before or after the period of office (subject to any immunity ratione materiae for official acts); and international criminal courts such as the ICTY, the ICTR and the ICC may have jurisdiction. The judgment was not unanimous — Judge Oda and others explained their positions separately, and a joint separate opinion of Judges Higgins, Kooijmans and Buergenthal disagreed with the majority's refusal to accept any exception.

Why it matters for Topic 7. The case separates the three layers of protection which the notes keep in view throughout: the State's immunity (argued by the State itself, as in cases 40 and 42); the official's immunity ratione materiae, which survives office and belongs to the category of State immunity because the act is the State's; and the agent's personal immunity under the Vienna Convention of 1961, which is thicker than the others in one respect (it covers private acts) but thinner in another (it lasts only for the mission). Verma's discussion of immunity of State officials in foreign territory is built around Congo v. Uganda (2005) and the same Arrest Warrant judgment, and his treatment of heads of State begins with the older cases — Mighell v. Sultan of Johore (1894) 1 Q.B. 149, where a sovereign was held immune even from a suit for breach of a promise of marriage, because the immunity "attaches to the status".

Table 7.14 — Who is protected, from what, and for how long
PersonType of immunityScopeDuration and exceptions
Head of State, Head of Government, Minister for Foreign Affairs (the "troika")Immunity ratione personae of a customary character; the Head of State's immunity is also reflected in the older "personal sovereign" immunity."Full immunity" from the criminal jurisdiction of foreign national courts and from civil and administrative jurisdiction save in limited situations; immunity from arrest, detention and from execution.While in office only. No exception for international crimes before national courts (Arrest Warrant, 2002). Ends with office, after which immunity ratione materiae may still cover official acts; the State may waive; international criminal courts may exercise jurisdiction.
Diplomatic agents and members of the diplomatic missionPersonal immunity under the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961 (given effect in India by the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972).Inviolability of the person (Art. 29); immunity from criminal jurisdiction (absolute) and from civil and administrative jurisdiction save the three exceptions in Art. 31(1)(a)–(c).From entry on the territory, or notification of appointment, until departure (Art. 39); after the mission, immunity continues "in respect of acts performed … in the exercise of his functions as a member of the mission" (Art. 39(2)) — i.e. functional immunity survives.
Consular officersFunctional immunity under the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963 (India: Consular Relations Act, 1968 analogue; VCCR Articles 43, 53).Liability to the jurisdiction of the receiving State only "in respect of acts performed in the exercise of consular functions" (Art. 43(1)); in criminal matters arrest and detention only for grave crimes pursuant to a judicial decision (Art. 41).The official-act immunity "shall continue without limitation of time" in respect of acts performed in the exercise of consular functions (Art. 53(4)).
Other State officials and agents (including former officials)Immunity ratione materiae only.Covers conduct performed in an official capacity on behalf of the State, because that conduct is attributable to the State itself.Survives the termination of office as to past official acts; does not cover private acts and does not bar the criminal responsibility of the individual under international law.

In India the same hierarchy is visible in the statute. Section 86(4) of the Code of Civil Procedure extends the protection of a foreign State to "any ruler of a foreign State, any ambassador or envoy of a foreign State, any High Commissioner of a Commonwealth country, and any such member of the staff of any of those persons as the Central Government may, by general or special order, specify", and provides that such persons cannot be arrested — read with section 8 of the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972, whose certificate of the Secretary in the Ministry of External Affairs is conclusive evidence of whether a person is entitled to any privilege or immunity.

I. After 2012 — Ferrini, Distomo, and Germany v. Italy No. 2

The 2012 judgment did not end the dispute; it moved it to Rome and Athens. This sub-head is important in an exam because it shows that the classification of acts "sovereign" and "non-sovereign" is a live political question, and because the DU syllabus offers case 42 as the leading modern case. The full digest of the case is given in the Case 42 block; what follows is the chronological aftermath.

  1. The Greek Distomo litigation (1995–2010). Claimants from the village of Distomo, where German SS units killed more than two hundred civilians on 10 June 1944 in reprisal for partisan activity, sued Germany in the Greek courts. The Court of First Instance of Leivadia gave judgment in their favour in 1997, and the Areios Pagos (Supreme Court), in Prefecture of Voiotia v. Federal Republic of Germany (2000), upheld it, refusing to apply State immunity to a claim arising from a crime against humanity. In 2002 the Special Supreme Court (Anotato Eidiko Dikastirio) reversed the position in Case 36/2002, holding that the acts of the German armed forces were acta jure imperii and that immunity could not be set aside, while leaving open whether an exception might be required where the claimants had no other means of redress; in Margellos v. Germany (2002) the same court applied the European Convention on State Immunity and held that the tort exception in its Article 11 could not extend to acts of armed forces in the conduct of a war. The claimants then took the Greek judgment to Italy for enforcement.
  2. Ferrini and the Italian line of authority (2004–2009). In Ferrini v. Federal Republic of Germany, No. 5044 of 11 March 2004, the Italian Court of Cassation held that immunity could not be pleaded against a claim arising from the deportation of an Italian national to forced labour in Germany in 1944, reasoning that a crime against humanity cannot be treated as a sovereign act entitled to immunity, and that where a peremptory norm of international law is violated the immunity rule must yield. In Federal Republic of Germany v. Prefecture of Voiotia (enforcement), No. 14201 of 2008, the same court declared the Greek Distomo judgment enforceable in Italy; further exequatur proceedings followed in Florence, and in 2009 a legal charge (ipoteca giudiziale) was entered on the Villa Vigoni, a property on Lake Como held by a private German-Italian entity, in order to enforce the Greek judgment: these Italian decisions gave the ICJ case three of its strands.
  3. The ICJ's judgment of 3 February 2012 (case 42). Germany's claim succeeded: the civil claims entertained by the Italian courts, the measure of constraint on the Villa Vigoni, and the declaration of enforceability of the Greek judgments all violated the immunity which Germany enjoyed. Operative paragraph 139: the Court found, by 12 votes to 3, that Italy had violated its obligation to respect Germany's jurisdictional immunity by allowing civil claims for the violations of international humanitarian law committed by the German Reich between 1943 and 1945; by 14 votes to 1, that Italy had violated Germany's immunity by taking measures of constraint against the Villa Vigoni and by declaring the Greek judgments enforceable; and that Italy "must, by enacting appropriate legislation, or by resorting to other methods of its choosing, ensure that the decisions of its courts … infringing the immunity of the Federal Republic of Germany … cease to have effect". The Court also held that no exception to immunity arose from the gravity of the acts (no jus cogens exception: the rules of immunity and the rules prohibiting the conduct "address different matters"), and it rejected Italy's "last resort" argument — that immunity is conditional on the claimant having an effective alternative means of redress — holding that no such precondition exists in customary international law, in the European Convention or in the United Nations Convention, and that any such condition "would be exceptionally difficult in practice".
  4. Italy's constitutional answer (2014). Italy's attempt to comply was met by the judgment of its Constitutional Court, No. 238 of 22 October 2014. The Court declared the provisions of the 1957 law which had given effect to the UN Charter, and the 2013 law which had been enacted to give effect to the ICJ's judgment, unconstitutional in so far as they required Italian courts to refuse jurisdiction over claims for crimes against humanity and violations of fundamental rights committed by Germany; it held that the protection of fundamental rights guaranteed by the Italian Constitution prevails over the customary rule of State immunity, and that the duty to comply with the ICJ judgment could not deprive victims of the "fundamental principle of judicial protection of fundamental rights". The consequence was that Italian courts continued to accept jurisdiction over Second World War claims.
  5. A second case before the Court (2022 — ). On 29 April 2022 Germany filed a fresh Application against Italy. The case is now numbered 183 and is entitled Questions of Jurisdictional Immunities of the State and Measures of Constraint against State-Owned Property (Germany v. Italy). Germany asserted that, notwithstanding the 2012 judgment — and referring among other things to the Constitutional Court's judgment No. 238/2014 — at least 25 new claims had been brought against it before Italian courts and at least 15 had been entertained and decided in respect of the conduct of the German Reich during the Second World War, and it complained also of measures of constraint against State-owned property, which is precisely the second stage of analysis described in §7.3 F. Germany invoked Article 36(1) of the Statute and Article 1 of the European Convention for the Peaceful Settlement of Disputes, 1957, as the basis of jurisdiction, and accompanied its Application with a request for provisional measures, which it later withdrew — the President placed the withdrawal on record by an Order of 10 May 2022. Time-limits were twice extended (Orders of 30 May 2023 and 5 December 2023), and by an Order dated 17 December 2024 the Court suspended the proceedings, following a request from Germany which Italy did not oppose, "pending the completion of certain domestic proceedings in Italy, which could, depending on their outcome, lead to the discontinuance of the case before the Court" (ICJ Press Release 2024/83 of 20 December 2024). Proceedings in the case therefore remain pending and largely frozen: the Memorial and Counter-Memorial dates stand at 12 January 2025 and 12 August 2027.
Syllabus note — the citation of case 42

The DU syllabus cites case 42 as "Jurisdictional Immunities of the State (Germany v. Italy: Greece Intervening), ICJ Rep. (2008)". The year refers to the institution of the proceedings (the Application was filed on 23 December 2008). The judgment was delivered on 3 February 2012, and is reported at I.C.J. Reports 2012, p. 99. The casebook does not print the case, so the digest in this file is taken from the Court's own Summary of the Judgment and the judgment's operative clause, with a provenance note to that effect. A third Germany–Italy case was instituted in 2022 and is suspended: see §7.3 I.5.

Three lessons should be drawn from the episode, and they are the substance of a "state immunity after 2012" answer. First, gravity does not create an exception: the ICJ rejected the argument that a peremptory norm (the prohibition of crimes against humanity) displaces a procedural rule of immunity, because the two rules operate on different planes — the first on lawfulness, the second on jurisdiction. Secondly, the last word may be national: the Italian Constitutional Court's insistence on the supremacy of fundamental rights within the Italian legal order shows that the "harmonisation" of immunity with human rights is now conducted in domestic constitutional courts as much as in The Hague. Thirdly, what is genuinely still open is narrower but important: the Court expressly declined to decide whether customary international law contains a "tort exception" to immunity for acta jure imperii generally — confining its holding to acts of armed forces and other organs of State acting with them in the course of an armed conflict (¶ 62 of the judgment) — and it left the practical question of enforcement (§7.3 F) exactly where the statutes leave it. The Italian Constitutional Court's answer of 2014, and the second German application of 2022 with its second limb on "measures of constraint against State-owned property", now operate on precisely those open edges.

J. Sovereign and Non-Sovereign Acts in Practice — Five Working Examples

Classification is learned through examples. The five below are drawn from the authorities cited in this head; use them in answers as illustrations, and in problem questions as the first step of the analysis.

Table 7.15 — Five working examples of classification
SituationClassificationResult and authorityWhy
A foreign ambassador rents private residential premises in Delhi and stops paying rentJure gestionisConsent to sue directed; the landlord's remedy lies in the civil court — Century Twenty One (P) Ltd. v. Union of India, AIR 1987 Del. 124; and for the diplomat personally, VCDR Article 31(1)(a) expressly excepts an action relating to private immovable property.A lease of private premises is an act a private person could perform; the purpose (housing the ambassador) is irrelevant.
A firm contracts to repair the heating system of the Iranian Embassy in Cologne, or to build housing for a foreign aid agencyJure gestionisNo immunity — Claim against the Empire of Iran (1963) 45 I.L.R. 57; Planmount Ltd. v. Republic of Zaire; in India, M/s Uttam Singh Duggal & Co. v. USAID, ILR (1982) II Delhi 273 ("purely a construction contract … best termed a private commercial act"); Harbhajan Singh v. Union of India, AIR 1987 SC 9 (consent must be reconsidered).The contract "does not fall within the core of the State's political authority"; the nature test governs, and even the purpose test would not help where the transaction is a construction or service contract.
A State-owned airline seizes a foreign carrier's aircraft during an invasion and afterwards flies them as part of its own fleetMixed — classification changes with the actKuwait Airways Corp. v. Iraqi Airways Co. [1995] 3 All E.R. 694 (HL): the seizure and removal of the aircraft were acts of sovereign authority (immune); but after the Iraqi resolution vested the aircraft in IAC, its retention and use of them as its own fleet were not acts of sovereign authority, so IAC was not immune (Lord Mustill and Lord Slynn dissenting).The "whole context" approach of I Congreso del Partido; the ultimate test is whether the act "is of its own character a governmental act, as opposed to an act which any private citizen can perform" — and the mere fact that the separate entity acted on the State's directions, or that its motive was to serve the State, does not suffice.
A judgment creditor seeks to attach an embassy's bank account to satisfy a commercial debtEnforcement stage, not jurisdiction stageAttachment refused — Alcom Ltd. v. Republic of Colombia [1984] A.C. 580 (HL): funds used for the day-to-day running of a diplomatic mission are not "in use or intended for use for commercial purposes" under section 13(4) of the State Immunity Act, 1978; see also UN Convention 2004, Article 21(1)(a); FSIA section 1609; CPC section 86(3).Immunity from execution is stricter than immunity from jurisdiction; a successful suit does not entitle the creditor to execute (ICJ, Germany v. Italy, 2012, on Article 19 of the 2004 Convention).
A State's armed forces commit killings or deportations in the forum State or abroad, and victims sue civillyJure imperii for immunity purposesImmunity upheld — Jurisdictional Immunities of the State (Germany v. Italy), ICJ Rep. 2012, p. 99: no jus cogens exception; UN Convention 2004, Article 12, carves out the activities of armed forces during an armed conflict; European Convention on State Immunity, 1972, Article 31. Contrast the statutory "terrorism exceptions" — FSIA section 1605A (formerly 1605(a)(7)) and the legislation held valid in Bank Markazi v. Peterson, 578 U.S. 212 (2016), which directed execution against Iranian central-bank assets.The restrictive theory bites on commercial and private-law activity; it has not been extended by general international law to the conduct of armed forces in armed conflict. Where States want a different result they legislate for it, which is itself the strongest evidence that no general exception exists.

Two of these examples deserve one further remark each. In Kuwait Airways, the House of Lords also held that service of a writ on a foreign State's diplomatic mission is not valid service on the State under section 12(1) of the State Immunity Act, 1978, because that provision requires service at the Foreign Ministry of the State: a neat procedural illustration, for Topic 7, of the reach of the inviolability of mission premises which §7.4 develops under VCDR Article 22. And in Bank Markazi, the United States Supreme Court upheld a statute which identified particular frozen assets and directed that they be used to satisfy the judgments of victims of Iranian-sponsored terrorism; the case marks the outer limit of what domestic legislation can do to the enforcement immunity, and shows why the ICJ's second Germany–Italy case, with its second limb on "measures of constraint against State-owned property", is followed closely by every State that holds assets abroad.

K. Examination Angle for 7.3

Questions actually asked, and how to plan them

(a) "What is sovereign immunity? Discuss the shift from absolute immunity to restrictive immunity with the help of cases." Skeleton: (1) definition and rationale — par in parem, non-intervention, comity, implied licence, plus the two Marshall reasons (function and non-enforceability); (2) The Schooner Exchange (1812) — the nine propositions, the licence theory, the "manifest distinction" between a prince's private property and the nation's armed force; (3) the absolute era — The Parlement Belge, Mighell, Kahan, Thai-Europe, Baccus; (4) the turn — Tate Letter 1952, Empire of Iran (1963), Philippine Admiral (1976), Trendtex (1977), I Congreso (1983), and the statutes of 1972/1976/1978; (5) the codification — UN Convention 2004, Articles 5 and 10–17 and Article 2(2); (6) India — CPC 86–87 with Century Twenty One, Harbhajan Singh, Mirza Ali Akbar Kashani and Uttam Singh Duggal; (7) the modern frontier — Germany v. Italy (2012), the 2014 Italian Constitutional Court judgment, and the suspended second case of 2022. Close with the criticism: classification by national courts applying national criteria; and the enforcement gap.

(b) "What are acts jure imperii and acts jure gestionis? How does the distinction apply in India?" Give the Empire of Iran passage verbatim; explain the nature test and the survival of purpose under Article 2(2) of the 2004 Convention; use I Congreso and Kuwait Airways to show that the breach and not merely the contract may be classified; then set out CPC section 86(1)–(4) with the four grounds of consent, and the case law table. Note the missing legislation and Verma's criticism that consent "is not always forthcoming".

(c) "Distinguish immunity from jurisdiction from immunity from execution." Two-stage analysis: Article 18/19 of the 2004 Convention, Article 21's protected categories, FSIA sections 1609–1610 (and 1610(a)'s "used for a commercial activity"), SIA section 13(4) with Alcom, CPC section 86(3) and VCDR Articles 22(3) and 31(1)(b) and (c) for the diplomatic overlay; conclude with Verma's criticism that the restrictive rule has been left "half-hearted" at the enforcement stage, and with the 2012 judgment's point that a lawful judgment does not automatically authorise measures of constraint.

(d) Short notes: the Tate Letter of 1952; the Villa Vigoni; Alcom; the Ferrini case; the "troika"; Article 21 of the 2004 Convention; section 86(2) CPC; and the "last resort" argument — rejected by the ICJ in 2012 but taken up by the Italian Constitutional Court in 2014.

Five errors that cost marks

(1) Confusing State immunity with diplomatic immunity: the first belongs to the State (and to officials ratione materiae) and is now restrictive; the second belongs to the agent personally and is, in criminal matters, absolute. (2) Saying that absolute immunity still governs: it does not, save in a few States, and the restrictive theory is reflected in one treaty and five statutes. (3) Forgetting that immunity is procedural: it does not make the act lawful, and the ICJ says so expressly. (4) Treating purpose as the test: the test is the nature of the act, with purpose admitted only as a secondary consideration under Article 2(2). (5) Ignoring the enforcement stage: most claims fail there, not at the jurisdictional stage — which is exactly why section 86(3) CPC and section 13(4) SIA matter.

↑ top

40Case 40 · The Schooner Exchange v. McFaddon (1812) 7 Cranch 116

The Schooner Exchange v. McFaddon — "The Exchange v. McFaddon", 11 U.S. (7 Cranch) 116 (1812)

Supreme Court of the United States · Opinion of Marshall C.J. (the whole Court concurring in the judgment) · Case 40 in the DU syllabus, cited as "(1812) 7 Cranch 116" · Appeal from the Circuit Court of the United States for the District of Pennsylvania

Provenance note. The DU LB-205 case material does not print this case: it appears in the syllabus list by citation only, without a page number, and the word "Schooner" does not occur anywhere else in the casebook text. The digest below is drawn from the report of the opinion (11 U.S. (7 Cranch) 116, at pages 116–147 — the "Syllabus" of the reporter and Marshall C.J.'s opinion) and from S.K. Verma's treatment of the case in Chapter 7 ("State Jurisdiction") as the source of the rationale of jurisdictional immunity. Every proposition is attributed to one or other of those sources. Nothing in this digest is presented as casebook material.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

The Schooner Exchange is the ancestor of the entire law of State immunity. It is the first great judicial statement — in the United States, and one of the first anywhere — of the propositions on which every later development rests: that a State's jurisdiction over its own territory is exclusive and absolute; that every exception to it must be traced to the State's own consent; that consent to the entry of a foreign sovereign's public ship (or, later, of a foreign sovereign himself, his envoys and his property) may be implied and carries with it an implied exemption from the local jurisdiction; and that the courts will not construe their general jurisdictional powers so as to defeat an immunity which the political sovereign has, in effect, granted. S.K. Verma cites the case for exactly that purpose: "In The Schooner Exchange v. McFaddon, Marshall C.J. of the Supreme Court of America laid down the rationale of jurisdictional immunity, by stating that the jurisdiction of the nation within its own territory is 'necessarily exclusive and absolute'." Every later case in §7.3 — The Parlement Belge, Mighell v. Sultan of Johore, The Christina, Empire of Iran, Trendtex, I Congreso del Partido — is in one way or another a comment on this judgment.

2. Facts

The facts are best set out in the order in which the record presents them, because the case is a case about evidence and procedure as much as about immunity.

  • The libellants' claim. On 24 August 1811, John McFaddon and William Greetham, of the State of Maryland, filed a libel in the District Court of the United States for the District of Pennsylvania against the schooner Exchange. They asserted that they were her sole owners on 27 October 1809, when she sailed from Baltimore bound for St. Sebastians in Spain; that while lawfully and peaceably pursuing her voyage she was, on 30 December 1810, "violently and forcibly taken by certain persons, acting under the decrees and orders of Napoleon, Emperor of the French", out of the custody of the libellants and of their captain and agent, and disposed of in violation of their rights and of the law of nations; that no sentence or decree of condemnation had been pronounced against her by any court of competent jurisdiction, so that their property in her "remained unchanged and in full force"; that she had been brought into the port of Philadelphia and was then in the jurisdiction of the court in the possession of one Dennis M. Begon, her reputed captain or master; and they prayed the usual process of the court to attach the vessel and that she might be restored to them.
  • The proceedings below. Process was issued and returned; two proclamations were made for all persons to appear and show cause why the vessel should not be restored (on 6 and 13 September 1811), and no one appeared in opposition. Then, on 20 September 1811, Mr. Dallas, the Attorney of the United States for the District of Pennsylvania, appeared — "at the instance of the executive department of the government of the United States, as it is understood" — and filed a suggestion (the technical name of the pleading).
  • The Attorney General's suggestion. The Attorney did not admit the truth of the libel. He suggested that, inasmuch as a state of peace and amity existed between the United States and Napoleon, Emperor of France, "the public vessels of his said Imperial and Royal Majesty, conforming to the law of nations and laws of the said United States, may freely enter the ports and harbors of the said United States and at pleasure depart therefrom without seizure, arrest, detention or molestation"; that a certain public vessel described and known as the Balaou, or Vessel No. 5, belonging to the Emperor and actually employed in his service, under the command of the Sieur Begon, on a voyage from Europe to the Indies, having encountered great stress of weather on the high seas, was compelled to enter the port of Philadelphia for refreshment and repairs about 22 July 1811; that having entered from necessity and not voluntarily, and having procured the requisite refreshments and repairs and conformed in all things to the law of nations and the laws of the United States, she was about to depart and resume her voyage in the Emperor's service when, on 24 August 1811, she was seized, arrested and detained under the attachment issued at the libellants' prayer.
  • The judgments below. The District Court dismissed the libel. On appeal, the Circuit Court reversed the District Court and ordered the vessel to be restored to the libellants. From that decision the case came to the Supreme Court of the United States. Because the cause involved "the sovereign right claimed by Napoleon, the reigning emperor of the French, and the political relations between the United States and France", the Court, on the suggestion of the Attorney General, ordered it to be heard in preference to other causes on the docket.

3. The questions before the Court

  1. May a court of the United States, at the suit of private claimants who allege that their vessel was taken from them contrary to the law of nations and never condemned by any competent prize court, exercise jurisdiction over that vessel when she is now a public armed ship in the service of a foreign sovereign at peace with the United States, and is present in an American port?
