  2. If the vessel is exempt from the jurisdiction, by what means may that fact be brought before the court — in particular, is the suggestion of the Attorney of the United States a proper mode of doing so, when the foreign sovereign is not himself before the court and no claimant appears?

4. The arguments

(a) For the libellants (McFaddon and Greetham). Title to the vessel had passed by capture only if a competent court had condemned her as prize; no condemnation had been pronounced; the owners' property therefore "remained unchanged and in full force"; and "every person who is entitled to property brought within the jurisdiction of our courts has a right to assert his title in those courts unless there be some law taking his case out of the general rule". It was accordingly, they argued, not merely the right but the duty of the court to inquire whether the title had been extinguished by an act the validity of which is recognised by national or municipal law.

(b) For the United States (the suggestion of the Attorney). The vessel was a public vessel of war of a foreign sovereign with whom the United States was at peace; she had entered an American port which was open to her reception, from necessity, and was demeaning herself in a friendly manner; and public vessels of a friendly sovereign so entering were, by the practice of nations and the law of nations, not subject to the ordinary jurisdiction of the local courts. The exemption rested not on any treaty (there was none applicable) but on the sovereign's implied assent to their entry, on the terms on which such ships are universally received.

(c) The contrary authority considered by the Court. Marshall C.J. dealt expressly with Bynkershoek, "a jurist of great reputation", who had maintained "that the property of a foreign sovereign is not distinguishable by any legal exemption from the property of an ordinary individual", and who had cited cases in which courts had exercised jurisdiction over causes in which a foreign sovereign was made a party defendant. He distinguished the single "applicable case" cited by Bynkershoek — the Spanish ships of war seized at Flushing for a debt due from the King of Spain — on the ground that in that case the States General had interposed and the vessels were released; and he observed that the case was "the only case furnished by the history of the world of an attempt made by an individual to assert a claim against a foreign prince by seizing the armed vessels of the nation", and that its outcome furnished "no feeble argument in support of the universality of the opinion in favor of the exemption claimed for ships of war".

5. The reasoning of Marshall C.J. — nine propositions

The opinion proceeds by way of a chain of propositions, each of which has been quoted in later cases and in the textbooks. Learn the numbered order; it is the best possible skeleton for an answer on the rationale of sovereign immunity.

  1. Territorial jurisdiction is exclusive and absolute. "The jurisdiction of a nation within its own territory is exclusive and absolute. It is susceptible of no limitation not imposed by itself. Any restriction deriving validity from an external source would imply a diminution of its sovereignty to the extent of that restriction, and an investment of that sovereignty to the same extent in that power which could impose such restriction." (This is the sentence Verma quotes at the head of his discussion of State immunity.)
  2. Every exception to that jurisdiction must be traced to the State's own consent. "All exceptions to the full and complete power of a nation within its own territories must be traced up to the consent of the nation itself. They can flow from no other legitimate source."
  3. Consent may be express or implied. Consent may be given expressly, or it may be implied from the State's own conduct and from the usages of the civilised world: a nation would justly be considered as violating its faith, "although not expressly plighted", which should suddenly and without previous notice exercise its territorial powers in a manner not consonant to the usages and received obligations of the civilized world.
  4. Territorial jurisdiction does not "contemplate" foreign sovereigns as its objects. "The full and absolute territorial jurisdiction being alike the attribute of every sovereignty and being incapable of conferring extraterritorial power, does not contemplate foreign sovereigns, nor their sovereign rights as its objects."
  5. The foreign sovereign enters under an express licence or in the confidence of immunity. "One sovereign can be supposed to enter a foreign territory only under an express license or in the confidence that the immunities belonging to his independent, sovereign station, though not expressly stipulated, are reserved by implication and will be extended to him." And, because the perfect equality and absolute independence of sovereigns create "a class of cases in which every sovereign is understood to waive the exercise of a part of that complete exclusive territorial jurisdiction which has been stated to be the attribute of every nation", a licence to enter, although it contains no stipulation exempting the entrant's person from arrest, "is universally understood to imply such stipulation".
  6. The same implication explains the foreign minister and foreign troops. "A foreign minister is considered as in the place of the sovereign he represents, and therefore not in point of law within the jurisdiction of the sovereign at whose court he resides"; and where a sovereign allows the troops of a foreign prince to pass through his dominions, "he waives his jurisdiction over the army to which the right of passage has been granted without any express declaration to that effect".
  7. Ports open to public ships of friendly powers import an implied assent, and with it exemption. "If there be no prohibition the ports of a friendly nation are considered as open to the public ships of all powers with whom it is at peace, and they are supposed to enter such ports and to remain in them under the protection of the government of the place." If there is no treaty and the sovereign permits his ports to remain open, such ships "virtually enter by his assent"; and if they enter by an assent thus necessarily implied, "their case cannot be distinguished from that of vessels entering by express assent".
  8. The distinction between the private property of a prince and the armed force of the nation. Without indicating any opinion on Bynkershoek's general proposition, the Court affirmed that "there is a manifest distinction between the private property of the person who happens to be a prince and that military force which supports the sovereign power and maintains the dignity and the independence of a nation. A prince, by acquiring private property in a foreign country, may possibly be considered as subjecting that property to the territorial jurisdiction; he may be considered as so far laying down the prince and assuming the character of a private individual, but this he cannot be presumed to do with respect to any portion of that armed force which upholds his Crown and the nation he is entrusted to govern." This is the sentence which later generations would read as the seed of the restrictive theory.
  9. The implication may be destroyed by the territorial sovereign — but only clearly, and not by the courts' construction of general statutes. "Without doubt, the sovereign of the place is capable of destroying this implication. He may claim and exercise jurisdiction either by employing force or by subjecting such vessels to the ordinary tribunals. But until such power be exerted in a manner not to be misunderstood, the sovereign cannot be considered as having imparted to the ordinary tribunals a jurisdiction, which it would be a breach of faith to exercise." Hence general statutory provisions describing the ordinary jurisdiction of the courts "ought not … to be so construed as to give them jurisdiction in a case in which the sovereign power has impliedly consented to waive its jurisdiction". The Court added that the arguments drawn from the inability of the judicial power to enforce its decisions against a sovereign, and from the consideration that such questions "are rather questions of policy than of law" and are fit for diplomatic rather than legal discussion, "are of great weight and merit serious attention".

6. The application and the decision

Applying these principles, the Court held that "the Exchange must be considered as a vessel which was the property of the libellants, whose claim is repelled by the fact that she is now a national armed vessel, commissioned by and in the service of the Emperor of France"; that the evidence of that fact was uncontroverted; and that the vessel, "being a public armed ship in the service of a foreign sovereign with whom the government of the United States is at peace, and having entered an American port open for her reception on the terms on which ships of war are generally permitted to enter the ports of a friendly power, must be considered as having come into the American territory under an implied promise that while necessarily within it and demeaning herself in a friendly manner, she should be exempt from the jurisdiction of the country". On the procedural question the Court held that the fact might properly be disclosed to the court "by the suggestion of the attorney for the United States". Marshall C.J. concluded, "I am directed to deliver it as the opinion of the Court that the sentence of the circuit court reversing the sentence of the district court in the case of the Exchange be Reversed, and that of the district court dismissing the libel be affirmed."

The reporters' syllabus — the holding in its own words

"A public vessel of war of a foreign sovereign at peace with the United States, coming into our ports and demeaning herself in a friendly manner, is exempt from the jurisdiction of the country." Four further propositions from the same syllabus are worth memorising verbatim: that the jurisdiction of a nation within its own territory "is exclusive and absolute"; that "all exceptions to the full and complete power of a nation within its own territories must be traced up to the consent of the nation itself"; that a sovereign entering a foreign territory with the knowledge and licence of its sovereign enters under an implied stipulation of immunity; and that a foreign minister "is considered as in the place of the sovereign he represents".

7. The principle evolved

  1. The principle of sovereign (State) immunity. The judgment is the origin of the doctrine in Anglo-American law: the public property and public vessels of a foreign sovereign, and by extension the foreign sovereign himself and his State, are exempt from the jurisdiction of the local courts while within the territory on terms of friendship. The immunity, in Marshall's analysis, rests on implied consent — an implication which the territorial sovereign may rebut, but only clearly.
  2. The doctrine of absolute immunity went further than the case itself. Marshall decided a case about a public armed vessel, and expressly reserved the question of the private property of a prince. In the twentieth century the case was extended to State-owned merchant ships and to State trading (Berizzi Bros. Co. v. S.S. Pesaro (1926) 271 U.S. 562; The Parlement Belge (1880) 5 P.D. 197; Compania Mercantil Argentina v. United States Shipping Board (1924) 40 T.L.R. 601) and then cut back by the restrictive theory in the very terms Marshall had hinted at: the "manifest distinction" between sovereign property and private property became the distinction between acta jure imperii and acta jure gestionis (see §7.3 C–D).
  3. The "suggestion of the executive" as the procedural vehicle. Marshall's holding that the fact of immunity may be brought before the court "by the suggestion of the attorney for the United States" was the foundation of the American practice, lasting until 1952, of the executive's suggestion of immunity; the Tate Letter of that year transferred the decision to the courts in the case of commercial activity, and the Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act, 1976, completed the transfer by making immunity a matter of law for the courts to decide (see §7.3 C).
  4. Continued citation. The judgment is cited by S.K. Verma as the source of the rationale of jurisdictional immunity and remains the standard opening authority in any treatment of State immunity, including in India, where the discussion of CPC sections 86–87 begins from par in parem non habet imperium and from the proposition that consent lies at the root of every exception.
How to use Schooner Exchange in an answer

Open any answer on State immunity with the case and with three of Marshall's sentences: territorial jurisdiction is "exclusive and absolute"; "all exceptions … must be traced up to the consent of the nation itself"; and a foreign sovereign enters a foreign territory "only under an express license or in the confidence that the immunities belonging to his independent, sovereign station … will be extended to him". Then state the qualification which the modern law has added: what began as absolute immunity has become restrictive, so that the "manifest distinction" between the private property of a prince and the armed force of the nation has hardened into the distinction between acta jure gestionis and acta jure imperii; and cite for that development Empire of Iran (1963), The Philippine Admiral (1976), Trendtex (1977), the Tate Letter (1952), FSIA 1976, SIA 1978, and the UN Convention 2004 — before turning to cases 41 and 42 and to the Indian position under CPC 86–87.

↑ top

41Case 41 · United States Diplomatic and Consular Staff in Tehran (United States of America v. Iran)

Case Concerning United States Diplomatic and Consular Staff in Tehran (United States of America v. Iran)

International Court of Justice · Application filed 29 November 1979 · Order indicating provisional measures, 15 December 1979 · Judgment of 24 May 1980 · I.C.J. Reports 1980, p. 3 · Case 41 in the DU syllabus — printed in the LB-205 case material at pp. 189–207

Source note. This is one of the two prescribed cases that the DU casebook does print, and the digest below follows the casebook text (printed pp. 189–207) closely — its paragraph numbering, its findings and its operative clause. Where a proposition is drawn from the judgment outside the casebook's excerpt, the paragraph number of the judgment is given. Nothing here is supplied from secondary accounts.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Every rule of the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations was tested in Tehran between November 1979 and January 1981, and the judgment of 24 May 1980 is the leading judicial statement on four questions which the syllabus of Topic 7 asks directly: what inviolability of the mission and of the person of the diplomatic agent actually requires of the receiving State; when does a private act become the act of a State (imputability); what is the consequence when a receiving State, instead of protecting the mission, adopts the attackers' cause; and what remedies does diplomatic law itself provide to a receiving State that believes a mission is abusing its privileges. It is also, for the student, the best practical illustration of three further rules: the compulsory jurisdiction of the Optional Protocol on Disputes; the operation of Article 53 of the Statute when a respondent does not appear; and the remedy of reparation in inter-State litigation.

2. The facts

(a) The seizure. At about 10.30 a.m. on 4 November 1979, during the course of a demonstration of approximately 3,000 persons, the United States Embassy compound in Tehran was overrun by "a strong armed group of several hundred people". The Iranian security personnel who were present are reported to have simply withdrawn; no attempt was made by the police or the Revolutionary Guards to prevent the occupation. The Embassy was occupied by the militants; its diplomatic and consular personnel and other nationals present were seized and held as hostages, and the Embassy's property and archives were appropriated. Iranian authorities who later appeared on the scene explained their role as being merely to "protect the safety of both the hostages and the students". No attempt was made to clear the premises, to rescue the hostages, or to persuade the militants to leave.

(b) The consulates. During the morning of 5 November 1979, only hours after the seizure of the Embassy, the United States Consulates at Tabriz and Shiraz were also seized. The operation of those consulates had been suspended since an attack in February 1979, so that no United States personnel were seized there; the attacks were likewise executed by militants of no official character, and succeeded "because of lack of sufficient protection".

(c) The Chargé d'affaires. The United States Chargé d'affaires and two members of the mission staff were not in the Embassy; they were seized at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Iran, where they had gone to seek assistance, and were thereafter detained within the precincts of the Foreign Ministry for the remainder of the period with which the case deals.

(d) The Iranian response. The Iranian Government did not order the release of the hostages or the evacuation of the premises. On 6 November the militants proclaimed that the Embassy, which they called "the U.S. center of plots and espionage", would remain under their occupation and that they were watching "most closely" the diplomatic staff whom they called "U.S. mercenaries and spies". On 17 November 1979 the Ayatollah Khomeini issued a decree which "set the seal of official government approval" on the situation. The decree asserted that the American Embassy was "a center of espionage and conspiracy" and that "those people who hatched plots against our Islamic movement in that place do not enjoy international diplomatic respect"; it declared that the premises and the hostages would remain as they were until the United States had handed over the former Shah for trial and returned his property to Iran; and it was qualified only by a request that "the blacks and the women" be handed over (thirteen hostages, including women and African-American staff, were released on 20 November 1979). Over the following months the militants remained in control, under official protection; the Court's Order of 15 December 1979 indicating provisional measures was publicly rejected by the Minister for Foreign Affairs on the following day and "has been ignored by all Iranian authorities"; and on 23 February and 7 April 1980 the Ayatollah Khomeini laid it down that the hostages would remain as they were.

(e) The threat of judicial proceedings. Statements were made by Iranian authorities, including undertakings that the hostages would be put on trial as spies and that the staff would be required to give evidence, which the Court treated as raising distinct breaches of the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations (see paragraph 79 of the judgment, on Article 31(1) and (2)).

(f) Iran's attitude to the Court. Iran did not appear in the proceedings and was not represented at the hearings. Its position was placed before the Court in a letter of 9 December 1979 and in public statements: that the hostage-taking was a "reaction" by a revolutionary people to long years of American intervention in Iran's internal affairs, that the Court should not hear the case because the matter was not a legal question capable of objective determination, and that the taking of the hostages was in any event the consequence of the Shah's crimes and the United States' own wrongful conduct.

3. The questions before the Court

The Court framed its task in two steps, in terms which organise every answer on this head: "First, it must determine how far, legally, the acts in question may be regarded as imputable to the Iranian State. Secondly, it must consider their compatibility or incompatibility with the obligations of Iran under treaties in force or under any other rules of international law that may be applicable" (paragraph 56). The events, it said, "fall into two phases which it will be convenient to examine separately."

4. Jurisdiction and the basis of the Court's authority

  • The instruments in force between the parties included: the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961; the Optional Protocol to that Convention concerning the Compulsory Settlement of Disputes; the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963; the Optional Protocol to that Convention; and the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of Crimes against Internationally Protected Persons, including Diplomatic Agents, 1973; as also the Treaty of Amity, Economic Relations and Consular Rights between the United States and Iran of 1955, which entered into force on 16 June 1957.
  • Jurisdiction. As the Court had indicated in its Order of 15 December 1979, the Optional Protocols to the two Vienna Conventions "manifestly provide a possible basis for the Court's jurisdiction" in respect of the United States' claims under those Conventions, and the only remaining question was whether the dispute fell within the scope of their provisions: the Court held that it did, since the dispute concerned the interpretation or application of the two Conventions, and thus fell within Article I of the Protocols (paragraphs 45–49). The United States also advanced claims under Articles II(4), XIII, XVIII and XIX of the 1955 Treaty; the Court held that the Treaty, whose very purpose was "to promote friendly relations" and to ensure "the protection and security of their nationals", continued to operate precisely when difficulties arose, and that Article XXI(2) of the Treaty provided a further basis of jurisdiction (paragraphs 50–54).
  • Iran's non-appearance. The Court proceeded under Article 53 of its Statute, and satisfied itself that it had jurisdiction and that the claims were well founded in fact and in law — a point worth noting because non-appearance does not relieve the applicant of proof, nor the Court of the duty of scrutiny.

5. The first phase — the occupation and Iran's inaction (paragraphs 57–68)

Imputability. The Court held that the initiation of the attack on 4 November 1979 and of the attacks on the consulates on the following day could not be attributed to the Iranian State: they "appear to have been executed by militants not having an official character", and congratulations after the event — such as those reportedly telephoned by the Ayatollah Khomeini on the actual evening of the attack — and later statements of official approval did not "alter the initially independent and unofficial character of the militants' attack".

But the inaction was imputable, and unlawful. The Court held that the Iranian authorities' conduct after the attack — their complete failure to take the steps required of the receiving State — engaged Iran's responsibility for its own breaches of its obligations. On 4 November the authorities (a) were "fully aware of their obligations under the conventions in force to take appropriate steps to protect the premises of the United States Embassy and its diplomatic and consular staff from any attack and from any infringement of their inviolability, and to ensure the security of such other persons as might be present on the said premises"; (b) were fully aware of the need for action; (c) had the means at their disposal to perform their obligations; and (d) "completely failed to comply with these obligations". The breaches so found were of VCDR Articles 22(2), 24, 25, 26, 27 and 29 and VCCR Articles 5 and 36, together with the obligation in Article II(4) of the 1955 Treaty of Amity to ensure "the most constant protection and security" to each other's nationals (paragraphs 61–68). The Court was emphatic on the gravity of the failure: the Iranian authorities' inaction was a "clear and serious" violation of their obligations, not a mere omission of courtesy.

6. The second phase — endorsement, and the conversion of private acts into State acts (paragraphs 69–79)

The legal test. In the second phase, the Court's question was whether the continued occupation and detention — initially the work of private militants — had become the conduct of the Iranian State. The Court applied the ordinary law of State responsibility: conduct may become attributable to a State where the State itself adopts it as its own.

The finding of endorsement. "Above all, the Ayatollah Khomeini himself made crystal clear the endorsement by the State both of the take-over of the Embassy and Consulates and of the detention of the Embassy staff as hostages"; the decree of 17 November 1979 set "the seal of official government approval" on the situation (paragraphs 71–74). The Court concluded that the approval given by the State, and its decision to continue the situation so created, converted the occupation and detention into acts of the Iranian State itself, so that thereafter Iran was responsible for them as its own conduct. The Court also relied on the fact that the situation was maintained for six months without material change, and that the Order of 15 December 1979 was ignored (paragraph 75).

The continuing breaches. The Court found breaches extending to VCDR Articles 22(1) and 22(3) — the prohibition on entering the premises without consent and on their search, requisition, attachment or execution — as well as Articles 24–27 and 29 and the corresponding consular provisions; the detention of the Chargé d'affaires and two members of the staff in the precincts of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs was a breach of Articles 26 and 29 (paragraph 78); and the threatened judicial proceedings against the staff, and their threatened use as witnesses, was a breach of Article 31(1) and (2) — the immunity of the agent from criminal and civil (including evidentiary) process (paragraph 79).

7. Iran's defences — and why they failed (paragraphs 80–87)

The argument that the United States' own conduct justified the seizure. Iran argued, in effect, that the Embassy had been a centre of espionage and conspiracy and that the seizure was a reaction to the United States' wrongful interference and to the former Shah's crimes. The Court's answer is the foundation of the modern rule that diplomatic immunity is not a bargain to be enforced by self-help: the receiving State's protection of the mission is not conditional on its opinion of the mission's conduct; where a receiving State believes that a mission or its members are abusing their privileges, the remedy lies in the means which diplomatic law itself provides — the power to require the recall of the offending member, to declare him persona non grata (Article 9), and, "if abuses of their functions by members of a mission reach serious proportions", the "more radical remedy" of breaking off diplomatic relations and calling for the immediate closure of the mission. Above all, "the rules of diplomatic law … constitute a self-contained régime which, on the one hand, lays down the receiving State's obligations regarding the facilities, privileges and immunities to be accorded to diplomatic missions and, on the other, foresees their possible abuse by members of the mission and specifies the means at the disposal of the receiving State to counter any such abuse. These means are, by their nature, entirely efficacious" (paragraphs 83–86). The rights of protection under the Vienna Conventions "cannot be made dependent upon the performance of particular obligations by the sending State" — the protection is owed as an obligation of international law and not as a reciprocal favour.

The argument that the Court should decline jurisdiction. Iran's contention that the matter was beyond law was answered by the Court's jurisdiction under the Optional Protocols; and its refusal to appear did not prevent the Court from deciding (Article 53 of the Statute).

8. The United States' rescue operation (paragraphs 93–94)

The Court took note of the United States' attempt, on 24–25 April 1980, to rescue the hostages by a military operation in Iranian territory, and of Iran's reliance upon it. The Court observed that the operation had been launched after the situation complained of had been created by the Iranian Government "over six months earlier", and held that "the findings reached by the Court in this Judgment are not affected by that operation" (paragraph 94). The passage is used for two purposes: it shows that later conduct cannot retroactively justify an existing breach; and it preserves the question of the lawfulness of the rescue operation for another day.

9. The operative clause (paragraph 95) — reproduced from the casebook text

  1. By thirteen votes to two, decides that Iran, "by the conduct which the Court has set out in this Judgment, has violated in several respects, and is still violating, obligations owed by it to the United States of America under international conventions in force between the two countries, as well as under long-established rules of general international law".
  2. By thirteen votes to two, decides that the violations "engage the responsibility of the Islamic Republic of Iran towards the United States of America under international law".
  3. Unanimously, decides that Iran must immediately take all steps to redress the situation resulting from the events of 4 November 1979 and what followed, and to that end: (a) must immediately terminate the unlawful detention of the United States Chargé d'affaires and other diplomatic and consular staff and other United States nationals held hostage, and must immediately release each and every one and entrust them to the protecting Power (Article 45 of the 1961 Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations); (b) must ensure that all the said persons have the necessary means of leaving Iranian territory, including means of transport; (c) must immediately place in the hands of the protecting Power the premises, property, archives and documents of the United States Embassy in Tehran and of its Consulates in Iran.
  4. Unanimously, decides that no member of the United States diplomatic or consular staff may be kept in Iran to be subjected to any form of judicial proceedings or to participate in them as a witness.
  5. By twelve votes to three, decides that Iran is under an obligation to make reparation to the United States for the injury caused by the events of 4 November 1979 and what followed.
  6. By fourteen votes to one, decides that the form and amount of such reparation, failing agreement between the parties, shall be settled by the Court, and reserves for that purpose the subsequent procedure in the case.

(Signed: Humphrey Waldock, President.) The hostages were in the event released on 20 January 1981 under the Algiers Accords concluded between the two States; no second judgment on the form and amount of reparation was ever delivered, and the reparation question was dealt with through the machinery which those Accords set up.

10. The principle evolved — ten propositions

  1. Inviolability is an obligation of the receiving State, not a privilege of the mission only. Article 22(2) obliges the receiving State to take all appropriate steps to protect the premises "against any intrusion or damage and to prevent any disturbance of the peace of the mission or impairment of its dignity"; Article 29 obliges it to treat the person of the agent with due respect and "take all appropriate steps to prevent any attack on his person, freedom or dignity". Tehran is the measure of what "all appropriate steps" means: awareness, capacity and failure to act are enough to constitute a breach.
  2. Failure to protect is a breach in itself, distinct from the attack. The State that does nothing is responsible even if it did not organise the attack.
  3. Endorsement converts private conduct into State conduct. Where a State adopts and maintains the seizure of a mission as its own policy, the conduct becomes attributable to it — the principle later codified in Article 11 of the ILC Articles on State Responsibility, 2001 (conduct acknowledged and adopted by a State as its own).
  4. The threat of judicial proceedings is itself a breach. Articles 31(1) and (2) are not merely procedural shields; to require an agent to stand trial or to testify is unlawful, and the Court's fourth operative point is framed in absolute terms.
  5. Diplomatic law is a self-contained régime. Privileges are not conditional on reciprocity in the individual case; the remedy for abuse lies in Articles 9 and 45 — persona non grata, recall, closure and severance of relations — not in self-help.
  6. Interests and premises are protected even after relations break down. Operative point 3(c) applies Article 45: the premises, property, archives and documents were to be placed in the hands of the protecting Power.
  7. The Optional Protocol is the gateway to the Court. Article I of the Protocol concerning the Compulsory Settlement of Disputes gives the ICJ jurisdiction over disputes arising out of the interpretation or application of the Convention; Tehran is its leading application, and it is the same gateway that was used in Jadhav (case 43).
  8. Non-appearance does not simplify the applicant's case. Article 53 of the Statute obliges the Court to satisfy itself of jurisdiction and of the merits; the judgment is a model of careful proof.
  9. Reparation follows the breach. The judgment separates cessation (release, restoration of premises) from reparation in the narrow sense, and reserves the form and amount — the ordinary structure of the law of State responsibility.
  10. Political facts do not alter legal obligations. The Court recorded that the problem between the two States was "not a matter of a few individuals" but of a situation endorsed at the highest level, and yet insisted that the obligations of the Convention remained exactly the same.
Using case 41 in an answer — the three-line summary plus the fourth line

Three lines. (1) The 4 November 1979 attack by militants was not initially attributable to Iran, but Iran's complete failure to protect the mission and its staff violated VCDR Articles 22(2), 24–27 and 29 and VCCR Articles 5 and 36 (and Article II(4) of the 1955 Treaty of Amity). (2) From the Ayatollah Khomeini's decree of 17 November 1979, the State endorsed the occupation and detention, so that the militants' conduct became the conduct of Iran, with continuing breaches including Articles 22(1) and (3) and 31(1) and (2). (3) Iran's defences — espionage, United States misconduct, lack of a legal question — were rejected because diplomatic law is a self-contained régime with its own remedies (Article 9; severance of relations), and the Court ordered release, restitution of the premises to the protecting Power under Article 45, the discontinuance of judicial proceedings, and reparation. Fourth line — the one that distinguishes a first-class answer: the Court's holding on endorsement is the ancestor of Article 11 of the ILC Articles on State Responsibility 2001, and its "self-contained régime" holding is why the 2025 paper's hostage hypothetical cannot be met by a receiving State's complaint that the sending State behaved badly first.

42Case 42 · Jurisdictional Immunities of the State (Germany v. Italy: Greece Intervening)

Jurisdictional Immunities of the State (Germany v. Italy: Greece Intervening)

International Court of Justice · Application filed 23 December 2008 · Judgment of 3 February 2012 · I.C.J. Reports 2012, p. 99 · Case 42 in the DU syllabus, there cited as "ICJ Rep. (2008)" — the year of the Application, not of the judgment

Provenance note. The DU LB-205 case material does not print this case. It appears in the syllabus list by name and year only, and neither the casebook's Tehran excerpt nor any other part of the printed material refers to it ("Distomo", "Villa Vigoni", "Ferrini" and "jure imperii" return no hits in the extracted casebook). The digest below is therefore constructed from the Court's own materials: the Summary of the Judgment of 3 February 2012 (ICJ, Summary 2012/2 — the Court's Registry describes such summaries as "not an official document"), the judgment's operative clause, and the ICJ press releases relating to the case and to the second Germany–Italy case instituted in 2022. S.K. Verma's Chapter 7 supplies the framework of analysis (absolute and restrictive immunity, the jure imperii/jure gestionis distinction, the enforcement gap and the UN Convention of 2004). The verdict, however, is the Court's. Nothing here is presented as casebook material.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Case 42 is the modern restatement of the law of case 40. The Schooner Exchange (1812) asserted the immunity in its absolute form and rested it on implied consent; Germany v. Italy (2012) takes the restrictive theory for granted, decides what happens when the most serious violations of international humanitarian law are met with a claim of immunity, and then draws a sharp line between immunity from jurisdiction and immunity from enforcement. It is the case in which the International Court of Justice settled, for the first time, four questions that the statutes and the 2004 Convention had left to scholarship and to national courts: whether a peremptory norm (jus cogens) can displace the rule of immunity; whether the gravity of a violation affects it; whether the availability of an alternative remedy is a condition of immunity; and whether a State's property may be attached to satisfy a judgment once given. The result is a textbook illustration of Verma's observation that the law of State immunity is "essentially procedural" and that its classification of acts remains, in the last analysis, the work of national courts.

2. The historical background — the events of 1943–1945

The proceedings in the Italian courts had their origin in acts perpetrated by the German armed forces and other organs of the German Reich in Italy between 1943 and 1945, after the Italian armistice of 8 September 1943 and during the German occupation. The Court grouped the claims into three categories:

  1. The killing of civilians in occupied territory as part of a policy of reprisals. The example given by the Court is the massacre of 29 June 1944 at Civitella in Val di Chiana, Cornia and San Pancrazio, carried out by members of the "Hermann Göring" division of the German armed forces, in which 203 civilians were killed as hostages after resistance fighters had killed four German soldiers a few days earlier.
  2. The deportation of civilians to slave labour in Germany. Mr. Luigi Ferrini, a civilian resident of Italy, was deported from Italy to what was "in substance slave labour" in Germany; his case gave its name to the line of Italian judgments which the ICJ was asked to review.
  3. Italian military internees. Members of the Italian armed forces were denied the status of prisoner of war — and with it the protections of the Geneva Convention relative to the Treatment of Prisoners of War — to which they were entitled, and were used as forced labourers in German industry.

The Court recorded that there could be "no doubt that this conduct was a serious violation of the international law of armed conflict applicable in 1943–1945", but it emphasized that it was not called upon to decide whether those acts were illegal — a point not contested — but only whether, in proceedings claiming compensation for them, the Italian courts were obliged to accord Germany immunity. That distinction between the legality of the conduct and the jurisdictional consequence is the pivot of the whole case.

3. The proceedings in the Italian and Greek courts

  • The Ferrini line in Italy (2004–2011). Beginning with Ferrini v. Federal Republic of Germany, No. 5044 of 11 March 2004, the Italian Court of Cassation held that Italian courts had jurisdiction over claims for compensation for crimes against humanity committed by the German Reich, notwithstanding Germany's plea of immunity. Further judgments of the Italian courts followed between 2004 and 2011, and it was precisely these decisions that Germany impugned before the ICJ.
  • The Greek Distomo line and its exequatur in Italy. In the litigation arising from the Distomo massacre of 10 June 1944, the Court of First Instance of Livadia (Greece) had given judgment against Germany in favour of the claimants; the Areios Pagos upheld the refusal of immunity in 2000, while the Greek Special Supreme Court reversed the approach in 2002. The claimants then sought to enforce the Livadia judgment in Italy: by a decision of 13 June 2006 the Florence Court of Appeal declared it enforceable in Italy, and on 7 June 2007 the Greek claimants entered a legal charge (ipoteca giudiziale) in the Land Registry of the Province of Como against Villa Vigoni, a property of the German State near Lake Como, to enforce the judgment. The charge was later suspended pending the outcome of the ICJ proceedings.
  • The procedural framework before the Court. Germany invoked Article 1 of the European Convention for the Peaceful Settlement of Disputes, 1957, as the basis of jurisdiction. Italy's counter-claim — raising "the question of the reparation owed to Italian victims of grave violations of international humanitarian law committed by forces of the German Reich" — was held inadmissible by an Order of 6 July 2010 (it did not fall within the Court's jurisdiction, and was inadmissible under Article 80(1) of the Rules of Court). Greece was authorized to intervene as a non-party, in so far as its intervention was limited to the decisions of the Greek courts which had been declared enforceable in Italy, by an Order of 4 July 2011.

4. The subject-matter of the dispute, and the jurisdiction of the Court

Germany's requests. Germany asked the Court to declare that Italy had failed to respect Germany's jurisdictional immunity (i) by allowing civil claims to be brought against it in the Italian courts seeking reparation for injuries caused by violations of international humanitarian law committed by the German Reich during the Second World War; (ii) by taking measures of constraint against Villa Vigoni, German State property on Italian territory; and (iii) by declaring enforceable in Italy decisions of Greek civil courts rendered against Germany on the basis of similar acts; and, further, to order Italy to ensure that all decisions of its courts infringing Germany's sovereign immunity became unenforceable.

Italy's response. Italy asked the Court to adjudge Germany's claims unfounded, adding that it would have no objection to an order that the measures of constraint against Villa Vigoni be brought to an end.

The jurisdictional question. Under Article 1 of the 1957 European Convention the parties submitted to the Court "all international legal disputes" arising between them. Article 27(a) of the Convention excludes disputes "relating to facts or situations prior to the entry into force of this Convention as between the parties to the dispute"; the Convention entered into force between Germany and Italy on 18 April 1961. Germany's claims, however, related to disputes whose subject-matter consisted of the Italian judicial decisions and measures of constraint taken between 2004 and 2011 — long after 1961 — and not to the events of 1943–1945. The temporal limitation was therefore inapplicable, and the Court had jurisdiction. The Court added that it was also required to determine a broader question of law: whether a State's failure to perform completely an alleged duty of reparation is capable of affecting the existence and scope of that State's jurisdictional immunity before foreign courts — the "functional" question which Italy's defences raised.

5. The arguments of the parties

(a) Germany's argument. Immunity is governed by international law, not by comity; both parties agreed on that. States are entitled to immunity in respect of acta jure imperii, and there is no relevant limitation on that immunity where the acts are those of the armed forces of a State and other organs of State acting in co-operation with them in the course of an armed conflict. Neither the European Convention of 1972 nor the United Nations Convention of 2004 supports the Italian position, and the practice of States does not contain an exception. The law to be applied is the law in force when the impugned Italian acts occurred — that is, the contemporary law of immunity — because the unlawfulness of the underlying conduct is not in issue.

(b) Italy's argument — two branches and, within the second, three strands. First, Italy maintained that immunity in respect of acta jure imperii does not extend to torts or delicts occasioning death, personal injury or damage to property committed on the territory of the forum State — the "territorial tort" argument, for which Italy relied on Article 11 of the European Convention and Article 12 of the United Nations Convention. Secondly, Italy maintained that, irrespective of where the acts took place, Germany was not entitled to immunity for three cumulative reasons, which its counsel expressly asked the Court to view together: (i) the gravity of the violations; (ii) the peremptory character (jus cogens) of the rules violated, which must prevail over a mere customary rule of immunity; and (iii) the absence of any effective alternative means of redress for the victims — the "last resort" or "no alternative remedy" argument.

(c) Greece's observations. Greece intervened as a non-party, confined to the decisions of its courts declared enforceable in Italy. Its observations put before the Court the Greek case law (including the 2000 judgment of the Areios Pagos in the Distomo case and the later reversal by the Special Supreme Court in 2002) and the practical situation of the Distomo claimants, who had obtained a judgment but no payment.

6. The decision — seven holdings

  1. The applicable law is the immunity law of the time of the Italian proceedings, and immunity is procedural. Applying the principle reflected in Article 13 of the ILC Articles on State Responsibility — that the compatibility of an act with international law can be determined only by the law in force when the act occurred — the Court held that the "relevant acts" were the denial of immunity and the exercise of jurisdiction by the Italian courts, which took place only when those proceedings were conducted; the law to be examined was therefore the contemporary law of State immunity, not that of 1943–1945. The Court added that "the law of immunity is essentially procedural in nature": it regulates the exercise of jurisdiction in respect of particular conduct and is "entirely distinct" from the substantive law which determines whether that conduct is lawful or unlawful.
  2. The "territorial tort" argument fails. The Court first considered Article 11 of the European Convention (the territorial tort principle) and found that it had to be read with Article 31 of the same Convention, which provides that nothing in the Convention is to affect immunities or privileges enjoyed by a Contracting State "in respect of anything done or omitted to be done by, or in relation to, its armed forces when on the territory of another Contracting State". Article 31 operated as a "saving clause": the immunity of a State for the acts of its armed forces therefore fell entirely outside the Convention and had to be determined by customary international law. Turning to custom, the Court examined State practice (national legislation and judicial decisions) and found that the territorial tort exception, so far as it exists, is confined to acts of a private-law character — the traffic accident is the classic example — and has not been extended by States to acts of armed forces in the conduct of an armed conflict. Article 12 of the United Nations Convention points the same way, since it contains an express carve-out for the activities of armed forces during an armed conflict. The Court noted expressly that it was not called upon to decide whether a general tort exception exists for acta jure imperii — the issue was confined to acts of armed forces and organs of State acting in co-operation with them in the course of an armed conflict — and it held that customary international law continues to require immunity for such acts. (Italy's reliance on Article 11 was further weakened by the fact that the European Convention, though in force between the parties, was displaced by its own Article 31 on these facts, and by the observation that the "presence" requirement in Article 11 could not be satisfied in respect of the relevant conduct in any event.)
  3. There is no jus cogens exception. Assuming for the sake of argument that the rules of the law of armed conflict prohibiting the murder of civilians in occupied territory, the deportation of civilian inhabitants to slave labour, and the deportation of prisoners of war to slave labour are rules of jus cogens, the Court found no conflict between those rules and the customary rule requiring immunity: "the two sets of rules address different matters." The rules of State immunity are procedural; they determine only whether the courts of one State may exercise jurisdiction in respect of another; they do not bear upon whether the conduct was lawful or unlawful. The Court added two empirical observations: the argument had been rejected "by the national courts" generally — the Italian judgments were "the only decisions of national courts to have accepted the reasoning on which Italy's argument is based" — and no national legislation on State immunity limits immunity where jus cogens violations are alleged.
  4. There is no "last resort" or "no alternative remedy" condition. The Court could "find no basis in the State practice from which customary international law is derived that international law makes the entitlement of a State to immunity dependent upon the existence of effective alternative means of securing redress". Neither the national legislation nor the case law of national courts facing immunity objections contained any such precondition; neither convention included it; and any such condition "would be exceptionally difficult in practice", particularly where the claims had been the subject of extensive intergovernmental discussion. Italy's third strand was rejected.
  5. The "combined effect" of gravity, jus cogens and the absence of redress does not change the result. The Court held that, since none of the three strands sufficed on its own, they could not suffice cumulatively; and it added, in a passage worth memorising, that to ask a national court to balance the gravity of the violation against the interests protected by immunity "would disregard the very nature of State immunity".
  6. The measures of constraint against Villa Vigoni violated immunity from enforcement. Immunity from enforcement "goes further" than immunity from jurisdiction: even a judgment lawfully rendered against a foreign State does not ipso facto permit measures of constraint against its property in the forum State or in a third State, and a waiver of jurisdictional immunity does not implicitly waive immunity from enforcement. Relying on Article 19 of the United Nations Convention — "inasmuch as it reflects customary law on the matter" — the Court held that at least one of three conditions must be satisfied before any measure of constraint: the property must be in use for an activity that does not pursue governmental non-commercial purposes; or the State must have expressly consented; or it must have allocated the property for the satisfaction of the claim. Villa Vigoni was the seat of a cultural centre promoting German–Italian cultural exchanges — governmental, entirely non-commercial — and Germany had neither consented nor allocated it. The registration of the legal charge was therefore a violation of Germany's immunity. The Court also held that it could decide this issue without first determining whether the Greek judgments themselves were in breach of Germany's jurisdictional immunity, since the two immunities are distinct.
  7. The exequatur decisions violated jurisdictional immunity. A court seised of an application to enforce a foreign judgment rendered against a third State is itself exercising jurisdiction in respect of that third State. Although exequatur proceedings do not decide the merits, the court granting exequatur "exercises a jurisdictional power which results in the foreign judgment being given effects corresponding to those of a judgment rendered on the merits in the requested State"; the proceedings must therefore be regarded as conducted against the third State. The test is: would the court have been obliged to accord immunity to the respondent State had it been seised of the merits of an identical dispute? Applying that test, the Italian courts which declared enforceable in Italy the decisions of the Greek courts against Germany violated Germany's immunity — and it was unnecessary (and, the Court said, not open to it) to decide whether the Greek courts themselves had breached Germany's immunity.

7. The operative clause and the remedies

Germany's first three requests were upheld, and in its operative clause (paragraph 139) the Court found that Italy had violated its obligation to respect the jurisdictional immunity which Germany enjoys under international law: (i) by allowing civil claims based on violations of international humanitarian law by the German Reich between 1943 and 1945 to be brought against Germany, by 12 votes to 3; (ii) by taking measures of constraint against Villa Vigoni, by 14 votes to 1; and (iii) by declaring enforceable in Italy the Greek judgments, by 14 votes to 1. The Court did not consider it necessary to include an express declaration that Italy's international responsibility was engaged (Germany's fourth submission). On the remedy, the Court recalled the law of cessation and restitution: the decisions and measures infringing Germany's immunities which were still in force must cease to have effect, and the effects already produced by them must be reversed, so that the situation existing before the wrongful acts is re-established — it not having been shown that restitution would be materially impossible or disproportionately burdensome. Italy was free to choose the means: "by enacting appropriate legislation, or by resorting to other methods of its choosing having the same effect" (fourth operative point, by 14 votes to 1).

8. Separate and dissenting opinions

The judgment was not unanimous, and the dissents are part of the case's importance. Judge Yusuf and Judge ad hoc Gaja (appointed by Italy, which had no judge of its nationality on the bench) appended dissenting opinions. Judge Gaja's dissent is the one most often cited by critics of the majority: he considered that the legislative and judicial practice of States — in particular the territorial-tort exception written into most national statutes on State immunity — pointed towards a narrower immunity for acts committed on the territory of the forum State than the Court was prepared to accept, and that the judgment treated a contested area of practice as though it were settled. Other judges appended separate opinions explaining their understanding of particular points (including the consequences of the judgment for the position of the victims, and the "openness" of the question of alternative remedies at the inter-State level). In an examination answer the dissents should be used in one sentence only: to show that the boundary between jure imperii and jure gestionis, and the reach of the tort exception, remain contested.

9. The principle evolved

  1. State immunity is a rule of customary international law, not a matter of comity. The ICJ endorsed the ILC's 1980 conclusion that the rule of State immunity is "adopted as a general rule of customary international law solidly rooted in the current practice of States", confirmed by national legislation, judicial decisions and State comments on the 2004 Convention.
  2. Immunity is procedural and distinct from the substantive law of responsibility. It does not make unlawful conduct lawful; it allocates jurisdiction. Hence the law of 2012 could be applied to the events of 1943–1945 without retroactivity.
  3. Gravity and peremptory character do not displace immunity. The prohibition of crimes against humanity is not a jurisdictional key; the two sets of rules "address different matters".
  4. No alternative-remedy precondition. A State's entitlement to immunity is not conditional on the claimant having another forum — a holding which expressly rejected the reasoning that had been persuasive in some national courts.
  5. Enforcement is stricter than jurisdiction. Article 19 of the 2004 Convention reflects custom; only three conditions permit measures of constraint; and an embassy, its cultural centre or its bank account used for governmental purposes is out of reach (compare Alcom Ltd. v. Republic of Colombia [1984] A.C. 580).
  6. A court granting exequatur exercises jurisdiction. This closes the last practical loophole in the restrictive theory: a claimant who has won abroad cannot simply enforce elsewhere without the enforcing court asking the immunity question for itself.
  7. Compliance may require legislation. The remedy — "cease to have effect", by "appropriate legislation or other methods" — makes State immunity an obligation of result addressed to the political branches, not merely a plea for the courtroom.

10. The aftermath — and why the case is still open

Italy attempted to comply; its Constitutional Court resisted. By judgment No. 238 of 22 October 2014, the Italian Constitutional Court declared unconstitutional the domestic provisions which required Italian courts to deny jurisdiction in such claims, holding that the protection of fundamental rights under the Italian Constitution prevailed, and that the duty to comply with the ICJ's judgment could not deprive victims of judicial protection — while acknowledging that duty in principle. Since then, further claims have been brought against Germany in Italy, and on 29 April 2022 Germany filed a second Application, now pending as case No. 183, Questions of Jurisdictional Immunities of the State and Measures of Constraint against State-Owned Property (Germany v. Italy). Germany's Application complains that, since 2012, at least 25 new claims had been brought before Italian courts and at least 15 had been entertained and decided in respect of the conduct of the German Reich during the Second World War; the case's second limb concerns measures of constraint against State-owned property, the very problem of §7.3 F. Germany withdrew its request for provisional measures (placed on record by an Order of 10 May 2022); time-limits were extended twice; and by an Order of 17 December 2024 the Court suspended the proceedings, at Germany's request and without opposition from Italy, "pending the completion of certain domestic proceedings in Italy, which could, depending on their outcome, lead to the discontinuance of the case before the Court" (Press Release 2024/83 of 20 December 2024). The judgments of the Italian Constitutional Court and the second case are considered in §7.3 I above.

Using case 42 in an answer — five sentences worth marks

(1) "The ICJ held that the rules of State immunity are procedural and that the rules prohibiting the conduct — even those of a peremptory character — address different matters, so that no jus cogens exception exists." (2) "The Court confined the 'territorial tort' exception to acts of a private-law character and refused to extend it to acts of armed forces in the course of an armed conflict, Article 31 of the European Convention and Article 12 of the United Nations Convention confirming that carve-out." (3) "It rejected the argument that immunity is conditional upon the claimant's having an effective alternative means of redress." (4) "It held, on the basis of Article 19 of the 2004 Convention, that immunity from enforcement goes further than immunity from jurisdiction, so that even a lawful judgment does not permit execution against property used for governmental non-commercial purposes — the Villa Vigoni charge was therefore unlawful." (5) "It held that a court seised of an application for exequatur of a foreign judgment against a third State must itself ask whether the third State would have enjoyed immunity before it; and it ordered that the infringing decisions cease to have effect, by legislation or otherwise." Add, as the latest development: the Italian Constitutional Court's judgment No. 238/2014, and the second German Application of 2022 (case No. 183), suspended by the Court's Order of 17 December 2024.
