43Case 43 · Jadhav Case (India v. Pakistan)

Jadhav Case (India v. Pakistan) — Kulbhushan Sudhir Jadhav

International Court of Justice · Application filed 8 May 2017 · Order indicating provisional measures, 18 May 2017 · Judgment of 17 July 2019 · I.C.J. Reports 2019, p. 418 · Case 43 in the DU syllabus — printed in the LB-205 case material at pp. 208–219

Source note. This is one of the two prescribed cases that the DU casebook prints. The digest follows the casebook text (printed pp. 208–219) — facts, jurisdiction, the 2008 Agreement, the three alleged breaches and the remedy of review and reconsideration. The casebook includes the Court's reasoning but does not reproduce the operative clause; the operative clause at paragraph 149 of the judgment, together with the voting figures and the separate opinions, is therefore taken from the Court's official Summary of the Judgment of 17 July 2019 (the official Summary of the Judgment of 17 July 2019) and the judgment itself, and is identified as such.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Jadhav is the Indian consular case. It is prescribed because it includes within a single judgment: the whole of Article 36 of the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963 — notification, access and legal representation — read as creating individual rights as well as inter-State obligations; the compulsory jurisdiction of the ICJ through the Optional Protocol on Disputes; the treatment of a bilateral agreement (the India–Pakistan Agreement on Consular Access of 21 May 2008) against the yardstick of Article 73(2) of the Convention; the interaction of consular law with the fair-trial guarantees of a domestic constitutional order and with a system of military justice; and the remedial question — what a court can do, and what it cannot do, about a death sentence passed in breach of a consular treaty. For the Indian student it is also the concrete illustration of a consular officer's daily work — the protection of a national in detention abroad — and of what happens when access is refused.

2. The facts

(a) The arrest and the detention. Mr. Kulbhushan Sudhir Jadhav, an Indian national, was arrested in Pakistan on 3 March 2016. Pakistan's account, from the FIR of 8 March 2016 and a supplementary FIR of 6 September 2016, was that he had been involved in espionage and subversive activities; at the time of his arrest he was said to be carrying an Indian passport bearing the name "Hussein Mubarak Patel". On 22 July 2016 he is said to have made a confessional statement recorded before a magistrate.

(b) The notification to India. Pakistan informed India of the arrest and detention of Mr. Jadhav by a note verbale dated 25 March 2016 — some three weeks after the arrest. India requested consular access on the same day and repeated the request many times (the casebook records that India made a number of requests for consular access from 25 March 2016 onwards). Pakistan did not grant access. Its first response to the request, in a note verbale dated 21 March 2017, was that the question of consular access "shall be considered, in the light of Indian side's response to Pakistan's request for assistance in investigation process and early dispensation of justice".

(c) The trial and the sentence. The trial of Mr. Jadhav began on 21 September 2016 before a Field General Court Martial. It appears from the material made public that he was tried under section 59 of the Pakistan Army Act, 1952 and section 3 of the Official Secrets Act, 1923. Pakistan stated that after the trial had begun he was given an additional three weeks to prepare his defence and that a "law qualified field officer" was appointed for him. The details of the trial were made public by press release and statement of 10 and 14 April 2017, from which it appeared that Mr. Jadhav had been convicted and sentenced to death. He was, on Pakistan's account, informed of his right to appeal: an appeal to a military appellate court within 60 days, and a petition to the Federal Government within 90 days of the decision of the Chief of Army Staff. On 26 April 2017 the High Commission of India in Islamabad transmitted, on behalf of Mr. Jadhav's mother, an "appeal" under section 133(B) and a petition to the Federal Government under section 131 of the Pakistan Army Act — both, India said, prepared on the basis of public information alone because Pakistan had denied it access to the case file. On 22 June 2017 Pakistan announced that Mr. Jadhav had made a mercy petition to the Chief of Army Staff.

(d) The proceedings in the Court. India filed its Application on 8 May 2017, founding jurisdiction on Article 36(1) of the Statute of the Court and on Article I of the Optional Protocol to the Vienna Convention concerning the Compulsory Settlement of Disputes. On 18 May 2017 the Court made an Order indicating provisional measures, directing Pakistan to "take all measures at its disposal to ensure that Mr. Jadhav is not executed pending the final decision in these proceedings" and to inform the Court of all measures taken — the Order was unanimous. Pakistan raised preliminary objections to jurisdiction and admissibility; public hearings were held in February 2019; and judgment was delivered on 17 July 2019.

3. India's claims and Pakistan's answers

India's final submissions. India asked the Court to declare that Pakistan had breached Article 36(1)(a), (b) and (c) of the Vienna Convention by (i) not informing India without delay of the detention of Mr. Jadhav; (ii) not informing Mr. Jadhav of his rights under Article 36; and (iii) denying Indian consular officers access to him; and to order that the sentence of the Field General Court Martial be declared void, that Mr. Jadhav be released and that his safe passage to India be facilitated — the relief of restitutio in integrum.

Pakistan's three arguments on applicability, and its objection to admissibility. First, that the Vienna Convention "does not apply in prima facie cases of espionage". Secondly, that customary international law governs cases of espionage in consular relations and permits exceptions to consular access. Thirdly, that it is the 2008 Agreement on Consular Access between the two countries, rather than Article 36, which regulates consular access in this case. Pakistan also objected to the admissibility of India's Application, and informed the Court, through its Agent and counsel, that the right to a fair trial in Pakistan is constitutionally guaranteed and "absolute", that all trials are conducted accordingly, and that judicial review is always available — citing the review jurisdiction of the High Courts and, as an example, a decision of the Peshawar High Court of 2018.

4. Jurisdiction and admissibility

  • Jurisdiction. India and Pakistan had been parties to the Vienna Convention since 28 December 1977 and 14 May 1969 respectively, and both were parties to the Optional Protocol without reservations or declarations. Article I of the Optional Protocol provides that "disputes arising out of the interpretation or application of the Convention shall lie within the compulsory jurisdiction of the International Court of Justice". The dispute concerned the interpretation and application of Article 36, and the Court therefore had jurisdiction (operative point 1: unanimous).
  • Admissibility. Pakistan's objections were rejected: the Application was admissible (operative point 2: 15 votes to 1). Two points of principle were settled here: that India was entitled to bring the claim in its own right and in the exercise of consular protection; and that the dispute was one of treaty interpretation and application and not an appeal against a criminal conviction as such.
  • The Court's limits. The Court was careful, throughout, to state what it was not deciding: it was not sitting in appeal from the Field General Court Martial; it did not pass on the truth of the allegations of espionage; and it did not seek to annul the conviction as a matter of Pakistan's criminal law — the remedy it fashioned is described in §8 below.

5. The first question — does Article 36 apply at all? (the three arguments rejected)

(a) No espionage exception in the text. "Neither Article 36 nor any other provision of the Vienna Convention contains a reference to cases of espionage." Reading Article 36 in its context and in the light of the object and purpose of the Convention — whose preamble speaks of contributing "to the development of friendly relations among nations" and whose Article 36(1) opens with the purpose of "facilitating the exercise of consular functions relating to nationals of the sending State" — the Court held that consular officers may in all cases exercise the rights of access, and that "it would run counter to the purpose of that provision if the rights it provides could be disregarded when the receiving State alleges that a foreign national in its custody was involved in acts of espionage".

(b) The travaux préparatoires confirm the reading. The Court examined the ILC discussions of 1960 on "consular intercourse and immunities" and the records of the Vienna Conference of 1963; they confirmed that Article 36 does not exclude classes of persons such as those suspected of espionage. The single point for which Pakistan found support in the travaux — the proposal of the ILC's Special Rapporteur that notification might be deferred — went no further than to allow a short delay, and was not adopted as an exception. (This is the point which students should keep: the negotiation history supports "delay", not "denial".)

(c) Custom cannot supply an exception where the treaty speaks. Article 36 "expressly regulates the question of consular access to, and communication with, nationals of the sending State and makes no exception with regard to cases of espionage", so that Article 36, and not customary international law, governed the matter in the relations between the parties. Having so held, the Court found it unnecessary to determine whether the customary rule alleged by Pakistan had existed in 1963.

(d) The 2008 Agreement does not displace Article 36. Point (vi) of the India–Pakistan Agreement on Consular Access of 21 May 2008 provides: "In case of arrest, detention or sentence made on political or security grounds, each side may examine the case on its merits." The Court held that point (vi) "cannot be read as denying consular access" in such cases: had the parties intended to restrict the rights guaranteed by Article 36, "one would expect such an intention to be unequivocally reflected in the provisions of the Agreement"; and any derogation from Article 36 for political or security reasons "may render the right related to consular access meaningless as it would give the receiving State the possibility of denying such access". Turning to Article 73(2) of the Convention — which preserves the freedom of States to conclude agreements "confirming or supplementing or extending or amplifying" its provisions — the Court held that the paragraph contemplates only agreements of that character, that the 2008 Agreement had been negotiated by the parties in full awareness of Article 73(2), and that it was a subsequent agreement intended to "confirm, supplement, extend or amplify" the Convention. Consequently point (vi) does not displace the obligations under Article 36. The conclusion of the whole section: "the Vienna Convention is applicable in the present case, regardless of the allegations that Mr. Jadhav was engaged in espionage activities."

6. The second question — what Article 36 required, and what was breached

Table 7.16 — The three alleged breaches, as found by the Court
Obligation under Article 36(1)What the Court foundResult
(b), first limb — to inform the detained national of his rightsPakistan never claimed to have informed Mr. Jadhav of his Article 36 rights; on the contrary, it maintained throughout that the Convention did not apply to a person suspected of espionage. The Court inferred from that position that the information had not been given.Breach of Article 36(1)(b) (operative point 3: 15-1).
(b), second limb — to inform the consular post of the sending State "without delay", if the national so requestsNotification was in fact given on 25 March 2016 (and enabled India to request access the same day). But there were "sufficient grounds at the time of the arrest on 3 March 2016 or shortly thereafter for Pakistan to conclude that the person was, or was likely to be, an Indian national"; the delay of some three weeks was not "without delay". The Court held that "without delay" is not to be read as "immediately upon arrest and before interrogation", but also that the circumstances here made three weeks excessive.Breach of Article 36(1)(b) — the notification limb (operative point 4: 15-1).
(a) and (c) — the right of consular officers to visit, converse and correspond with the national, and to arrange his legal representationNo consular access was ever granted. Pakistan's condition — that access would be considered in the light of India's co-operation in the investigation — was no answer: India's alleged failure to co-operate "does not relieve Pakistan of its obligation to grant consular access". Nor did the appointment of a defending officer for Mr. Jadhav dispense with the consular officers' right to arrange legal representation, which presupposes conversation and correspondence with the detained person.Breach of Article 36(1)(a) and (c) (operative point 5: 15-1).

Three structural points from this section should be memorised. First, the Court treated Article 36(1) as creating individual rights — of the detained person — which, by Article I of the Optional Protocol, may be invoked before the Court by the national State (the line of reasoning begun in LaGrand and Avena). Secondly, the duty to notify the consular post is implied by Article 36(1)(c) as well as by (b): the rights of consular officers to visit and to arrange representation presuppose that they know of the detention. Thirdly, the duty is not conditional on reciprocity or on the sending State's conduct: a receiving State cannot make consular access a bargaining counter in an investigation.

7. The remedy — why "annulment" was refused and "review and reconsideration" ordered

India sought the annulment of the conviction and sentence and Mr. Jadhav's release. The Court refused that formulation, and the passage in which it did so is the passage to reproduce: the Court "reiterates that it is not the conviction and sentence of Mr. Jadhav which are to be regarded as a violation of Article 36 of the Vienna Convention"; and "it is not to be presumed that partial or total annulment of conviction or sentence provides the necessary and sole remedy in cases of violations of Article 36". The appropriate remedy is effective review and reconsideration of the conviction and sentence — a remedy which Pakistan itself acknowledged to be appropriate. The Court then qualified it in five ways:

  1. Effectiveness. "Special emphasis must be placed on the need for the review and reconsideration to be effective": it must give full weight to the effect of the violation of Article 36(1) and must guarantee that the violation, and any prejudice caused by it, are fully examined.
  2. Procedure. Effective review presupposes a procedure which can examine both the violation and its consequences — including any implications for the evidence and for the right of defence of the accused, and the requirements of a fair trial.
  3. Choice of means. The obligation may be performed "in various ways", and the choice of means is left to Pakistan; but the freedom is "not without qualification".
  4. An obligation of result. The obligation to provide effective review and reconsideration "is an obligation of result which must be performed unconditionally"; Pakistan "shall take all measures to provide for effective review and reconsideration, including, if necessary, by enacting appropriate legislation". Clemency procedures may supplement, but cannot substitute for, judicial review and reconsideration.
  5. The stay. The Court recalled the provisional measure of 18 May 2017 and declared that "a continued stay of execution constitutes an indispensable condition for the effective review and reconsideration of the conviction and sentence of Mr. Jadhav".

8. The operative clause (paragraph 149 of the judgment) — from the official summary

  1. Unanimously, finds that it has jurisdiction, on the basis of Article I of the Optional Protocol concerning the Compulsory Settlement of Disputes to the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations of 24 April 1963, to entertain the Application filed by India on 8 May 2017.
  2. By fifteen votes to one, rejects Pakistan's objections to the admissibility of the Application and finds the Application admissible (against: Judge ad hoc Jillani).
  3. By fifteen votes to one, finds that, by not informing Mr. Jadhav without delay of his rights under Article 36(1)(b), Pakistan breached the obligations incumbent upon it under that provision.
  4. By fifteen votes to one, finds that, by not notifying the appropriate consular post of India without delay of the detention of Mr. Jadhav, and thereby depriving India of the right to render the assistance provided for by the Convention, Pakistan breached the obligations incumbent upon it under Article 36(1)(b).
  5. By fifteen votes to one, finds that Pakistan deprived India of the right to communicate with and have access to Mr. Jadhav, to visit him in detention and to arrange for his legal representation, and thereby breached its obligations under Article 36(1)(a) and (c).
  6. By fifteen votes to one, finds that Pakistan is under an obligation to inform Mr. Jadhav without further delay of his rights and to provide Indian consular officers access to him in accordance with Article 36.
  7. By fifteen votes to one, finds that the appropriate reparation consists in the obligation of Pakistan to provide, by the means of its own choosing, effective review and reconsideration of the conviction and sentence of Mr. Jadhav, so as to ensure that full weight is given to the effect of the violation of the rights set forth in Article 36, taking account of paragraphs 139, 145 and 146 of the judgment.
  8. By fifteen votes to one, declares that a continued stay of execution constitutes an indispensable condition for the effective review and reconsideration of the conviction and sentence of Mr. Jadhav.

Judge Cançado Trindade appended a separate opinion; Judges Sebutinde, Robinson and Iwasawa appended declarations; and Judge ad hoc Jillani (chosen by Pakistan under Article 31(2) of the Statute) appended a dissenting opinion — he disagreed with the majority on the admissibility of the Application and on each of the findings of breach.

9. The principle evolved — ten propositions

  1. Article 36 is a self-executing guarantee, not a discretionary courtesy. It creates rights for the individual and obligations for the receiving State, enforceable by the sending State before the ICJ under the Optional Protocol.
  2. There is no espionage exception. Neither the text nor the travaux of the Vienna Convention permits a receiving State to withhold consular access because it alleges that the detained national was a spy.
  3. "Without delay" is a substantive standard. It is not "immediately upon arrest and before interrogation", but it is measured against the circumstances and against the information reasonably available to the receiving State; three weeks, where nationality was indicated from the outset, was too long.
  4. Notification and access are linked. The duty to inform the consular post flows from Article 36(1)(c) as well as (b); and the duty to inform the detainee of his rights is the practical precondition of his ability to request notification.
  5. Access cannot be made conditional. Neither the sending State's co-operation in an investigation, nor the appointment of a domestic defence officer, answers the consular right to visit, converse, correspond and arrange legal representation.
  6. Subsequent bilateral agreements are read against Article 73(2). The 2008 India–Pakistan Agreement confirms and amplifies Article 36; point (vi) does not displace or restrict it — a rule which now governs the interpretation of every consular-access agreement.
  7. Consular law interacts with, but does not itself supply, fair-trial guarantees. The violation must be examined through the lens of fair trial in the review process; the Court's remedy presupposes that the domestic procedure can give the violation meaningful effect.
  8. Annulment is not automatic. A breach of Article 36 does not ipso facto void a conviction; the remedy is effective review and reconsideration — the remedy settled in Avena and applied, in a death-penalty context, in Jadhav.
  9. The remedy is an obligation of result. Means are left to the respondent State, but the result is not: the review must be effective, unconditional and capable of giving full weight to the violation, by legislation if necessary.
  10. Life is protected pending compliance. A continued stay of execution is "indispensable" — the operative expression of the provisional measure of 18 May 2017 and of the Court's practice since LaGrand.
Using case 43 in an answer — the compression

One sentence for the facts: an Indian national was arrested in Pakistan in March 2016, tried by a Field General Court Martial under section 59 of the Pakistan Army Act, 1952 and section 3 of the Official Secrets Act, 1923, convicted and sentenced to death, while India was notified only after three weeks and was never given consular access. One sentence for the law: Pakistan's three arguments — espionage exception in the text, espionage exception in custom, and the 2008 Agreement — were all rejected, and Pakistan was held to have breached Article 36(1)(b) (both limbs) and Article 36(1)(a) and (c). One sentence for the remedy: the Court refused annulment but ordered effective review and reconsideration as an obligation of result, with a continued stay of execution as an indispensable condition. Add the two connecting points which answer a problem question: Article I of the Optional Protocol is the jurisdictional gateway (the same gateway used in Tehran, case 41); and the case shows the practical meaning of VCCR Article 36(2) — the laws and regulations of the receiving State "must enable full effect to be given to the purposes for which the rights accorded under this article are intended".

7.4Diplomatic Immunity — Personal and Property

Case 41 (Tehran) supplied the facts; this head supplies the law. The syllabus asks for "diplomatic immunity: personal and property", and the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961, answers the question in two packages. The personal package protects the human being: his person is inviolable (Article 29), his private residence, papers and property are protected (Article 30), he is immune from the criminal, civil and administrative jurisdiction of the receiving State subject to three express exceptions (Article 31), and he is exempt from taxes, customs and social-security obligations (Articles 33–36). The property package protects the mission as an institution: premises are inviolable (Article 22), archives and documents are inviolable "at any time and wherever they may be" (Article 24), official correspondence is inviolable and the diplomatic bag may not be opened or detained (Article 27). Surrounding both packages are the rules of duration (Articles 39–40), duties (Articles 41–42), the self-help remedies of the receiving State (Article 9), and the rules for died-out relations and armed conflict (Articles 44–45). The whole of Topic 7's sub-head 7.4 — and most of what the 2025 paper's hypothetical demanded — lies in these twenty-eight articles.

A. The Architecture of the Immunities Chapter (Articles 20–47 at a Glance)

Before the articles are taken one by one, three distinctions must be grasped, because examiners test them directly.

  • Privilege, immunity and inviolability are not synonyms. A privilege is an exemption from a substantive burden of the local law — taxes (Article 34), customs duties (Article 36), social security (Article 33), personal services and military obligations (Article 35). An immunity is an exemption from the jurisdiction of the local courts and authorities — Articles 31 (from jurisdiction) and 22(3), 30(2) (from enforcement). Inviolability is thicker still: it means not merely that the receiving State's courts must decline to act, but that its authorities may not act at all — no arrest, no detention, no entry, no search, no attachment (Articles 22, 24, 27, 29, 30). Inviolability cannot be waived retrospectively by a court; only the sending State can waive (Article 32).
  • Every privilege in the Convention belongs to the sending State, not to the individual. The preamble and the operative provisions make the immunities functional — they exist "not to benefit individuals but to ensure the efficient performance of the functions of diplomatic missions as representing States" (preamble, fourth recital). The individual agent may not therefore waive them; only the sending State may, and it may do so even against the agent's wishes (Article 32).
  • Immunity is accompanied by duty. Articles 41 and 42 impose on the mission and its members the duty to respect the laws of the receiving State, not to interfere in its internal affairs, and not to use the premises for purposes incompatible with the functions of the mission; and the receiving State's remedy for abuse lies in Article 9, not in self-help. Section 7.4 L returns to this "self-contained régime" which case 41 established.
Table 7.17 — The immunities chapter (VCDR Articles 20–47): the whole of §7.4 on one page
ArticleSubjectThe rule in one line
20Flag and emblemThe mission and its head may fly the sending State's flag and display its emblem on the premises (including the head's residence) and on his means of transport.
21Assistance in obtaining premisesThe receiving State must either facilitate the acquisition of premises or assist the mission in obtaining accommodation, and must where necessary assist members in finding accommodation.
22Premises(1) Premises inviolable; no entry by agents of the receiving State without the consent of the head of mission. (2) Special duty to protect them against intrusion or damage and to prevent disturbance of the peace of the mission or impairment of its dignity. (3) Premises, furnishings, other property and means of transport immune from search, requisition, attachment and execution.
23Taxation of premisesThe sending State and the head of mission are exempt from all dues and taxes on the premises, owned or leased, save charges for specific services; but persons contracting with them remain liable.
24Archives and documents"The archives and documents of the mission shall be inviolable at any time and wherever they may be."
25Facilities"The receiving State shall accord full facilities for the performance of the functions of the mission."
26Freedom of movementMembers of the mission may move and travel freely, subject to laws on security zones.
27CommunicationFree communication for all official purposes; inviolability of official correspondence; the diplomatic bag may not be opened or detained; couriers protected; the wireless transmitter requires consent.
28Fees charged by the missionFees and charges levied in the course of official duties are exempt from all dues and taxes.
29Inviolability of the personThe person of a diplomatic agent is inviolable: he may not be arrested or detained; the receiving State must treat him with due respect and take all appropriate steps to prevent any attack on his person, freedom or dignity.
30Residence, papers, propertyThe private residence enjoys the same inviolability as the premises; papers, correspondence and, subject to Article 31(3), property also enjoy inviolability.
31Immunity from jurisdiction(1) Immunity from criminal, civil and administrative jurisdiction, save three exceptions. (2) No obligation to give evidence. (3) No measures of execution save in the three excepted cases and without prejudice to Article 22. (4) Immunity from counterclaim.
32WaiverWaiver must be express; and waiver of immunity from jurisdiction does not imply waiver of immunity from execution, which must be separate.
33Social securityExemption in respect of services for the sending State, with the corresponding obligations of the agent as an employer.
34TaxationExemption from dues and taxes, personal or real, national, regional or municipal — with four exceptions, including indirect taxes and income from commercial activity in the receiving State.
35Personal servicesExemption from all personal services, public service of any kind, and military obligations such as requisitioning, contributions and billeting.
36CustomsImportation free of duty for the mission's official use and for the agent's personal use, subject to procedures; baggage may be inspected only in the presence of the agent or his representative.
37Family and staffExtends privileges and immunities downwards through family members, administrative and technical staff, service staff and private servants, in a graded manner.
38Nationals and permanent residentsImmunity from jurisdiction and inviolability only; no other privileges, for members who are nationals of or permanently resident in the receiving State.
39Commencement and durationPrivileges arise from entry (or notification) and end with departure; but immunity continues for acts performed in the exercise of the agent's functions.
40Third StatesA third State must accord inviolability and transit facilities to agents crossing its territory; special rules for force majeure.
41DutiesRespect for the laws of the receiving State; no interference in internal affairs; premises not to be used incompatibly with the mission's functions.
42No private gainful activityNo member of the mission may practise a professional or commercial activity for personal profit in the receiving State.
43End of functionsEnds on notification by the sending State, on refusal to recognise the agent under Article 9(2), and on the recalling of the mission or severance of relations.
44Armed conflictFacilities for departure of privileged persons and their families even in armed conflict, including means of transport.
45Broken relationsRespect and protection of premises, property and archives; third-State custody of the premises; third-State protection of interests and of nationals.
46Temporary protection of a third State's interestsA sending State may, with the receiving State's consent and at the request of a third State not represented there, take over the temporary protection of the third State's interests and those of its nationals.
47Non-discrimination and reciprocityNo discrimination between States; but restrictive application answering restrictive application, and favourable treatment by custom or agreement, are permitted.

Immunities beyond the mission — States, international organisations and special missions

The topic list asks for the related instruments at the place where they matter, and the place is here, because the rationale of the diplomat's immunity is the same as that of the State's and of the international organisation's: functional independence. Five instruments extend the pattern.

  • UN Charter, Articles 104–105. Article 104: "The Organization shall enjoy in the territory of each of its Members such legal capacity as may be necessary for the exercise of its functions and the fulfilment of its purposes." Article 105(1): "The Organization shall enjoy in the territory of each of its Members such privileges and immunities as are necessary for the fulfilment of its purposes." Article 105(2): "Representatives of the Members of the United Nations and officials of the Organization shall similarly enjoy such privileges and immunities as are necessary for the independent exercise of their functions in connection with the Organization." The Charter thus states the principle; the detail comes from the conventions below.
  • The General Convention on the Privileges and Immunities of the United Nations, 1946 (and the parallel Convention on the Specialised Agencies, 1947): the property and assets of the Organization are immune from legal process; its premises inviolable; its archives inviolable; it is exempt from taxes and customs duties; officials enjoy immunity "in respect of words spoken or written and all acts performed by them in their official capacity", continued after they cease to be officials. Section 22, on "experts on mission", was the provision applied in the advisory opinion on the Difference Relating to Immunity from Legal Process of a Special Rapporteur of the Commission on Human Rights (1999): the Court held that a Special Rapporteur was entitled to immunity and that the determination of the Secretary-General, as the organ with the primary responsibility for determining whether an official's conduct fell within his official functions, was entitled to the greatest possible weight. The case is the international-organisation analogue of Article 31(1)(c)'s official-act immunity.
  • International Organisations (Privileges and Immunities) Act, 1948 (India). This is the statute under which India confers privileges and immunities upon international organisations of which it is a member, and on their officers and representatives — the statutory route by which Article 105 of the Charter is implemented in Indian law, read with the notifications issued thereunder. It is the Indian counterpart of the "relevant headquarters or seat agreements" of the kind concluded by host States with international organisations (and, in respect of the specialised agencies, of the 1947 Convention).
  • Convention on Special Missions, 1969 (and its Optional Protocol concerning the Compulsory Settlement of Disputes): applies the 1961 Convention's logic to ad hoc missions — the special mission as a whole and its members enjoy the facilities, privileges and immunities necessary for their functions; the head of a special mission and its members enjoy the same inviolability and immunity as the corresponding ranks in a permanent mission; a special mission is not a diplomatic mission and the sending State must notify the receiving State of its composition and arrival.
  • Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of Crimes against Internationally Protected Persons, including Diplomatic Agents, 1973 (India acceded on 11 April 1978; the United States invoked Articles 4 and 7 of this Convention in the Tehran pleadings): it obliges States to criminalise attacks on Heads of State and Government, ministers for foreign affairs and diplomatic agents, to establish jurisdiction, and to co-operate in prevention, prosecution and extradition. It is the receiving State's own duty of protection (Article 22(2) of the VCDR and Article 29) backed by criminal law — a point to remember in Indian contexts, where attacks on Indian missions abroad or foreign missions in India raise the Convention as well as local law.

Two further instruments complete the picture and are cited in the syllabus list. The ILC's 1989 Draft Articles on the Status of the Diplomatic Courier and the Diplomatic Bag not Accompanied by a Diplomatic Courier are the framework for the recurring problem discussed at §7.4 D — the unaccompanied bag — and were the product of the same ILC study that produced the 1975 Convention on the Representation of States in their Relations with International Organisations of a Universal Character. And the 1994 Convention on the Safety of United Nations and Associated Personnel (with its 2005 Optional Protocol) extends criminal protection to peacekeeping and humanitarian personnel — the international-organisation counterpart of the 1973 Convention.

B. The Premises of the Mission (Article 22)

"1. The premises of the mission shall be inviolable. The agents of the receiving State may not enter them, except with the consent of the head of the mission. 2. The receiving State is under a special duty to take all appropriate steps to protect the premises of the mission against any intrusion or damage and to prevent any disturbance of the peace of the mission or impairment of its dignity. 3. The premises of the mission, their furnishings and other property thereon and the means of transport of the mission shall be immune from search, requisition, attachment or execution." (VCDR Article 22)

1. The three paragraphs as three distinct duties

  1. Inviolability and the consent rule (Article 22(1)). No agent of the receiving State — police, revenue officer, process server, fire officer — may enter the premises without the consent of the head of mission. Consent may be implied by an emergency, but that implication is a matter of grace and practice, not of obligation: unlike Article 31(2) of the consular Convention, Article 22 contains no presumption of consent for fire or other disaster. Consent, where given, may be made subject to conditions (a police officer may be admitted only to investigate a specific incident, and not to conduct a general search). The deliberate entry of premises without consent is a breach of international law and, under the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972, is also a breach of Indian statutory law (see §7.4 N).
  2. The "special duty" of protection (Article 22(2)). The duty is not to avoid attacking the mission but to protect it — "all appropriate steps" against intrusion or damage, and to prevent "any disturbance of the peace of the mission or impairment of its dignity". Tehran is its authoritative interpretation: the Iranian authorities were "fully aware" of the obligations, had the means, and failed to act; the failure was itself the breach (see case 41, paragraphs 61–68). The duty explains India's own practice of policing demonstrations outside foreign missions, and it explains why a receiving State cannot plead that the crowd was "not its own": the obligation is one of due diligence, not merely of non-participation.
  3. Enforcement immunity of the mission's property (Article 22(3)). Premises, furnishings, other property "thereon" and the means of transport of the mission cannot be searched, requisitioned, attached or executed. This paragraph is the diplomatic counterpart of the State-immunity rule in §7.3 F: a private creditor with a judgment cannot attach the embassy building or the mission's vehicles. Where the premises are leased to the mission by a private landlord, the landlord's remedy is against the sending State in the forum permitted by international law and the Convention, and not by self-help on the premises: the juxtaposition of Article 31(1)(a) — the private-immovable-property exception for the diplomatic agent personally — with Article 22(3) — the inviolability of the mission — is a favourite examination problem (compare Century Twenty One (P) Ltd. v. Union of India, AIR 1987 Del. 124, discussed at §7.3 G).

2. The auxiliary articles

Article 21 obliges the receiving State to facilitate the acquisition of premises and to assist, "where necessary", in obtaining accommodation for members of the mission. Article 23 exempts the sending State and the head of mission from all national, regional or municipal dues and taxes in respect of the premises, owned or leased, "other than such as represent payment for specific services rendered" — while preserving the liability of the persons who contract with them. Article 20 allows the flag and emblem of the sending State on the premises, the residence of the head of mission and his means of transport. Article 25 requires "full facilities for the performance of the functions of the mission" — the general clause of which Articles 21–27 are particular applications; and Article 30(1) extends the inviolability and protection of Article 22 to the private residence of the diplomatic agent and to his papers, correspondence and property (with the Article 31(3) qualification for execution).

3. Diplomatic asylum — what the premises are not for

The Convention nowhere confers a right of diplomatic asylum. General international law does not recognise such a right; the ICJ said so in the Asylum case (Colombia v. Peru, 1950) and in Haya de la Torre (1951), where a grant of asylum in the Colombian embassy in Lima was held not to be opposable to Peru, and the dispute ended not with a right of safe conduct but with the termination of the asylum. Diplomatic asylum exists as a regional institution in Latin America (the Havana Convention of 1928 and the Caracas Convention on Diplomatic Asylum of 1954) and in practice — a State may allow a person to remain on its premises without that person acquiring any right to remain or to leave safely, and without the receiving State acquiring any right to enter. Because the premises are inviolable, a stand-off is legally possible: the receiving State cannot enter, and the sending State is not obliged to hand the person over; but Article 41(3) forbids the premises to be used "in any manner incompatible with the functions of the mission", and the receiving State may respond by declaring the head of mission persona non grata (Article 9) or by severing relations (Article 45). The prolonged stand-off at the Ecuadorean embassy in London (2012–2019) is the modern illustration: asylum was granted to Mr Julian Assange in 2012, the United Kingdom refused to recognise any right of safe passage, the premises remained inviolable, and the situation was resolved only years later by the withdrawal of that asylum and the arrest of the person outside the embassy.

4. Real incidents — how Article 22 is used and abused

Table 7.18 — Eight incidents that illustrate inviolability of premises and of the person
EpisodeWhat happenedThe Article engaged, and the lesson
United States Embassy, Tehran, 4 November 1979Several hundred armed militants overran the compound; Iranian authorities stood aside and thereafter endorsed the occupation.Articles 22(1), 22(2) and 22(3) — the leading case (41). Failure to protect is itself a breach; endorsement makes the occupiers' conduct the State's.
Iraqi Embassy, Islamabad, February 1973Pakistani forces searched the embassy and seized a large quantity of arms and ammunition alleged to have been intended for Baluch insurgents.Articles 22(1) and 41 — the converse problem: the receiving State's entry is unlawful even where the mission's premises were being used, allegedly, for interference in the receiving State's internal affairs. The lawful remedy is Article 9 and Article 45, not entry.
Libyan People's Bureau, London, April 1984Shots were fired from the Bureau during a demonstration against the Libyan Government, killing WPC Yvonne Fletcher and injuring others.Articles 22(1) and 22(2); Article 9. The United Kingdom did not enter while the Bureau remained a mission: it severed diplomatic relations, allowed the occupants to depart under immunity, and only then searched the premises — the Article 45 route. The episode is also the parent of the 1984–86 diplomatic-police reforms in London.
Chinese Embassy, Belgrade, 7 May 1999NATO aircraft bombed the embassy during the Kosovo campaign, killing three Chinese journalists.Article 22(2) in its starkest form: the duty of protection is owed even in armed conflict. The incident is also used to show that a receiving State's duty under Article 22 does not disappear when its own forces are engaged.
Kuwait, August 1990 — and the Security Council's responseDuring the invasion and subsequent occupation, diplomatic and consular premises and staff in Kuwait were subjected to interference and duress.Articles 22, 24, 29 and 45, enforced by the Security Council: Resolutions 664 (1990) and 667 (1990) demanded that Iraq ensure the safety and security of diplomatic and consular personnel and premises. The episodes show that a chapter-VII organ may take up the protection of missions where the receiving State is itself the offender — a remedy outside the Convention.
Ecuadorean Embassy, London, 2012–2019Asylum was granted to Mr Julian Assange in the embassy; the United Kingdom declined to recognise any right of safe passage; the stand-off lasted seven years.Article 22(1) against Article 41(3); no right of diplomatic asylum in general international law (Asylum 1950; Haya de la Torre 1951). A stand-off is lawful but is resolved by political means, by persona non grata under Article 9, or by severance under Article 45.
Attacks on Indian missions abroad (Kabul 2008 and 2009; Mazar-i-Sharif 2010; Herat 2014); the 2024 India–Canada expulsionsIndian missions and their personnel have been attacked in Afghanistan; and in 2024 a dispute between India and Canada led to mutual expulsions and the withdrawal of a large number of diplomats.Article 22(2) (protection) and Article 29 (person) — the receiving State's due-diligence duty; and Articles 9, 11 and 47 (the size and composition of missions, and non-discrimination), which govern the political fall-out. Where a receiving State cannot or will not protect, the sending State may reduce its mission or suspend its functioning, and Article 45 governs the protection of its premises thereafter.
Arrest and strip-search of Devyani Khobragade, New York, 12 December 2013India's Deputy Consul-General in New York was arrested, handcuffed in public and strip-searched on charges of visa fraud and underpayment of a domestic worker; she was later transferred to India's Permanent Mission to the United Nations, acquiring diplomatic status.VCCR Articles 41 and 43 — a consular officer may be arrested or detained only for a grave crime and pursuant to a decision of the competent judicial authority, and immunity from jurisdiction is limited to acts performed in the exercise of consular functions. See §7.5 K. The episode illustrates the price of the narrower consular package, and the practice of "re-designation" to obtain the wider diplomatic one.

↑ top

C. Archives, Documents and Correspondence (Articles 24 and 27)

"The archives and documents of the mission shall be inviolable at any time and wherever they may be." (VCDR Article 24)

Article 24 is the shortest and one of the strongest provisions of the Convention. Four features of its wording carry the whole weight of the rule.

  • "Archives and documents." The expression covers the mission's records in the broadest sense — correspondence, cipher and code, letter books, registers, files, tapes, discs, electronic mail and every other form of record of the mission's activity. It is not confined to papers physically stored on the premises: the definition of "archives" carries a functional character, so that a document relating to the mission's work remains an archive wherever it is.
  • "Inviolable." Not merely immune from seizure, but immune from any interference: no examination, no copying, no inspection, no requisition, no production in evidence — as Article 31(2) reinforces by exempting the agent from the obligation to give evidence.
  • "At any time." Inviolability does not lapse with the end of the mission, the recall of the head of mission, or the severance of diplomatic relations. Article 45 expressly requires the receiving State, even in case of armed conflict, to "respect and protect the premises of the mission, together with its property and archives".
  • "Wherever they may be." The archives may not be interfered with even when they are outside the premises — in a store, in transit, at a bank, or in the hands of a third party. In Tehran, the appropriation of the Embassy's property and archives by the militants was one of the breaches which Iran, having endorsed the occupation, was held to have committed; and operative point 3(c) of the judgment ordered that "the premises, property, archives and documents of the United States Embassy in Tehran and of its Consulates in Iran" be placed in the hands of the protecting Power — the remedy of Article 45 applied, as it were, in advance.

Correspondence (Article 27(1)–(2)). "The receiving State shall permit and protect free communication on the part of the mission for all official purposes. In communicating with the Government and the other missions and consulates of its own country, wherever situated, the mission may employ all appropriate means, including diplomatic couriers and messages in code or cipher. However, the mission may install and use a wireless transmitter only with the consent of the receiving State." Article 27(2) adds: "The official correspondence of the mission shall be inviolable. Official correspondence means all correspondence relating to the mission and its functions." Three practical consequences follow. First, the consent requirement for wireless transmitters is the reason why the installation of an unregistered transmitter is a serious matter: it converts a permitted facility into an act outside the Convention. Secondly, the inviolability of official correspondence is not limited to correspondence in transit inside a bag: correspondence intercepted in any manner — even by electronic surveillance or by reading messages — engages the receiving State's obligation, though the treaty's remedy for interception is diplomatic rather than judicial. Thirdly, the "free communication" clause applies only to official purposes; private business and the professional or commercial activity prohibited by Article 42 receive no protection. Article 26 completes the package: members of the mission enjoy freedom of movement and travel in the territory of the receiving State, subject to its laws and regulations concerning zones entry into which is prohibited or regulated for reasons of national security.

D. The Diplomatic Bag and the Courier (Article 27(3)–(7))

"3. The diplomatic bag must not be opened or detained. … 4. The packages constituting the diplomatic bag must bear visible external marks of their character and may contain only diplomatic documents or articles intended for official use. 5. The diplomatic courier … shall be provided with an official document indicating his status and the number of packages constituting the diplomatic bag. He shall be protected by the receiving State in the performance of his functions. He shall enjoy personal inviolability and shall not be liable to any form of arrest or detention. 6. The sending State … may designate ad hoc diplomatic couriers. … 7. The diplomatic bag may be entrusted to the captain of a commercial aircraft …" (VCDR Article 27, in outline)

Table 7.19 — The diplomatic bag: what may and may not be done
QuestionThe rule
May the receiving State open the bag?No. Article 27(3) is absolute: "The diplomatic bag shall not be opened or detained."
May it scan or X-ray the bag without opening it?The Convention is silent. Practice tolerates non-intrusive external examination (and the increasing use of scanning equipment at airports), and the ILC's 1989 draft articles proceed on the footing that the bag may not be opened but may be returned. Scanning which reveals the contents only in outline is generally accepted; any step which amounts to an inspection of the contents is not.
What if the receiving State has "serious reason to believe" the bag contains items other than documents or articles for official use?Article 27(4) provides the only permitted response: the receiving State "may request that the bag be returned to its place of origin". It may not open it, and it may not detain it indefinitely.
What are the formal conditions?The packages must bear visible external marks of their character (Article 27(4)). A package which does not bear them — or which is not presented as a bag at all — does not attract the protection.
What may the bag contain?"Only diplomatic documents or articles intended for official use." A bag containing, say, commercial goods or a human being is not a diplomatic bag; its use is a breach of Article 27(4) and of the receiving State's rights.
What protection does the courier have?He must carry an official document indicating his status and the number of packages; he is protected in the performance of his functions; and he enjoys personal inviolability and immunity from arrest or detention (Article 27(5)). An ad hoc courier may be appointed for a single journey (Article 27(6)).
What about the bag entrusted to an aircraft captain?Article 27(7): the bag may be entrusted to "the captain of a commercial aircraft scheduled to land at an authorised port of entry"; the captain must be provided with an official document indicating the number of packages, but he is not a diplomatic courier; and the mission may send a member to take delivery "directly and freely" from him.
What is the unaccompanied bag problem?The Convention assumes the bag will be accompanied by a courier. Where it is not, States have differed: some treat an unaccompanied bag as immune; others require that it be delivered only to the mission. The ILC's 1989 Draft Articles on the Status of the Diplomatic Courier and the Diplomatic Bag not Accompanied by a Diplomatic Courier were produced precisely because practice had been inconsistent, and remain the principal statement of the competing positions.

The Dikko affair (July 1984) — the bag as an instrument of crime. In July 1984 a Nigerian exile in London, Dr Umaru Dikko, was seized by agents acting for the Nigerian Government, drugged and placed in a crate marked "diplomatic baggage" which was taken to Stansted airport for a flight to Lagos. The crate was intercepted, and it was opened — Nigeria protesting that the United Kingdom had violated the inviolability of the diplomatic bag, and the United Kingdom maintaining that the crate was not a diplomatic bag within the meaning of Article 27, because the conditions of the article had not been satisfied and the bag had not been claimed as such by the mission. Two lessons follow. First, the diplomatic bag is a facility for official traffic; its misuse for the abduction of persons (or for drugs or arms) is itself a breach of the Convention and of Article 41(1) — and the sending State cannot convert an unlawful act into a protected one by attaching a label. Secondly, because the only lawful response to a suspected bag is to require its return to origin, the receiving State's protection in practice depends on the sending State's good faith; where the bag is misused, the receiving State's remedies are the same as those for any other abuse — the withdrawal of the offending personnel under Article 9, and, in the last resort, the severance of relations under Article 45.

E. Personal Inviolability (Article 29)

"The person of a diplomatic agent shall be inviolable. He shall not be liable to any form of arrest or detention. The receiving State shall treat him with due respect and shall take all appropriate steps to prevent any attack on his person, freedom or dignity." (VCDR Article 29)

  1. The rule is absolute in terms, and it is absolute in substance. There is no exception for serious offences, for hot pursuit, for national security or for the "grave crimes" for which a consular officer may be arrested under VCCR Article 41. A diplomatic agent may not be stopped, searched, handcuffed, arrested, detained, subpoenaed or compelled to attend any proceeding; his baggage may not be searched against his will; and he may not be escorted off the premises of the mission.
  2. "Due respect" and "dignity" are operative words. The second and third sentences impose a positive duty on the receiving State: it must prevent attacks on the agent's person, freedom or dignity, not merely abstain from them. Two consequences follow which students should be able to state. First, a police officer who stands by while a diplomat is assaulted breaches Article 29, exactly as Tehran showed for Article 22(2). Secondly, the manner of official action around a diplomat matters: the public handcuffing and strip-searching of an officer whose status is in issue has been treated by sending States as an affront to "dignity" — which is why the friction over the arrest of India's Deputy Consul-General in New York in December 2013 became a diplomatic incident of the first order even though the officer's consular immunity was, as a matter of the Vienna Convention of 1963, limited to acts performed in the exercise of consular functions (see §7.5 K).
  3. The receiving State is not without means. Where an agent has committed a serious offence, the receiving State may (a) ask the sending State to waive immunity under Article 32 and to permit prosecution; (b) if the offence is serious or the sending State refuses, declare the agent persona non grata under Article 9(1) and require his recall, and if the sending State does not recall him within a reasonable time, refuse to recognise him as a member of the mission, whereupon he loses his immunity (Article 9(2)); or (c) expel him after the termination of his functions, subject to Articles 39 and 44. What the receiving State may not do is to arrest him and litigate the question later.
  4. Waiver in criminal matters — the Makharadze example. In January 1997 a fatal road accident in Washington, D.C., involving Georgia's deputy chief of mission, Mr Gueorgui Makharadze, placed the issue on the front pages. Georgia waived his immunity, the United States prosecuted him, and he was convicted and sentenced — the clearest modern illustration of Article 32 being used for its intended purpose: the sending State may give up the immunity of its agent so that justice can be done, and Article 32(1) expressly permits waiver even against the wish of the agent.
  5. Limits of "restraint" allowed in self-defence. Practice recognises that a police officer may use such force as is reasonably necessary to protect the life or property of others — for example, to stop a diplomat driving dangerously, or to restrain a diplomat who is committing an assault — without thereby infringing Article 29, because the officer acts in the immediate defence of persons rather than in exercise of jurisdiction over the agent. Every such case, however, must be justified on that basis; there is no general "public safety" exception, and the Convention's answer to a criminal diplomat remains Article 9, not the police cell.

F. Residence, Papers and Property (Article 30)

"1. The private residence of a diplomatic agent shall enjoy the same inviolability and protection as the premises of the mission. 2. His papers, correspondence and, except as provided in paragraph 3 of article 31, his property, shall likewise enjoy inviolability." (VCDR Article 30)

  • The residence is the mission's twin. Because Article 30(1) applies the same inviolability and protection as Article 22 gives to the premises, everything said at §7.4 B about entry, the consent of the head of mission and the "special duty" of protection applies to the agent's private residence. Police cannot enter a diplomat's home without consent even to arrest a person suspected of crime, to serve process, or to search for a fugitive; the receiving State's remedy is again Article 9.
  • Papers and correspondence. Articles, files, letters and electronic records of the agent are inviolable — a protection that overlaps with, but is wider than, Article 24's protection of the mission's archives, because it attaches to the agent's papers personally. Again, the protection covers material of a private character as well as the official: the Convention draws no such line in Article 30.
  • Property — and the one qualification. The agent's property is inviolable, "except as provided in paragraph 3 of article 31": that is, measures of execution may be taken against it in the three cases in which the agent is not immune from jurisdiction — a real action relating to private immovable property, a succession matter, and an action relating to a professional or commercial activity outside his official functions — "provided that the measures concerned can be taken without infringing the inviolability of his person or of his residence". The proviso is the practical heart of the matter: a creditor who has obtained a judgment in one of the excepted cases may execute against the agent's property wherever it is lawfully found, but not by entering his residence and not by any means that touch his person. In practice, enforcement against a diplomat's goods is carried out in the rare case in which the goods can be reached without breaching Articles 22 or 30 — for example goods in the hands of a third party, or a bank balance in the forum (subject to the further hurdle of diplomatic inviolability in practice).
  • Accommodation of other members. Article 21(2) obliges the receiving State, where necessary, to assist missions in obtaining suitable accommodation for members; but the strict inviolability of Article 30 attaches to the diplomatic agent's residence, while the residence of administrative and technical staff enjoys the inviolability of Article 37(2) — a graded protection which is taken up at §7.4 J.

↑ top

G. Immunity from Jurisdiction (Article 31) — the Three Exceptions

"1. A diplomatic agent shall enjoy immunity from the criminal jurisdiction of the receiving State. He shall also enjoy immunity from its civil and administrative jurisdiction, except in the case of: (a) A real action relating to private immovable property situated in the territory of the receiving State, unless he holds it on behalf of the sending State for the purposes of the mission; (b) An action relating to succession in which the diplomatic agent is involved as executor, administrator, heir or legatee as a private person and not on behalf of the sending State; (c) An action relating to any professional or commercial activity exercised by the diplomatic agent in the receiving State outside his official functions. 2. A diplomatic agent is not obliged to give evidence as a witness. 3. No measures of execution may be taken in respect of a diplomatic agent except in the cases coming under subparagraphs (a), (b) and (c) of paragraph 1 of this article, and provided that the measures concerned can be taken without infringing the inviolability of his person or of his residence. 4. The immunity of a diplomatic agent from the jurisdiction of the receiving State does not exempt him from the jurisdiction of the sending State."

1. Criminal jurisdiction — immunity without exception

The first sentence of Article 31(1) has no proviso, and this is the sharpest contrast between diplomatic and consular immunity: a consular officer may be arrested and prosecuted for a "grave crime" and is immune only in respect of official acts (VCCR Articles 41 and 43), while a diplomatic agent cannot be prosecuted for any offence at all, however grave. The immunity covers the whole criminal process and not merely the trial: the agent may not be arrested, detained, questioned as a suspect, charged, summoned, tried or sentenced; his premises and residence may not be entered to secure evidence (Articles 22, 30); and he cannot be compelled to give evidence in any criminal proceeding (Article 31(2)). Article 31(4) preserves the sending State's own jurisdiction: the immunity is a transfer of jurisdiction, not a licence, and the sending State may (and in serious cases should) prosecute the agent under its own law, or recall and try him.

2. The three civil and administrative exceptions

  1. Private immovable property (Article 31(1)(a)). The exception is confined to a "real action relating to private immovable property situated in the territory of the receiving State" — that is, a proceeding whose object is the property itself (title, possession, boundaries, rent, recovery of premises). The proviso, "unless he holds it on behalf of the sending State for the purposes of the mission", preserves immunity where the property is in truth the mission's: a building leased or bought in the agent's name for the chancery or for the residence of the head of mission is not "private" property. This is the exception which Indian courts apply most often, because it is the statutory foundation of the landlord's remedy against a foreign mission: read Article 31(1)(a) together with section 86(2)(c) of the Code of Civil Procedure and with Century Twenty One (P) Ltd. v. Union of India (AIR 1987 Del. 124) and Harbhajan Singh v. Union of India (AIR 1987 SC 9), both discussed at §7.3 G.
  2. Succession (Article 31(1)(b)). A diplomatic agent who is involved as executor, administrator, heir or legatee "as a private person and not on behalf of the sending State" may be sued in the courts of the receiving State in matters of succession. The purpose is to prevent an agent from taking the benefit of a local estate while disabling the local court from administering it.
  3. Professional or commercial activity outside official functions (Article 31(1)(c)). Where the agent practises a profession or carries on business in the receiving State for private gain — conduct forbidden to him by Article 42 — he loses his civil immunity in respect of that activity. The exception is thus the mirror image of the prohibition: the Convention removes the immunity exactly where the agent has placed himself in the commercial life of the receiving State. Examples in practice include a member of the mission running a trading business through a locally incorporated company, practising medicine or law, or engaging in speculation. Note the limits: the activity must be outside his official functions, so that the sale of a chancery building, or the buying of supplies for the mission, remains within the immunity; and the immunity of the sending State itself under §7.3 may still be available to the mission as an institution.

3. Three further rules — evidence, execution, counterclaims

  • No obligation to give evidence (Article 31(2)). The rule covers oral testimony, affidavits, depositions and documentary production; it applies in civil, criminal and administrative proceedings. The receiving State's remedy is to request the sending State to waive the immunity for that purpose; the sending State may grant a limited waiver (for example, permitting the agent to swear an affidavit in a civil suit in which the agent is himself the plaintiff). For the analogous (and narrower) consular rule, see VCCR Article 44: consular officers may be called upon to give evidence, but they are not compellable in respect of matters connected with the exercise of their functions, and no coercive or other measure of constraint may be taken against them in civil or criminal proceedings in respect of matters connected with the exercise of their functions.
  • Execution (Article 31(3)). Measures of execution may be taken only in the three excepted cases, and only "provided that the measures concerned can be taken without infringing the inviolability of his person or of his residence". The clause is the diplomatic analogue of the two-stage analysis of §7.3 F, and it is applied in practice with the further qualification of Article 32(4): waiver of immunity from jurisdiction does not imply waiver of immunity from execution.
  • Counterclaim and the plaintiff's position (Article 32(3)). A diplomatic agent (or a person enjoying immunity under Article 37) who initiates proceedings may not invoke immunity from jurisdiction in respect of any counterclaim "directly connected with the principal claim". If a diplomat sues his landlord for rent recovery, he must meet the landlord's counterclaim for repairs; and if he sues for the price of goods, he must answer the defence of defective quality.

Fig. 7.5 — Seven questions for any claim involving a diplomat: status; criminal or civil; the three exceptions; waiver; counterclaim; separate waiver for execution; and the inviolability overlay which no judgment can override.

H. Waiver (Article 32)

"1. The immunity from jurisdiction of diplomatic agents and of persons enjoying immunity under article 37 may be waived by the sending State. 2. Waiver must always be express. 3. The initiation of proceedings by a diplomatic agent or by a person enjoying immunity from jurisdiction under article 37 shall preclude him from invoking immunity from jurisdiction in respect of any counterclaim directly connected with the principal claim. 4. Waiver of immunity from jurisdiction in respect of civil or administrative proceedings shall not be held to imply waiver of immunity in respect of the execution of the judgement, for which a separate waiver shall be necessary."

  • The right to waive belongs to the sending State, not to the agent. "Immunity is a right of the State, not of the individual"; the sending State may waive it "even against the will of the person concerned" (as the ILC's commentary and the practice of States show). Hence an agent cannot bargain away his own immunity, and a receiving State cannot extract a waiver from the agent.
  • Waiver must be express (Article 32(2)). This is a deliberate departure from the position in State immunity, where a waiver may be implied from conduct (§7.3 A and E). For a diplomat, conduct — accepting service, appearing to defend on the merits, entering a contract with a jurisdictional clause, or failing to plead immunity — does not by itself amount to waiver. In practice a waiver is given by diplomatic note, ad hoc, or by an express clause identifying the dispute or the agreement. A well-drafted clause should be specific about both jurisdiction and execution, because of paragraph 4.
  • Waiver of jurisdiction is not waiver of execution (Article 32(4)). The sending State may be content to litigate and yet refuse to allow enforcement against its property; a separate waiver is required for execution. The rule mirrors the two-stage analysis of State immunity (§7.3 F) and is a standing trap for private litigants.
  • Waiver and the counterclaim (Article 32(3)). The agent who sues submits himself, for the purposes of that suit, to a directly connected counterclaim — a limited, functional waiver imposed by the Convention itself.
  • Waiver in the criminal field. The best-known modern example is the 1997 waiver by Georgia of the immunity of its deputy chief of mission in Washington, Mr Gueorgui Makharadze, after a fatal road accident, following which the United States prosecuted him: the episode illustrates how Article 32 enables the receiving State to obtain justice in a serious case, and how a sending State may protect its long-term interests better by waiving immunity than by insisting upon it.
  • Multi-level waivers. The sending State may waive immunity for particular purposes only — for example, to permit a member of the mission to give evidence, or to permit service of process, without waiving either jurisdiction generally or execution. A "limited purpose" waiver is construed as covering exactly the purpose stated.

I. Taxes, Customs and Social Security (Articles 33–36)

Table 7.20 — The privileges that are not immunities (Articles 28, 33, 34, 35, 36)
ArticlePrivilegeThe exceptions — what the diplomat does pay
28Fees and charges levied by the mission in the course of its official dutiesNone stated; the exemption is from all dues and taxes on those fees.
33Exemption from social security provisions in the receiving State with respect to services rendered for the sending State; the exemption extends to private servants in the sole employ of the agent on two conditions (not nationals of or permanently resident in the receiving State, and covered by social security of the sending or a third State)Where the exemption does not apply (for instance, where the agent employs a local national who is not exempt), the agent must observe the employer's obligations imposed by the receiving State's social security legislation (Article 33(3)).
34Exemption from all dues and taxes, personal or real, national, regional or municipalSix exceptions: (a) indirect taxes normally incorporated in the price of goods or services (VAT, sales tax, excise); (b) dues and taxes on private immovable property in the receiving State, unless held for the sending State for mission purposes; (c) estate, succession and inheritance duties (subject to the death provisions of Article 39(4)); (d) dues and taxes on private income having its source in the receiving State and capital taxes on investments in commercial undertakings there; (e) charges levied for specific services rendered; (f) registration, court and record fees, mortgage dues and stamp duty on immovable property, subject to Article 23.
35Exemption from all personal services, all public service of any kind, and military obligations such as requisitioning, military contributions and billetingNo exception stated. The importance of the article in India lies in the context of requisitioning and billeting legislation: a diplomatic agent's accommodation cannot be requisitioned for military purposes.
36Customs exemption for (a) articles for the official use of the mission and (b) articles for the personal use of the agent and of the members of his family forming part of his household, including articles intended for his establishmentBaggage may be inspected only on "serious grounds for presuming" that it contains articles outside the exemption, or articles whose import or export is prohibited or controlled by quarantine regulations — and then "only in the presence of the diplomatic agent or of his authorized representative" (Article 36(2)). Charges for storage, cartage and similar services remain payable.

Three practical observations complete the section. First, the tax exemption is a personal privilege of the agent and not of his household's commercial life: an agent who rents out his private flat in New Delhi pays tax on the rent (Article 34(b)), and an agent who earns income from shares in a local company pays tax on the dividends (Article 34(d)). Secondly, the customs exemption is administrative rather than automatic: it is to be enjoyed "in accordance with such laws and regulations as [the receiving State] may adopt", which is why receiving States maintain a system of prior intimation and clearance for diplomatic imports, and why disputes about the import of luxury goods by members of a mission are dealt with under Article 9 rather than in the customs courts. Thirdly, Article 36(2)'s inspection provision is the only place in the diplomatic Convention where a package belonging to an agent may be opened, and only under the double condition of "serious grounds" and the presence of the agent or his representative; opening a diplomatic bag remains forbidden by Article 27 in every case (§7.4 D).

J. Family, Staff, Nationals and Permanent Residents (Articles 37–38)

The ladder of protection inside a mission is set out in Article 37, and the treatment of local residents in Article 38. The categories and their privileges are tabulated at §7.1 D (Table 7.8); what follows are the four points that examination questions turn on.

  1. Family. Members of the family of a diplomatic agent forming part of his household enjoy the privileges and immunities of Articles 29 to 36 — the full package — provided they are not nationals of the receiving State (Article 37(1)). The limiting words are "forming part of his household": a married adult child living independently abroad is not covered, and the sending State's notification practice (the diplomatic list) usually identifies the family members who are attached to the mission.
  2. Administrative and technical staff. They and their families enjoy Articles 29 to 35 — the personal inviolability, the criminal immunity, the tax and social security exemptions — but with two cutting-down provisions: the civil and administrative immunity of Article 31(1) does not extend to acts performed outside the course of their duties (Article 37(2)); and the customs exemption of Article 36(1) is confined to articles imported at the time of first installation. The rationale is that such staff perform clerical and support functions, and the Convention extends to them the protection necessary for those functions but not the absolute civil immunity of the diplomatic agent.
  3. Service staff. Members of the service staff (drivers, cooks, domestic employees of the mission) who are neither nationals of nor permanently resident in the receiving State enjoy immunity only "in respect of acts performed in the course of their duties", together with exemption from dues and taxes on their emoluments and the social security exemption of Article 33 (Article 37(3)). They do not enjoy general inviolability, and they may be prosecuted locally for acts outside their duties — subject to the receiving State's obligation under Article 38(2) not to exercise its jurisdiction "in such a manner as not to interfere unduly with the performance of the functions of the mission".
  4. Private servants, nationals and permanent residents. Private servants of members of the mission are exempt from dues and taxes on their emoluments, and may enjoy other privileges only to the extent admitted by the receiving State (Article 37(4)). Under Article 38, a diplomatic agent who is a national of or permanently resident in the receiving State enjoys only immunity from jurisdiction and inviolability in respect of official acts performed in the exercise of his functions; and other members of staff and private servants who are local nationals or permanent residents enjoy privileges and immunities only to the extent admitted by the receiving State. This is the provision which prevents a State from staffing a mission with its own nationals who are habitually resident in the receiving State and thus immune from local law: a "local" member of a mission cannot escape responsibility for acts done outside his official functions.
A live problem in Articles 37 and 38 — domestic workers

The practice of employing domestic servants brought from the sending State, or recruited from third countries, has produced persistent abuse in several capitals: unpaid wages, retention of passports, confinement, and a claim to immunity by the employer when the worker seeks a local remedy. Three legal points govern such cases. First, the private servant himself is not protected by Article 37 except in the narrow respects stated, so the servant enjoys no immunity from the receiving State's labour or criminal law. Secondly, the agent's own immunity from civil jurisdiction does not extend to the professional or commercial activity exception (Article 31(1)(c)) in the way a litigant sometimes hopes: employment of a private servant is not a "commercial activity", and the receiving State's remedy in practice is diplomatic, under Articles 9 and 32. Thirdly, the sending State may waive immunity (Article 32) or recall the agent, and a number of sending States now require domestic workers to be covered by a written contract, minimum wages and social insurance — the policy response which treats the abuse as a matter of the sending State's own responsibility.

↑ top

K. Commencement, Duration and Third States (Articles 39–40)

"Every person entitled to privileges and immunities shall enjoy them from the moment he enters the territory of the receiving State on proceeding to take up his post or, if already in its territory, from the moment when his appointment is notified to the Ministry for Foreign Affairs …" (VCDR Article 39(1))

  • When privileges begin (Article 39(1)). The temporal trigger is either entry for the purpose of taking up the post, or — for a person already in the receiving State — the notification of his appointment to the Ministry for Foreign Affairs.
