This is why Articles 10 to 12 (notification of appointment, arrival, changes) matter so much in practice: the sending State must notify the receiving State of the appointment, the arrival and the final departure of members of the mission, and the receiving State's records (the "diplomatic list") operate as the practical proof of status. A person who arrives but whose appointment has not been notified enjoys no immunity for the interval.
  • When privileges end (Article 39(2)). They cease "at the moment when he leaves the country, or on expiry of a reasonable period in which to do so", and they subsist until that moment "even in case of armed conflict". The important tail is the last sentence: "with respect to acts performed by such a person in the exercise of his functions as a member of the mission, immunity shall continue to subsist." This is the diplomatic expression of the distinction between personal and functional immunity (see §7.3 H): the agent's personal immunity ends with his mission; his ratione materiae immunity for official acts is permanent. A former ambassador sued years later in respect of an official act remains immune; sued in respect of a private act committed during his mission, he is not.
  • Death (Article 39(3)–(4)). Where a member of the mission dies, his family enjoy their privileges until the expiry of a reasonable period to leave the country; the receiving State must permit the withdrawal of the deceased's movable property, except property acquired in the country whose export was prohibited at the time of death; and estate, succession and inheritance duties are not levied on the movable property situated in the receiving State by reason only of the death — the exception to the taxation rule in Article 34(c).
  • Third States (Article 40). A third State — a State which is neither sending nor receiving — must accord inviolability and such other immunities as are required to ensure transit to a diplomatic agent passing through its territory while proceeding to or returning from his post, or returning to his own country, provided the necessary visa was granted; the same applies to accompanying family members. Third States must not hinder the passage of administrative, technical and service staff and their families (Article 40(2)); they must accord official correspondence, code and cipher messages, couriers and diplomatic bags in transit the same freedom, protection and inviolability as the receiving State (Article 40(3)); and these obligations apply also where the person, communication or bag is present in the third State by reason of force majeure (Article 40(4)). The article matters in a world of transit hubs: an envoy en route to his post through a State with which his country has no relations still cannot be arrested at the airport. The difficulties of transit for protected persons in practice — airspace refusals, unscheduled landings, and searches of aircraft — recur in State practice and explain why Article 40 is invoked whenever a head of State or an envoy is inconvenienced in transit.

L. The Duties of the Mission and the Remedies for Abuse (Articles 41–43)

"1. Without prejudice to their privileges and immunities, it is the duty of all persons enjoying such privileges and immunities to respect the laws and regulations of the receiving State. They also have a duty not to interfere in the internal affairs of that State. 2. All official business with the receiving State entrusted to the mission by the sending State shall be conducted with or through the Ministry for Foreign Affairs … 3. The premises of the mission must not be used in any manner incompatible with the functions of the mission …" (VCDR Article 41)

  1. The duty to respect local law (Article 41(1), first sentence). Immunity is not a licence to break the law; it is an exemption from local enforcement. The duties are owed because the exemption is granted. This is why a diplomat who flouts local traffic, tax or labour law commits no crime that a local court can try, but does commit an internationally wrongful act for which his State is responsible — and why the receiving State may react under Article 9.
  2. The duty not to interfere in internal affairs (Article 41(1), second sentence). The classic applications are espionage, financing of political parties, arms trafficking, and (increasingly) public political statements about the receiving State's internal politics by heads of mission. The duty binds the sending State and not merely the individual agent; and it coexists with the freedom of the mission to explain its Government's position, so that the dividing line is between advocacy of the sending State's policies and intervention in the receiving State's domestic politics. Where the receiving State considers that the line has been crossed, its remedy is Article 9; and where the alleged interference is serious and repeated, it may also complain of a breach of Article 41 before an international tribunal or in the political organs of the United Nations.
  3. The channel of communication (Article 41(2)). Official business with the receiving State must be conducted with or through the Ministry for Foreign Affairs or such other ministry as may be agreed. The rule explains the primacy of the MFA in diplomatic practice, the practice of "notes verbales", and the irregularity of a mission's dealing directly with provincial authorities on matters of State.
  4. The use of premises (Article 41(3)). Premises must not be used "in any manner incompatible with the functions of the mission". The two applications which matter are diplomatic asylum (see §7.4 B.3) and commercial or partisan use — for instance, letting part of the chancery to a private business, or allowing a political party of the receiving State to use the mission's compound for its rallies. Like the other duties in Article 41, the obligation is enforced by the remedies in Article 9 and Article 45, not by entry.
  5. No private gain (Article 42). A diplomatic agent "shall not in the receiving State practise for personal profit any professional or commercial activity". This is the prohibition which works together with the civil exception in Article 31(1)(c): the activity is forbidden, and immunity is withdrawn in respect of it. Where a member of a mission engages in business through a locally registered company, the local courts will ordinarily deal with the company (a domestic person) and the individual's immunity will not protect the corporate veil.
  6. End of functions (Article 43). The function of a diplomatic agent comes to an end: (a) on notification by the sending State that it has come to an end; (b) on notification by the receiving State under Article 9(2) that it refuses to recognise him as a member of the mission; and (c) on the recall of the mission or the severance of relations (see Article 43's chapeau and Article 45). Article 9 is the engine: the receiving State may, "at any time and without having to explain its decision", notify the sending State that the head of mission or any member of the diplomatic staff is persona non grata (or that another member of the staff is "not acceptable"); the sending State must then recall him or terminate his functions; and if it fails to do so within a reasonable period, the receiving State "may refuse to recognize the person concerned as a member of the mission" — whereupon his immunity is at an end.
The remedies for abuse — and why self-help is unlawful

The Convention deals with abuse by providing, in Article 9, the power of the receiving State to terminate the offending agent's immunity at a stroke, without giving reasons, before or after his arrival: "A person may be declared non grata or not acceptable before arriving in the territory of the receiving State." Where abuse is systemic, the receiving State may call for the recall of the mission or sever relations (Articles 43 and 45), and it may apply the reciprocity rule in Article 47. Article 47 also prevents retaliation against third States: discrimination is prohibited, save where the receiving State answers a restrictive application of a provision to its own mission, or where States by custom or agreement extend to one another more favourable treatment. What the receiving State may not do is to take the law into its own hands — to enter the premises, arrest the agent, seize the archives or search the bag. That is the holding of Tehran on the "self-contained régime" (paragraph 86), and it is the reason why the answer to the 2025 paper's hostage hypothetical never begins with "Iran's grievances" but always with the receiving State's obligations and remedies: Article 9, Article 45, and the responsibility of the State for conduct it endorses.

M. Breaking of Relations and Protection of the Premises (Articles 44–46)

"If diplomatic relations are broken off between two States, or if a mission is permanently or temporarily recalled: (a) The receiving State must, even in case of armed conflict, respect and protect the premises of the mission, together with its property and archives; (b) The sending State may entrust the custody of the premises of the mission, together with its property and archives, to a third State acceptable to the receiving State; (c) The sending State may entrust the protection of its interests and those of its nationals to a third State acceptable to the receiving State." (VCDR Article 45)

  • Departure in armed conflict (Article 44). Even in armed conflict the receiving State must grant facilities to enable persons enjoying privileges and immunities, other than its own nationals, and members of their families, to leave at the earliest possible moment, and must where necessary place transport at their disposal for themselves and their property. The article is the diplomatic counterpart of the humanitarian rules on the departure of protected persons in time of war.
  • Protection after the break (Article 45). The three limbs of Article 45 are cumulative and distinct: the receiving State's duty to protect the premises, property and archives survives the severance of relations and applies even in armed conflict; the sending State may entrust custody to a third State acceptable to the receiving State; and the sending State may entrust the protection of its interests and of its nationals to such a third State. The third-State "protecting Power" — the State which looks after A's interests in B when A and B have no relations — is thus created by the Convention's own mechanism, and the practice is common: in the years after 1979, the interests of the United States in Iran were handled through a protecting Power, and the practice recurs whenever relations are severed.
  • The third-State route in practice. The mechanism has three practical consequences which are worth a line in any answer. First, the premises do not become a legal vacuum on severance: they remain inviolable and the receiving State must protect them (Article 45(a)). Secondly, custody may be transferred, but only to a third State acceptable to the receiving State (Article 45(b)); the sending State cannot impose a custodian. Thirdly, the protective function covers both interests and nationals (Article 45(c)), which is why the protecting Power in practice handles consular-type work — visits, notifications, repatriations — for the sending State's nationals.
  • Temporary protection of a third State's interests (Article 46). A sending State may, with the prior consent of the receiving State, and at the request of a third State not represented in the receiving State, undertake the temporary protection of the interests of the third State and of its nationals. This is the article under which one embassy handles the affairs of another without any formal severance of relations — the "interests section" arrangement familiar in the practice of States (for example, in the handling of diplomatic and consular work between States that do not recognise one another).
  • The lesson of Tehran applied. Operative point 3(c) of the 1980 judgment ordered Iran to place "in the hands of the protecting Power the premises, property, archives and documents" of the Embassy and consulates. The remedy is Article 45(c) being enforced by the Court: the premises were not to revert to the sending State, but to the protection of the third State — and the receiving State's duty of protection was held to survive everything that had happened.

N. Personal and Property Immunity in Indian Law (the 1972 Act and CPC 86–87)

India gives effect to the Vienna Convention of 1961 through the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972, and supplements it through the Code of Civil Procedure and the practice of the Ministry of External Affairs. Five features of the Indian machinery should be in every answer on 7.4.

  • The Act gives the Convention the force of law. The privileges and immunities set out in the Convention are given effect in Indian law by the Act, so that the question in an Indian court is not merely one of international law but of the statute; the Act is India's counterpart of the United Kingdom's Diplomatic Privileges Act, 1964 (which similarly gives the VCDR the force of law in the United Kingdom) and of the corresponding Australian and Canadian statutes of the same period.
  • The conclusive certificate (section 8). A certificate issued by or under the authority of the Secretary to the Government of India in the Ministry of External Affairs, as to whether a person is entitled to any privilege or immunity, is conclusive evidence of that matter in any proceeding before any court or authority. The device saves courts from the impossible task of investigating diplomatic status: the Executive's certificate settles it. (Contrast the position in the United Kingdom, where the Foreign and Commonwealth Office certificate serves a similar evidentiary function, and the older English cases collected by Verma in Chapter 8, in which persons claiming immunity failed because their status was not established.)
  • Protection of the premises from legal process (section 8, second limb). No public servant or agent of the Government of India, of a State Government or of any public authority may enter the premises of a diplomatic mission for the purpose of serving legal process, except with the consent of the head of the mission. The provision gives municipal teeth to Article 22(1) of the Convention: a process server, a bailiff or a police officer must obtain consent before entering the premises.
  • CPC section 86(4). The immunity of a foreign State under section 86 of the Code extends, as it applies to a foreign State, to "any ruler of a foreign State, any ambassador or envoy of a foreign State, any High Commissioner of a Commonwealth country, and any such member of the staff of any of those persons as the Central Government may, by general or special order, specify", and such persons cannot be arrested. Read with section 87 (the style of foreign Rulers as parties) and section 87B, this is the Indian statutory floor of diplomatic and sovereign immunity — supplemented, for the diplomat, by the 1972 Act and the Convention.
  • The two-stage practical difficulty. Verma's criticism applies with full force in India: even where consent to sue is obtained under section 86(2), the diplomat "may claim immunity from the court's jurisdiction and further, claim immunity from the execution of the decree", so that the private litigant who has the law on his side may still be left without a remedy. That is why the Indian route to relief in the embassy-lease cases runs through the executive's consent and the judiciary's supervisory jurisdiction over it (Century Twenty One; Harbhajan Singh, §7.3 G) rather than through direct proceedings against the mission.

Two refinements complete the Indian picture. First, India's obligations as a receiving State are the same in kind: the Ministry of External Affairs arranges police protection for missions and their personnel (Article 22(2) and Article 29), and the 1972 Act criminalises unauthorised entry for the service of process. Secondly, India's consular relations do not enjoy a comparable single statute: India acceded to the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations in November 1977 and applies it through the Ministry of External Affairs and the consular posts, but there is no Indian equivalent of the Diplomatic Privileges Act/Diplomatic Relations Act machinery specifically for consuls — a point developed at §7.5 K.

O. Examination Angle for 7.4

Four question-types, with skeletons

(1) "Discuss the privileges and immunities of diplomatic agents." Structure: (i) basis and rationale (functional necessity; preamble; §7.0 B); (ii) inviolability of the person (Article 29) with Tehran; (iii) inviolability of residence, papers and property (Article 30); (iv) immunity from criminal jurisdiction (without exception) and from civil and administrative jurisdiction, with the three exceptions of Article 31(1); (v) no obligation to give evidence (Article 31(2)); (vi) execution (Article 31(3)) and the two-stage rule; (vii) waiver (Article 32) with Makharadze; (viii) tax, customs, social security (Articles 33–36); (ix) duration (Article 39) and third States (Article 40); (x) the staff ladder (Articles 37–38) and, in India, the 1972 Act and CPC 86(4).

(2) "Inviolability of mission premises — discuss with reference to the case law." Begin with Article 22's three paragraphs; use the Tehran findings on Article 22(2) (the "fully aware … means … failed" formula) and Article 22(1) and (3); then the incidents: the Iraqi Embassy in Islamabad (1973) as the case of unlawful entry, the Libyan People's Bureau (1984) as the case of Article 9 and Article 45, the Chinese Embassy in Belgrade (1999) as the case of a mission in armed conflict, the Ecuadorean embassy in London (2012–2019) for asylum and Article 41(3), and the Khobragade affair (2013) for the personal counterpart. Close with the Indian statutory rule in section 8 of the 1972 Act.

(3) A problem: a diplomat commits a crime or a civil wrong, or a landlord sues an embassy for rent. Follow Fig. 7.5: establish status; criminal or civil; the three exceptions; waiver; counterclaim; execution; inviolability overlay. For the criminal case, the only lawful remedies are Article 32 and Article 9. For the lease case, Article 31(1)(a) and CPC section 86(2)(c) with Century Twenty One and Harbhajan Singh.

(4) Short notes: the diplomatic bag (Article 27 and the Dikko affair); the persona non grata procedure (Article 9); the "self-contained régime" (Tehran, paragraph 86); the protecting Power (Article 45); waiver (Article 32, Makharadze); the 1972 Act's conclusive certificate; the six exceptions to the tax exemption (Article 34).

Five errors that cost marks on 7.4

(1) Saying that a diplomat can be prosecuted for a "grave crime" — that is the consular rule (VCCR Article 41); the diplomatic immunity from criminal jurisdiction is absolute. (2) Treating the three Article 31(1) exceptions as exceptions to criminal immunity — they apply only to civil and administrative jurisdiction. (3) Forgetting Article 32(4): waiver of jurisdiction is not waiver of execution. (4) Confusing inviolability of the bag (Article 27: never to be opened) with the inspection of personal baggage (Article 36(2): permitted on serious grounds and in the agent's presence). (5) Answering an abuse of privileges with self-help — entering the premises, arresting the agent or seizing the archives — instead of Article 9 and Article 45.

↑ top

7.5Consular Privileges and Immunities

Consular law is the working end of diplomacy: visas, passports, the welfare of nationals, ships and cargo, births, deaths, marriages, notarial acts, the protection of a national who has been arrested. Its governing instrument is the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963 — "VCCR" in these notes — which India acceded to in November 1977 and which gave the Court its jurisdiction in case 43. The privileges and immunities of consular officers are narrower than those of diplomatic agents in almost every respect, and the reason is functional: a consul lives and works among the local population, deals daily with the local authorities, and cannot discharge those functions if he is entirely outside local law. Everything in this head follows from that single premise — and the whole of case 43 (Jadhav) is an application of it.

A. The Two Chapters of the VCCR — Career and Honorary Posts

The Convention distinguishes two kinds of consular post, and the distinction runs through the whole of the privileges chapter.

  • Career consular posts (Articles 28–57). Headed by a career consular officer — a member of the sending State's consular service, usually its own national, appointed under Article 10, admitted by exequatur under Article 12, and enjoying the package of facilities, privileges and immunities in Chapter III.
  • Honorary consular posts (Articles 58–68). Headed by an honorary consular officer — typically a local resident, often a national of the receiving State or a businessman with local ties, who performs consular functions part-time without being a career official. Article 58 supplies the schedule of which articles apply to such posts and officers (see §7.5 H), Article 68 confirms that "each State is free to decide whether it will appoint or receive honorary consular officers", and Articles 59–67 give them a thinner package — protection of premises, inviolability of archives, customs facilities for official use, freedom from personal services, but only function-based immunity from jurisdiction (Article 63) and no inviolability of the person.
  • Consular agents (Article 69). A State may also establish consular agencies conducted by consular agents who are not heads of consular posts, whose status is fixed by agreement between the States concerned.
  • Consular functions performed by diplomatic missions (Article 70; and Article 3(2) VCDR). "The provisions of the present Convention apply also, so far as the context permits, to the exercise of consular functions by a diplomatic mission" — with the proviso that the members of the mission assigned to the consular section continue to enjoy the privileges and immunities of the 1961 Convention and are not, in respect of their consular work, reduced to the consular standard. This is the structural reason why the Tehran case was a case under both Conventions, and why in Indian practice most consular work is done through the consular sections of embassies and high commissions (§7.2 F).
The definitions of Article 1 — learn at least these eight

"Consular post" means any consulate-general, consulate, vice-consulate or consular agency; "consular district" means the area assigned to a consular post for the exercise of consular functions; "head of consular post" means the person charged with the duty of acting in that capacity; "consular officer" means any person entrusted with the exercise of consular functions, including the head of a consular post; "consular employee" means any person employed in the administrative or technical service of a consular post; "member of the service staff" means any person employed in the domestic service of a consular post; "member of the consular post" means consular officers, consular employees and members of the service staff; and "consular premises" means the buildings or parts of buildings and the land ancillary thereto used exclusively for the purposes of the consular post. The definitions matter because privileges attach to categories: what a "consular employee" or a "member of the service staff" receives differs from what a "consular officer" receives, and the honorary post's family receives nothing at all (Article 58(3)).

B. The Consular Post and Its Protection (Articles 28–32)

"1. Consular premises shall be inviolable to the extent provided in this article. 2. The authorities of the receiving State shall not enter that part of the consular premises which is used exclusively for the purpose of the work of the consular post except with the consent of the head of the consular post or of his designee or of the head of the diplomatic mission of the sending State. The consent of the head of the consular post may, however, be assumed in case of fire or other disaster requiring prompt protective action. 3. Subject to the provisions of paragraph 2 … the receiving State is under a special duty to take all appropriate steps to protect the consular premises against any intrusion or damage … 4. The consular premises, their furnishings, the property of the consular post and its means of transport shall be immune from any form of requisition for purposes of national defence or public utility. If expropriation is necessary for such purposes, all possible steps shall be taken to avoid impeding the performance of consular functions, and prompt, adequate and effective compensation shall be paid to the sending State." (VCCR Article 31)

Table 7.21 — The consular premises compared with the diplomatic mission
PointDiplomatic mission (VCDR Article 22)Consular post (VCCR Article 31)
Scope of inviolabilityAbsolute: "the premises of the mission shall be inviolable".Qualified: "inviolable to the extent provided in this article".
Entry by local authoritiesNever without the consent of the head of mission — no exception.No entry to the part used exclusively for the work of the post without consent; but consent may be assumed in case of fire or other disaster requiring prompt protective action.
Duty to protectSpecial duty to protect against intrusion or damage and to prevent disturbance of the peace of the mission or impairment of its dignity.The same "special duty" (Article 31(3)).
Immunity from requisition and expropriationNot available: the premises, furnishings, other property and means of transport are immune from search, requisition, attachment and execution (Article 22(3)).Immunity from requisition for national defence or public utility, but expropriation is possible for such purposes against prompt, adequate and effective compensation (Article 31(4)).
TaxationThe sending State and the head of mission are exempt from dues and taxes on the premises (Article 23).Consular premises and the residence of the career head of post are exempt where owned or leased by the sending State or by a member of the post acting on its behalf; national and permanent-resident consular officers are treated differently (Article 32).
Where the consulate is in a building shared with others—Only the part "used exclusively for the purpose of the work of the consular post" attracts the entry prohibition — a practical rule for consulates housed in office blocks or in the same building as a business.

Article 28 obliges the receiving State to accord "full facilities for the performance of the functions of the consular post"; Article 29 permits the use of the national flag and coat-of-arms on the consular premises, on the residence of the head of post and on his means of transport; Article 30 requires the receiving State to facilitate the acquisition of premises and, where necessary, to assist in obtaining suitable accommodation. Article 27 supplies the endgame: in the event of the severance of consular relations, whether temporarily or permanently, the receiving State must "respect and protect the consular premises, together with the property of the consular post and the consular archives"; it must, if requested, place them in the custody of a third State acceptable to it; and it must protect the interests of the sending State and of its nationals — the consular analogue of Article 45 of the 1961 Convention, and one which may be applied even where the States concerned have no consular relations but only diplomatic ones.

C. Archives and Communication (Articles 33–35)

"The consular archives and documents shall be inviolable at all times and wherever they may be." (VCCR Article 33)

  • Archives (Article 33). The formula is the same as Article 24 of the 1961 Convention, and it carries the same consequences: inviolability of files, registers, records and electronic data relating to the consular post; no examination, copying or production; survival of the protection after the end of the post's functions (Article 27); and for honorary posts, the express continuation of the protection in Article 61.
  • Freedom of movement (Article 34). Members of the consular post may travel freely in the receiving State subject to laws on security zones — which in practice matters greatly because a consul's district may be extensive.
  • Freedom of communication and the consular bag (Article 35). The consular post enjoys freedom of communication for all official purposes, may use couriers (diplomatic or consular), bags (diplomatic or consular) and messages in code or cipher, and may install a wireless transmitter only with consent. Official correspondence is inviolable. The consular bag "shall be neither opened nor detained" (Article 35(3)) — but with a crucial qualification which distinguishes it from the diplomatic bag: "if the competent authorities of the receiving State have serious reason to believe that the bag contains something other than the correspondence, documents or articles referred to in paragraph 4 of this article, they may request that the bag be opened in their presence by an authorized representative of the sending State. If this request is refused by the authorities of the sending State, the bag shall be returned to its place of origin."
The one difference between the two bags that students must state

Diplomatic bag (VCDR Article 27): it can never be opened; the receiving State's only recourse, on serious suspicion, is to require its return to the place of origin; the packages must bear visible external marks and may contain only diplomatic documents or articles for official use. Consular bag (VCCR Article 35): it is not unqualifiedly unopenable — the receiving State may request that it be opened in its presence by an authorised representative of the sending State, and only if that request is refused is the bag returned to its place of origin. The practical consequence is that a sending State wishing to protect a consular dispatch against all curiosity will transmit it in a diplomatic bag through its embassy, not in a consular bag through its consulate — which is precisely why Article 35(1) permits the consular post to use "diplomatic or consular" couriers and bags, and why Article 70 (consular functions performed by missions) matters to practice.

D. Personal Inviolability of Consular Officers (Article 41)

"1. Consular officers shall not be liable to arrest or detention pending trial, except in the case of a grave crime and pursuant to a decision by the competent judicial authority. 2. Except in the case specified in paragraph 1 of this article, consular officers shall not be committed to prison or be liable to any other form of restriction on their personal freedom save in execution of a judicial decision of final effect. 3. If criminal proceedings are instituted against a consular officer, he must appear before the competent authorities. Nevertheless, the proceedings shall be conducted with the respect due to him by reason of his official position and … in a manner which will hamper the exercise of consular functions as little as possible. When … it has become necessary to detain a consular officer, the proceedings against him shall be instituted with the minimum of delay." (VCCR Article 41)

  1. There is a doorway, and it is narrow: "grave crime" plus a judicial decision. Unlike the diplomatic agent, the consular officer may be arrested or detained pending trial — but only where (a) the alleged offence is a grave crime, and (b) a competent judicial authority has so decided. Both conditions must be satisfied; an arrest by police on their own authority, without the decision of the competent judicial authority, is unlawful. Practice differs on what a "grave crime" is: the Convention does not define it, and States have generally treated it as an offence of a serious character (for example, an offence punishable with a substantial term of imprisonment, or one involving violence), not a traffic or petty offence.
  2. Once the criminal process is under way, the officer must appear. Consular immunity from jurisdiction (Article 43) is confined to acts performed in the exercise of consular functions — so where the charge concerns a private act or a serious crime, the officer must attend, may be tried, and, subject to paragraph 1, may be detained. The Convention's protection at that stage is procedural and dignitary: the proceedings must be conducted with respect for his official position, must hamper consular functions as little as possible, and — where detention has become necessary — must be instituted with the minimum of delay.
  3. Judicial decisions of final effect. Paragraph 2 permits imprisonment or other restriction of personal freedom only in execution of a judicial decision of final effect: a consular officer cannot be held under administrative detention, or kept in custody after acquittal or after the expiry of a sentence.
  4. Protection and notification. Article 40 obliges the receiving State to treat consular officers "with due respect" and to take all appropriate steps to prevent any attack on their person, freedom or dignity; and Article 42 requires the receiving State, in the event of the arrest or detention pending trial of a member of the consular staff, or of criminal proceedings against him, to inform the head of the consular post "without delay" (and, where the officer himself is the head of post, to inform the sending State through the diplomatic channel) — a notification duty which also carries the requirement that the person concerned be informed of his own rights, and that the receiving State give all assistance to obtain his release on bail.
Articles 41 and 43 in one breath

Consular inviolability of the person is conditional (grave crime + judicial decision); consular immunity from jurisdiction is functional (acts in the exercise of consular functions only, with two express civil exceptions in Article 43(2)). Everything else in this head — the consular bag's qualified protection, the partial inviolability of the premises, the liability of consular employees to give evidence — is a variation on the same theme: the consul is protected in his functions, not in his person or his property. The practical consequence for India is written in §7.5 K: an Indian consular officer abroad who is accused of a private wrong is not, as a matter of the Convention, beyond the reach of the local criminal process.

↑ top

E. Immunity from Jurisdiction and Liability to Give Evidence (Articles 43–44)

"1. Consular officers and consular employees shall not be amenable to the jurisdiction of the judicial or administrative authorities of the receiving State in respect of acts performed in the exercise of consular functions. 2. The provisions of paragraph 1 … shall not, however, apply in respect of a civil action either: (a) arising out of a contract concluded by a consular officer or a consular employee in which he did not contract expressly or impliedly as an agent of the sending State; or (b) by a third party for damage arising from an accident in the receiving State caused by a vehicle, vessel or aircraft." (VCCR Article 43)

  1. Functional immunity, unlimited in time. The immunity is confined to "acts performed in the exercise of consular functions", but where it applies it is permanent: Article 53(4) provides that "in respect of acts performed by a member of the consular post in the exercise of consular functions, immunity from jurisdiction shall continue to subsist without limitation of time". The reference point for "consular functions" is Article 5 — the protecting, promoting, assisting, notarial, shipping, aircraft, guardianship and passport functions there enumerated, together with the functions entrusted by Article 5(m) and by the laws of the sending State, so far as they are not prohibited by the receiving State.
  2. The two civil exceptions are the ones that get litigated. The first (Article 43(2)(a)) turns on the capacity in which the officer contracted: if he contracted "expressly or impliedly as an agent of the sending State", immunity survives; if he contracted for himself — as a private tenant, a purchaser of goods, an employer of a domestic worker — the exception applies and he is answerable in the local court. The second (Article 43(2)(b)) is the "consular road accident" exception: a third party's claim for damage arising from an accident in the receiving State caused by a vehicle, vessel or aircraft in which the officer or employee was involved. Two consequences follow in practice: consular staff must insure against third-party risks, as Article 56 expressly requires; and a consular officer cannot shelter behind his immunity in the ordinary traffic case, which is the commonest of all consular incidents.
  3. Consular employees share the functional immunity, not the inviolability. Article 43 applies to "consular officers and consular employees"; but the personal inviolability of Article 41 belongs to consular officers alone. Service staff enjoy neither, save that they are exempt from giving evidence about their official functions under Article 44(3).
  4. Evidence (Article 44). Members of a consular post may be called upon to attend as witnesses in judicial or administrative proceedings; consular employees and members of the service staff cannot decline to give evidence, except in the cases in paragraph 3; and if a consular officer declines to attend, "no coercive measure or penalty may be applied to him". The authority requiring the evidence must avoid interfering with the officer's functions and may, when possible, take the evidence at his residence or at the consular post, or accept a written statement. By paragraph 3, members of the consular post are under no obligation to give evidence "concerning matters connected with the exercise of their functions" or to produce official correspondence and documents relating thereto, and they may decline to give evidence as expert witnesses on the law of the sending State. The contrast with Article 31(2) of the 1961 Convention (an absolute exemption from giving evidence) is one of the clearest illustrations of the difference between the two packages and is tested every year.

F. Waiver (Article 45)

"1. The sending State may waive, with regard to a member of the consular post, any of the privileges and immunities provided for in articles 41, 43 and 44. 2. The waiver shall in all cases be express … and shall be communicated to the receiving State in writing. 3. The initiation of proceedings by a consular officer or a consular employee in a matter where he might enjoy immunity from jurisdiction under article 43 shall preclude him from invoking immunity from jurisdiction in respect of any counterclaim directly connected with the principal claim. 4. The waiver of immunity from jurisdiction for the purposes of civil or administrative proceedings shall not be deemed to imply the waiver of immunity from the measures of execution resulting from the judicial decision; … a separate waiver shall be necessary."

The article is the consular mirror of Article 32 of the 1961 Convention, with three points of detail worth stating: the waiver is in writing; the initiation of proceedings by the officer waives immunity for a directly connected counterclaim; and the two-stage rule for execution is repeated. The submission of a consular officer to the jurisdiction of the receiving State in an accident case — as happens when an insurer defends the claim — may amount to an express waiver if communicated in writing, but a merely defensive appearance should not be treated as a waiver of the immunity that the sending State has not given up. It is worth adding in an answer that the waiver of consular immunity is a matter of day-to-day practice: most accidents involving consular vehicles are dealt with by the insurer, and many sending States waive in order to avoid a diplomatic incident.

G. Facilities — Notification, Communication with Nationals, Registration, Work, Taxes (Articles 36, 42, 46–49)

Article 36 is the heart of consular law. Its three limbs are set out in full at §7.5 K and in case 43; in the notes they are given here in the order of the text.

  1. Freedom of communication and access (Article 36(1)(a)). "Consular officers shall be free to communicate with nationals of the sending State and to have access to them. Nationals of the sending State shall have the same freedom with respect to communication with and access to consular officers of the sending State."
  2. Notification of arrest or detention (Article 36(1)(b)). "If he so requests, the competent authorities of the receiving State shall, without delay, inform the consular post of the sending State if, within its consular district, a national of that State is arrested or committed to prison or to custody pending trial or is detained in any other manner. Any communication addressed to the consular post by the person arrested, in prison, custody or detention shall also be forwarded by the said authorities without delay. The said authorities shall inform the person concerned without delay of his rights under this sub-paragraph."
  3. Visits, correspondence and legal representation (Article 36(1)(c)). "Consular officers shall have the right to visit a national of the sending State who is in prison, custody or detention, to converse and correspond with him and to arrange for his legal representation. They shall also have the right to visit any national of the sending State who is in prison, custody or detention in their district in pursuance of a judgment. Nevertheless, consular officers shall refrain from taking action on behalf of a national who is in prison, custody or detention if he expressly opposes such action."
  4. The proviso (Article 36(2)). "The rights referred to in paragraph 1 of this article shall be exercised in conformity with the laws and regulations of the receiving State, subject to the proviso, however, that the said laws and regulations must enable full effect to be given to the purposes for which the rights accorded under this article are intended."
The three cases that must be cited with Article 36

LaGrand (Germany v. United States), ICJ Rep. 2001, p. 466: Article 36(1) creates individual rights for detained foreign nationals; the United States breached Article 36(1)(b) by failing to inform the LaGrand brothers of their rights; and where a person has been executed without the violation being remedied, the remedy of review and reconsideration must be available — with the further holding that the procedural default rule applied by the United States courts could not be applied to defeat the rights under Article 36. Avena and Other Mexican Nationals (Mexico v. United States), ICJ Rep. 2004, p. 12: the same principles applied to fifty-two Mexican nationals; the Court found breaches of Article 36(1)(b) and (c) and held that the United States was obliged to provide review and reconsideration of the convictions and sentences of those who had not yet been executed. Jadhav (India v. Pakistan), ICJ Rep. 2019, p. 418 (case 43): the three limbs were applied to a death sentence passed by a Field General Court Martial, and the Court added two propositions of lasting importance — there is no espionage exception to Article 36, and the duty of notification and access cannot be made conditional on the sending State's co-operation.

Notification of arrest (Article 42). Where a member of a consular post is arrested or detained pending trial, or criminal proceedings are instituted against him, the receiving State must promptly notify the head of the consular post — and if the head of post is himself the subject of the measure, must notify the sending State through the diplomatic channel. The article is the consular counterpart of Article 36(1)(b) (which protects the national); it protects the consul.

Registration, residence and work permits (Articles 46–47). Consular officers, consular employees and members of their families forming part of their households are exempt from the obligations of the receiving State relating to the registration of aliens and residence permits — with the exception of a consular employee who is not a permanent employee of the sending State or who carries on a private gainful occupation, and of his family. Members of the consular post are exempt from work-permit requirements "with respect to services rendered for the sending State"; private staff of officers and employees are exempt if they do not carry on any other gainful occupation. The exemptions exist because consular staff are posted for years and must be able to live, work and move with their families without being dependent on the receiving State's immigration discretion.

Social security (Article 48). Members of the consular post and their families are exempt from the social security provisions of the receiving State with respect to services rendered for the sending State; the exemption extends to private staff in the sole employ of members on the same two conditions as under the diplomatic Convention; and where the exemption does not apply, the employer must observe the receiving State's employer obligations.

Taxation, customs, estates and personal services (Articles 49–52). Article 49 exempts consular officers and employees and their families from all dues and taxes, personal or real, national, regional or municipal, subject to six exceptions which mirror Article 34 of the 1961 Convention (indirect taxes; taxes on private immovable property; estate and succession duties; taxes on private income sourced in the receiving State and capital taxes on local commercial investments; charges for specific services; and registration, court, record, mortgage and stamp duties). Article 50 grants customs exemptions for articles for the official use of the consular post and for articles for the personal use of consular officers and their families, and permits inspection of personal baggage only in the circumstances and manner there specified (the same "serious grounds" and "presence" conditions as in the diplomatic Convention). Article 51 deals with the estate of a deceased member of the consular post. Article 52 exempts members of the consular post and their families from personal services and contributions, and from military obligations, including requisitioning, contributions and billeting.

Private gainful occupation (Article 57). Career consular officers may not carry on any professional or commercial activity for personal profit in the receiving State; and the privileges and immunities of the chapter are not accorded to consular employees or service staff who do so, nor to the family members or private staff of such persons, nor to family members of a member of the consular post who themselves carry on such an activity. The provision works together with Articles 46(2), 65 and 71: the Convention consistently withdraws the privileges where the individual has placed himself in the commercial life of the receiving State — an approach which also explains the exception in Article 43(2)(a) and the rule for honorary consuls who are engaged in trade.

H. Honorary Consular Officers (Articles 58–68)

Article 58 lays down the schedule; the rest of the chapter fills it in. For a consular post headed by an honorary consular officer, the following apply: Articles 28 (facilities), 29 (flag and coat-of-arms), 30 (accommodation), 34 (freedom of movement), 35 (communication, including the consular bag), 36 (communication with nationals), 37 (information on deaths, guardianship, wrecks and air accidents), 38 (communication with the authorities of the receiving State) and 39 (fees), together with paragraph 3 of Article 54 and paragraphs 2 and 3 of Article 55 — and, by Articles 59 to 62, protection of the consular premises, exemption from taxation of those premises, inviolability of the archives and customs facilities. For the honorary consular officer himself, there apply Articles 42 (notification of arrest), 43 (immunity from jurisdiction in respect of official acts), paragraph 3 of Article 44 (no obligation to give evidence on matters connected with his functions), 45 (waiver) and 53 (beginning and end of privileges), together with Articles 63 to 67 — criminal proceedings (he must appear, but the proceedings must respect his official position and hamper his functions as little as possible), protection (such protection as may be required by reason of his official position), exemption from registration of aliens and residence permits (unless he carries on a private gainful occupation), exemption from dues and taxes on the remuneration received from the sending State, and exemption from personal services and contributions.

Two negative points define the honorary office. First, Article 58(3): "Privileges and immunities provided in the present Convention shall not be accorded to members of the family of an honorary consular officer or of a consular employee employed at a consular post headed by an honorary consular officer." Secondly, no personal inviolability: Article 41 is not in the list, and Article 65 makes the residence-permit exemption conditional on the honorary officer not carrying on a private gainful occupation — because an honorary consul is usually a local business or professional person, and the Convention takes care that the privilege does not become a commercial advantage. Article 68 states the freedom of choice: "Each State is free to decide whether it will appoint or receive honorary consular officers" — which is also why a State may refuse to receive an honorary consul proposed by another and why the practice of honorary consuls varies greatly between States.

I. Nationals and Permanent Residents of the Receiving State (Article 71)

"1. Except insofar as additional facilities, privileges and immunities may be granted by the receiving State, consular officers who are nationals of or permanently resident in the receiving State shall enjoy only immunity from jurisdiction and personal inviolability in respect of official acts performed in the exercise of their functions, and the privileges provided in paragraph 3 of article 44. …"

The provision is the consular analogue of Article 38 of the 1961 Convention, and it is the harder case here, because honorary consuls and locally recruited consular employees are frequently nationals of or permanent residents in the receiving State. Three rules follow. First, such a consular officer enjoys immunity from jurisdiction and inviolability in respect of official acts only — everything private is out of the protection, including the private-gainful-occupation matters in Articles 46(2) and 57. Secondly, the receiving State remains bound by the notification duty of Article 42 even in respect of such an officer; and where criminal proceedings are instituted against him, they must be conducted, except where he is under arrest or detention, in a manner which will hamper the exercise of consular functions as little as possible. Thirdly, other members of the consular post who are nationals of or permanently resident in the receiving State — and the families of such persons — enjoy only such privileges as the receiving State chooses to grant, subject to the receiving State's obligation to exercise its jurisdiction "in such a way as not to interfere unduly with the performance of the functions of the consular post".

J. Duties, Third States and Termination (Articles 52–55 and 53)

  • Duties (Article 55). Members of the consular post must respect the laws and regulations of the receiving State and must not interfere in its internal affairs; and the consular premises must not be used in a manner incompatible with the exercise of consular functions. Because of the consular office's commercial and notarial work, this duty has a practical edge which its diplomatic counterpart lacks: the consul is often the point of contact between the sending State and local traders, shipping agents and lawyers, and the line between assisting a national and intervening in local affairs has to be drawn with care.
  • Consular officers may perform diplomatic acts and vice versa (Article 17; Article 70). Where the sending State has no diplomatic mission and is not represented by a third State, a consular officer may, with the consent of the receiving State, be authorised to perform diplomatic acts, without being entitled to diplomatic privileges; conversely, a diplomatic mission exercising consular functions (Article 70) does so without thereby reducing its members to the consular standard. The provisions exist because the diplomatic and consular services are functionally interchangeable at the edges, and they explain the "consular section" arrangements of Indian and other missions.
  • Third States (Article 54). A third State which has granted a visa must accord a consular officer passing through or in its territory, while proceeding to take up or return to his post or returning to the sending State, inviolability and such other immunities as may be required to ensure his transit; it must not hinder the passage of other members of the consular post and their families; and it must accord official communications and the consular bag in transit the same freedom and protection as the receiving State — with the obligations applying also where the presence is due to force majeure. The article mirrors Article 40 of the 1961 Convention, and the differences are instructive: the transit protection of the consular officer is expressed in terms of "inviolability and such other immunities as may be required to ensure his transit", not by conferring the full personal immunity of the receiving State's regime.
  • Beginning and end of privileges (Article 53). Privileges begin when the member of the consular post enters the receiving State to take up his post or, if already there, when he enters on his duties; they end, normally, when he leaves the receiving State or on the expiry of a reasonable period in which to do so, "whichever is the sooner", and they subsist until then even in case of armed conflict; but "in respect of acts performed by a member of the consular post in the exercise of consular functions, immunity from jurisdiction shall continue to subsist without limitation of time" (Article 53(4)). The family and private staff have derivative protection measured from the same dates. Termination of functions (Article 25) occurs on notification by the sending State, on the withdrawal of the exequatur (Article 23), and in the cases there enumerated.
  • The receiving State's remedy (Article 23). The receiving State may at any time notify a consular officer that he is persona non grata or that any other member of the consular post is not acceptable; if the sending State refuses or fails to recall him, the receiving State "may, as the case may be, withdraw the exequatur from the person concerned or cease to consider him as a member of the consular post". Note the difference from Article 9 of the 1961 Convention: the consular remedy includes the withdrawal of the exequatur, the consular instrument of admission (Articles 12 and 23).

K. Consular Privileges in Indian Law — and the Khobragade Affair

The legal framework in India. India acceded to the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations in November 1977 and applies it through the Ministry of External Affairs: consular work is done by consular posts abroad and, for the most part, by the consular sections of India's embassies and high commissions — an arrangement which the VCCR itself contemplates (Article 70) and which explains how a single office can be both a diplomatic mission and a consular post for different purposes. Unlike the VCDR, the VCCR has no single comprehensive Indian implementing statute of the kind found in the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972; consular privileges and immunities are applied administratively and are pleaded before Indian courts through the Convention, with the Ministry of External Affairs providing the necessary certification of status as a matter of practice. (The United Kingdom's Consular Relations Act, 1968 performs the function which in India is left to the treaty and administrative practice; in the United States, consular immunities flow from the VCCR as applied in the courts through the doctrine of self-executing treaties and the Vienna Convention's implementing practice.)

The Khobragade affair (December 2013). On 12 December 2013, Dr Devyani Khobragade, then India's Deputy Consul-General in New York, was arrested in public, handcuffed and strip-searched on charges arising from the employment of a domestic worker — visa fraud and false statements in relation to the worker's employment. India protested strongly, on three grounds: that a consular officer cannot be arrested except for a grave crime and pursuant to a decision of the competent judicial authority (VCCR Article 41(1)); that the manner of the arrest was inconsistent with the respect due to her official position (Articles 40, 41(3) and 63); and that the conduct complained of related to a private contract of employment, which was a matter for the sending State's waiver or for civil process, not for a criminal arrest of that kind. The United States took the position that the alleged offences were grave and that a federal court had issued the necessary warrant, and that her consular immunity under Article 43 did not cover the conduct because it was not an exercise of consular functions — a position which, on the text of Article 43(2)(a), is difficult to resist in relation to a contract concluded in the officer's private capacity. India transferred her to its Permanent Mission to the United Nations, where she acquired immunity as a member of a diplomatic mission; it took reciprocal administrative measures in respect of United States diplomatic and consular personnel in India (including the removal of certain privileges such as airport passes and the withdrawal of some facilities); and the two States, after a period of open friction, allowed the matter to lapse without trial. The episode is studied for five reasons, all of them examinable: it tests the difference between Article 31(1) of the 1961 Convention (absolute criminal immunity) and Article 41(1) of the 1963 Convention (arrest only for a grave crime and by judicial decision); it tests the scope of Article 43(1) and the exception in Article 43(2)(a); it raises the "dignity" obligation in Articles 40 and 41(3); it illustrates the practice of re-designating a consular officer to diplomatic status to obtain the wider immunity; and it shows how a consular incident escalates into a general diplomatic dispute governed by Article 47 of the 1961 Convention (non-discrimination and reciprocity) and by the principle of reciprocity in the treatment of missions.

Three further Indian features. First, India appoints and receives honorary consuls — a practice which brings Articles 58 to 68 into Indian consular relations, and with them the rule that no privileges are accorded to the family of an honorary consular officer (Article 58(3)). Secondly, India's consular protection of Indian workers in the Gulf and South-East Asia is exercised under Article 5(a), (e) and (g) and Article 36, and has produced a large body of administrative practice: labour-attaché and welfare-fund arrangements, memoranda of understanding with receiving States, and the practice of seeking consular access through notes verbales and, where refused, at the diplomatic level. Thirdly, in Indian courts a claim of consular immunity is raised like any other question of treaty law, and the courts of India have, as a matter of practice, sought the assistance of the Ministry of External Affairs — the consular counterpart of the certificate device in section 8 of the 1972 Act.

L. Diplomatic versus Consular Immunity — the Full Comparison (Table 7.22)

This is the table the 2025 paper asked for in terms ("Explain the difference between diplomats and consuls"), and it is the fastest source of marks in the whole topic. Learn the rows in bold; the rest can be reconstructed.

Table 7.22 — Diplomatic agent (VCDR 1961) compared with consular officer (VCCR 1963)
Point of comparisonDiplomatic agentConsular officer
Representative characterRepresents the sending State itself; the preamble and Article 3 make the mission the channel of communication between Governments. The diplomat is "in the place of the sovereign".Represents the sending State in a defined district for consular functions — protecting nationals, shipping, notarial and administrative work. Not a political representative.
InstrumentVienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961; in India the Diplomatic Relations (Vienna Convention) Act, 1972 (United Kingdom: Diplomatic Privileges Act, 1964).Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963; India acceded in November 1977; no single Indian statute (United Kingdom: Consular Relations Act, 1968).
ClassesThree classes of heads of mission (Article 14): ambassadors/nuncios, envoys/ministers/internuncios, chargés d'affaires.Four classes of heads of consular post (Article 9): consuls-general, consuls, vice-consuls, consular agents.
Appointment and admissionPrior agrément of the head of mission (Article 4); letters of credence; the receiving State may refuse the agrément without explanation.Appointment by the sending State; admission by exequatur (Article 12), which may be refused without explanation; provisional admission is possible pending the exequatur (Article 13).
Territorial scopeWhole territory of the receiving State.A defined consular district; functions outside the district (Article 6) or in a third State (Article 7) only with consent.
Personal inviolabilityAbsolute: "The person of a diplomatic agent shall be inviolable. He shall not be liable to any form of arrest or detention" (Article 29).Qualified: not liable to arrest or detention pending trial "except in the case of a grave crime and pursuant to a decision by the competent judicial authority" (Article 41(1)); imprisonment only in execution of a judicial decision of final effect.
Criminal jurisdictionAbsolute immunity, without exception, for any offence (Article 31(1), first sentence).Immunity only "in respect of acts performed in the exercise of consular functions" (Article 43(1)); private acts and grave crimes are triable.
Civil and administrative jurisdictionImmune, subject to three exceptions — private immovable property, succession, and professional or commercial activity outside official functions (Article 31(1)(a)–(c)).Immune in respect of official acts only, with two express civil exceptions — a contract not concluded as agent of the sending State, and third-party claims for damage from a vehicle, vessel or aircraft accident (Article 43(2)).
Giving evidenceNot obliged to give evidence at all (Article 31(2)).May be called as a witness; cannot refuse, but no coercive measure or penalty may be applied to a refusing consular officer, and nothing connected with his functions or official documents may be demanded (Article 44).
PremisesInviolable absolutely; no entry without the consent of the head of mission; immune from search, requisition, attachment and execution (Article 22).Inviolable "to the extent provided": no entry to the part used exclusively for consular work without consent, but consent may be assumed in case of fire or disaster; immunity from requisition for national defence or public utility, but expropriation possible with prompt, adequate and effective compensation (Article 31).
ResidenceSame inviolability and protection as the premises (Article 30(1)).No equivalent general inviolability of the consul's residence; the residence of the career head of post enjoys the tax exemption of Article 32.
ArchivesInviolable "at any time and wherever they may be" (Article 24).Inviolable "at all times and wherever they may be" (Article 33) — the same rule.
BagMay never be opened or detained; on serious suspicion the receiving State may require its return to origin (Article 27(3)).May not be opened or detained, but on serious suspicion the receiving State may request that it be opened in its presence by an authorised representative of the sending State; if refused, it is returned to origin (Article 35(3)).
Taxes and customsExemption from dues and taxes with six exceptions (Article 34); customs exemption for official and personal use, with baggage inspection exceptionally allowed on serious grounds and in the agent's presence (Article 36).Equivalent exemptions with equivalent exceptions (Articles 49–50); consular officers must comply with insurance requirements against third-party risks (Article 56).
Social securityExemption under Article 33.Exemption under Article 48, in the same terms.
Private gainful activityNo professional or commercial activity for personal profit (Article 42); doing so removes civil immunity for that activity (Article 31(1)(c)).Career consular officers may not carry on gainful occupation (Article 57(1)); doing so — or being a consular employee or family member who does — withdraws the chapter's privileges (Article 57(2)).
WaiverBy the sending State; must be express; separate waiver needed for execution (Article 32).By the sending State; express and in writing; separate waiver needed for execution (Article 45).
DurationFrom entry or notification until departure or the expiry of a reasonable period; official-act immunity subsists (Article 39).From entry or entry on duty until departure or expiry of a reasonable period; official-act immunity subsists "without limitation of time" (Article 53).
Third StatesInviolability and immunities necessary for transit; correspondence, couriers and bags protected; force majeure covered (Article 40).Inviolability and such other immunities as are required to ensure transit; correspondence, couriers and bags protected; force majeure covered (Article 54).
Honorary or part-time variantNone. Every member of the mission is appointed under the 1961 Convention's regime.Honorary consular officers exist (Articles 58–68): functional immunity, protection of premises and archives, customs facilities, but no personal inviolability and no privileges for their families.
Nationals and permanent residentsImmunity from jurisdiction and inviolability for official acts only (Article 38(1)).Immune from jurisdiction and inviolable for official acts only; other staff only such privileges as the receiving State grants (Article 71).
Function with nationals of the sending StateNot a general function; the protection of nationals is ordinarily consular work, and a mission exercising it does so through its consular section (Article 3(2); VCCR Article 70).Central function: Article 5(a) protection, Article 36 access and notification, Article 37 information in deaths and accidents.
Remedy for abuse by the receiving StateArticle 9: declaration of persona non grata and, on failure to recall, refusal to recognise; severance under Articles 43 and 45.Article 23: declaration of persona non grata and, on failure to recall, withdrawal of the exequatur or ceasing to consider him a member of the post.
Leading casesCase 41, Tehran (1980) — both Conventions applied; Arrest Warrant (2002) on the troika.Case 43, Jadhav (2019); LaGrand (2001) and Avena (2004) on Article 36; the Khobragade affair (2013) on Articles 41 and 43.

M. Examination Angle for 7.5

Four question-types, with skeletons

(1) "Explain the difference between diplomats and consuls." Use Table 7.22 as the spine, but write it as five paragraphs, not as a list: (i) the different character and functions of the two offices; (ii) appointment and admission (agrément and letters of credence against commission and exequatur; three classes against four); (iii) the different packages — absolute criminal immunity and inviolability against the "grave crime" rule of Article 41 and the functional immunity of Article 43; (iv) the property package — premises, residence, archives and bag — with the two bag rules side by side; (v) legal basis and the honorary variant. Close with the practical point of the 2025 paper: a mission's consular section enjoys the diplomatic standard (Article 70 VCCR; Article 3(2) VCDR), which is why "diplomat or consul?" is often a question of designation rather than of person.

(2) "Consuls do not enjoy full diplomatic immunity — discuss." Trace the functional basis of the consular package; then the four great downgrades: Article 41(1) (grave crime and judicial decision), Article 43(1) and the two civil exceptions in Article 43(2), Article 44 (liability to give evidence), and Article 31 (premises inviolable "to the extent provided", with the fire presumption). Explain the reason — the consul's ordinary contact with the local community and its courts — and add the practice: the honorary consul, the private-gainful-occupation rules of Articles 46, 57 and 65, and the rule for nationals and permanent residents in Article 71.

(3) A problem on Article 36. Method: identify the national's position (arrested, in prison, in custody, detained in any other manner); was he informed of his rights (Article 36(1)(b), second limb); did he request notification; was the consular post informed "without delay"; were visits, correspondence and legal representation permitted (Article 36(1)(c)); is there any reliance on local law or on a security exception, and does Article 36(2)'s proviso permit it? Then the remedies: Jadhav's "effective review and reconsideration", the continued stay of execution, and the individual-rights reasoning of LaGrand and Avena.

(4) Short notes: the consular bag (Article 35(3)); the exequatur (Articles 11–13); consular functions (Article 5); Article 43(2)(b) and the motor-accident exception; honorary consuls (Articles 58–68); Article 71 and local consuls; the Khobragade affair; and the distinction between the two Optional Protocols as a route to the ICJ.

Five errors that cost marks on 7.5

(1) Saying that consuls enjoy "diplomatic privileges" — they enjoy consular privileges, and the difference is the point of the syllabus head. (2) Treating Article 43 as absolute: it is limited to official acts, with two express civil exceptions. (3) Forgetting Article 41(1): the arrest of a consul is not per se unlawful, but only on a grave crime and by judicial decision. (4) Confusing the consular bag with the diplomatic bag — the consular bag may be requested to be opened, and if refused, is returned to origin. (5) Missing Article 44: a consular officer is a compellable witness on non-official matters, with the single protection that no coercive measure or penalty may be applied to him.

QPast Year Questions (DU) and Model Answers — Topic 7

What is reproduced here, and where it comes from

Part A contains the Topic-7 question from the Delhi University LB-205 paper available in the project materials — question 7 of the paper of May–June 2025 (paper code 8741 J) — quoted verbatim, together with a set of questions of the same pattern which recur in the paper and in the syllabus. The recurring questions are marked as reconstructed and are not presented as quotations from any paper. Part B contains four full model answers written to the standard of a 20-mark university answer, with the marking skeleton shown for each. Part C contains six short notes. Every proposition in the answers is supported by the materials elsewhere in these notes: the Vienna Conventions themselves, the casebook text of Tehran and Jadhav, the judgments and summaries of the cases that the casebook does not print (with the provenance notes given in their case blocks), S. K. Verma's chapters on State jurisdiction and diplomatic and consular relations, and the course materials of the syllabus.

A. The Questions

Q.7, May–June 2025 (8741 J)"Explain the difference between diplomats and consuls. State 'UFO' has an embassy premise in Greedo, capital city of State of Zedex. The embassy of UFO was captured by a certain group of people of Zedex after a provocative speech given by a local leader. Many diplomats and other attaché of UFO were taken hostages. State Zedex failed to provide any assistance despite several requests from UFO. All efforts to arrange meetings were also forbidden by the local leaders. UFO brought a claim before the International Court of Justice, claiming that the attack was carried out by 'agents' of Zedex and Zedex is responsible for breach of Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961. In light of the privileges and immunities of diplomats under the said Convention and previously decided cases discuss the responsibility of Zedex."Part B, Answer 1
